PREFACE 

The twenty-one Introductory Lessons of this book present the subjunctive mood and those principles of syntax which, in accordance with the Report of the Classical Investigation, are preferably deferred until the second year of Latin. These lessons, together with the eighteen carefully graded Latin Readings which follow them, are intended for study in the first half of the second year. With classes that have completed First Year Latin Enlarged (containing the Intermediate Latin Lessons, 1−14) the work of the second year should begin with lesson 15; the preceding lessons may be used for review. 

The cultural background of the work of the first half year is provided in brief accounts on important subjects in ancient life and history, and in short biographies of the authors from whom the Latin Readings of the book are adapted - Nepos, Cicero, Livy, Aulus 

Gellius, Suetonius, and Pliny.

The working vocabulary for the first half year is presented in the Introductory Lessons of Part One and in the special vocabularies with the Latin Readings; in Part Two the special vocabularies with the Selections from Caesar provide 300 additional words for mastery.

The vocabularies include the words in the College Board List and other approved lists for the second year. The treatment of Formation of Latin Words, given on pages 493-496, will help the pupils to gain a recognition knowledge of many words beyond the scope of the required vocabulary for second-year Latin. There is included in the book a Classified Vocabulary (see page 497), which will prove useful for intensive reviews, especially for pupils who expect to take the College Entrance Board examination in elementary Latin. 

The Exercises in Derivation (pages 395-397) continue the correlation of Latin words with English words, which is a distinctive feature of First Year I atin. 

The student is prepared for the Selections from Caesar, which constitute Part Two, by a vivid Historical Introduction (pages 109-159), interpreting the eventful nine years of the War in Gaul as an episode

vi 

PREFACE 

of fundamental importance in the development of the peoples who to-day inhabit England, Germany, and France. A complete survey of Caesar's Gallic War is provided. The most significant portions of the text are selected for translation; English summaries and translations of the intervening passages unite these into an impressive whole. In the Notes, after the usual explanations of the difficulties in each chapter, a question or two is raised in regard to the facts contained in it. The purpose of these questions is to concentrate the pupil's attention upon the content of each selection from the Dē Bellō Gallico.

The work in Latin Composition supplies a comprehensive review of the principles of syntax apportioned to the second year. The sentences are not difficult; and they employ the special vocabularies given with the Selections from Caesar.

Finally, as a brief introduction to Latin poets, selections are added, chiefly from Catullus, Vergil, and Ovid. Each of these is also arranged as prose, which the student is expected to read first. Unfamiliar words are glossed. Even these few selections give some suggestion of the beauty and greatness of Latin poetry. 

Grateful acknowledgment is made to T. Rice Holmes, Hon. Litt. D. (Dublin), for extended use of his Caesar's Conquest of Gaul, Second Edition, Oxford, at the Clarendon Press, 1911. This work is invaluable to every teacher and student. Acknowledgment is also due to the Clarendon Press, Oxford, for courteous permission to incorporate certain portions of Long's excellent translation of Book VII of Caesar's 

Gallic War.

The author takes pleasure in expressing his thanks to his collaborators, who have brought to their task scholarly attainments and painstaking care.

SECOND LATIN COURSE PART ONE LESSON 1 SUBJUNCTIVE MOOD: FIRST CONJUGATION AND SUM EXHORTATIONS 1. THE MOODS IN LATIN. 

Mood, or mode, indicates the manner in which the action is expressed by a verb. Thus the indicative mood is used to declare or assert something as a fact, or to ask about something as a fact; and the imperative mood is used chiefly to express the action of the verb as a command. 

The uses of the Latin indicative and imperative, which have already been studied, correspond very closely to the uses of the same moods in English. The infinitive also is a mood, expressing action as unlimited by person and number.

The subjunctive mood is used chiefly to express something merely as an idea. The meaning of the subjunctive varies with its use; it is sometimes translated by the English indicative, and sometimes with the aid of English auxiliaries, may (past, might), can (past, could), would, and should.

The subjunctive mood has four tenses present, imperfect perfect, and pluperfect. 

1 The name subjunctive refers to the frequent use of this mood in subordinate (or "subjoined ") clauses of a complex sentence. The subjunctive is also sometimes used as the main verb in independent sentences (see 6).

SUBJUNCTIVE OF FIRST CONJUGATION 

2. 

SUBJUNCTIVE OF THE FIRST CONJUGATION 

PASSIVE PRESENT 

vocem vocer 

IMPERFECT 

vocārem vocărer PERFECT vocāverim vocātus (-a, -um) sim

PLUPERFECT 

vocāvissem vocātus (-a, -um) essem The subjunctive tenses of voco are conjugated on page 410.

OBSERVE. In the subjunctive active, the present ends in -em; the imperfect is the present stem +-rem; the perfect is the perfect stem +-erim; the pluperfect is the perfect stem + -issem. 

In the subjunctive passive, the perfect and pluperfect are formed by the perfect passive participle, with sim for the perfect, with essem for the pluperfect. 3.

PRESENT IMPERFECT 

PERFECT

SUBJUNCTIVE OF Sum sim, sīs, sit, etc. essem, essēs, esset, etc. fuerim, fueris, fuerit, etc. 

PLUPERFECT fuissem, fuissēs, fuisset, etc.

The subjunctive tenses of sum are conjugated on page 419. 

4. DRILL. a. Conjugate the subjunctive tenses of vocō 

and sum, testing your speed. b. Give the subjunctive synopsis (first pers. sing.) of laudō and culpō, active and passive. 

5. EXERCISE. Pronounce and give the tense, person, number, voice, and mood of each verb: 

1. laudent, essētis, fueris. 2. sīmus, vocet, vocāvissent. 

3. vocārēs, culparent, fuerimus. 4. vocēris, vocārēminī, laudentur. 5. laudāverītis, laudātus esset, vocārentur. 6. datus sit, demus, darentur.

EXHORTATIONS 

3 

EXHORTATIONS 

6. RULE. The present subjunctive, first person plural, is used in independent sentences to express an exhortation. The negative with exhortations is nē, not.

EXAMPLES OF EXHORTATIONS : 

1. Ducem nostrum laudēmus. Let us praise our leader. 

2. Nē culpēmus hunc virum bonum. Let us not blame this good man. 

3. Fortēs simus. Let us be brave. 

4. A duce nostrō laudēmur. Let us be praised by our leader. 

OBSERVE. The subjunctive in an exhortation expresses the action merely as an idea, as something urged (or willed) by the speaker with reference to himself and certain others. 

7. EXERCISE. Express in Latin : 

1. Let us praise the courage of these citizens. 2. Let us not hesitate to blame those disloyal citizens. 3. Let us fight bravely for (pro) our country. 4. Let us not blame our very brave leader. 5. Let us give aid to him. 6. Let us call together all our friends. 8.

MARCUS AND SEXTUS DISCUSS THE LESSON 

Mārcus. — Hodiē multa cognovimus dē modō subiunctīvō. 

Ego tamen exīstimō modum subiūnctīvum esse difficilem. 

Sīmus vērō diligentēs, ita enim mox poterimus dicere hanc quoque rem nobis (for us) facilem esse. 

Sextus. Tu quidem vēra dīcis, nam mihi multa difficilia 5 sunt. Linguam nostram tamen pulcherrimam esse existimō. Cūram adhibēbimus, et mox etiam modum subiūnctivum intellegēmus optimē!

10. EXERCISE. 

Express in Latin: 

1. Let us ask Aemilius, a friend of Fabius, concerning the customs of the Gauls. 2. He was a trader many years in that part of Gaul which is called the "Province.” 3. He had explored many parts of Gaul and had learned the customs of many tribes. 4. He had often caught sight of the Germans, who lived across the Rhine. 5. Caesar decided to send the fourth cohort across the river, for the camp of the Germans was in sight. 

6. When that part of Germany has been explored (abl. abs.), he will decide to attack the camp of the Germans. 7. In sight of Caesar, Manlius had said to the fourth cohort: "Let us not be afraid; let us fight with great courage." 11.

A ROMAN BOY AND HIS MOTHER 

Marcus a schola domum iam revenerat. Dēpositīs librīs suis, mātrem petīvit; sed eam invenīre nōn poterat.

LATIN READING 

Celerrime in omnes partes domus contendit. Tandem illam in hortō conspexit. Ubi māter Marcum vidit, librum 

5 quem legebat deposuit et illum ad sẽ vocavit. 

Dextram (manum) leniter (gently) in capite fili sui posuit et dixit: "Pater tuus hodie Romam discessit. Epistulam enim ab Aemilio, propinquo suō, qui mercator Rōmānus est, acceperat. Itaque constituit statim ad urbem dis- 

Io cedere. Illius consilium pater dixit se accepturum esse. 

Id mihi exposuit; nam studet tibi disciplīnam (education) optimam dare. Magistri autem optimi sunt in urbe magna. 

Pater vērō pecuniam diligentissimē servavit, et filius noster optimōs magistrōs habere poterit. Ah! Quo modo ego 

15 sine te esse poterō? Dolorem tamen meum sustinēbō, neque me deficient animi. Sīmus fortes, Marce, et cōnsilium patris laudēmus." 

Tum Marcus respondit: "Te amo, māter mea carissima; itaque hoc consilium, quod propositum est, accipiam. In- 

20 tellegēs mē fidum esse discipulum, labōrābō enim diligentissimē. Tē, māter optima, non deficiam: puer Rōmānus sum nos quidem epistulas mittemus plūrimas, et ego reveniam domum saepe. Quando (when) te iterum cōnspiciam et in conspectu tuo manēbō? Semper te amābō."

LESSON 3 SUBJUNCTIVE MOOD: SECOND CONJUGATION AND POSSUM CLAUSES OF PURPOSE 

12. In studying the subjunctive of the second conjugation, you should observe carefully the present tense; the other tenses are formed as in the first conjugation.

In the following synopsis, pronounce carefully each tense; then conjugate each tense, changing the personal endings. 

SUBJUNCTIVE OF THE SECOND CONJUGATION 

ACTIVE 

PASSIVE PRESENT 

moneam monear

IMPERFECT monērem monerer PERFECT monuerim monitus (-a, -um) sim PLUPERFECT monuissem monitus (-a, -um) essem The subjunctive tenses of moneō are conjugated on page 412. 

OBSERVE. In the subjunctive active, the present is the present stem +-am; the imperfect is the present stem +-rem ; the perfect is the perfect stem +-erim; the pluperfect is the perfect stem + -issem. 

SLC. 2

SUBJUNCTIVE OF POSSUM 

In the subjunctive passive, the perfect and pluperfect are formed as in the first conjugation. 

13. DRILL. 1. With the book closed, give the subjunctive synopses (first pers. sing.) of moneō and video. 2. Conjugate each tense of these verbs, testing your speed. 14.

SUBJUNCTIVE OF Possum PRESENT 

possim, possīs, possit, etc.

IMPERFECT 

possem, possēs, posset, etc. PERFECT potuerim, potueris, potuerit, etc.

PLUPERFECT potuissem, potuissēs, potuisset, etc. 

The subjunctive tenses of possum are conjugated on page 420. 

15. EXERCISE. 

Pronounce and give the tense, person, number, voice, and mood of each verb: 

1. monērētis, monueris, monuissētis. 2. possimus, possēs, potuissent. 3. moneamur, monērēminī, monitus esset. 

4. possis, possent, potuerimus. 5. moniti simus, monuerint, monerentur, moneāris, monuerimus, monērēris.

CLAUSES OF PURPOSE 

16. In English, we may express the purpose of an act (a) by an infinitive, and (b) by a subordinate clause introduced by the conjunction that. Thus : 

1. We come to praise the soldiers. 

2. We come that we may praise the soldiers. 

3. We came that we might praise the soldiers. 

17. RULE. The subjunctive is used in a subordinate clause to express the purpose of an act. The clause of purpose, if affirmative, is introduced by ut, that, or in order that; if nega tive, by nē, that . . . not, or in order that . . . not.

CLAUSES OF PURPOSE 

9 

EXAMPLES OF CLAUSES OF PURPOSE : 

PRESENT 

1. Venīmus ut mīlitēs laudēmus. 

We come to praise the soldiers (lit., that we may praise the soldiers). 

2. Pugnant në servi sint.

PAST They fight that they may not be slaves (or not to be slaves).

I. Vēnimus ut mīlitēs laudārēmus. 

We came to praise the soldiers (lit., that we might praise the soldiers). 

2. Pugnabant nē servi essent.

They were fighting that they might not be slaves (or not to be slaves).

OBSERVE. The subjunctive is used in clauses of purpose because the action of the verb is expressed merely as an idea (as something intended or willed). Compare the use of the subjunctive in independent sentences to express an exhortation (6). 

In the clauses of purpose the tenses of the Latin subjunctive correspond to the English auxiliaries used to translate them: may (present), might (past).

USE OF TENSES IN CLAUSES OF PURPOSE 

18. RULE. After a present or future tense in the main clause, a present subjunctive is used in the clause of purpose. 

After a past tense in the main clause, the imperfect subjunctive is used in the clause of purpose. 

OBSERVE. - The main verb of a complex sentence places the whole sentence in a division of time (past, present, or future), and the verb of the subordinate clause must express action

IO 

in relation to the time indicated by the main clause. This grammatical principle is called Sequence of Tenses. See pages 485-487. 

19. EXERCISE. 

- Express in Latin : 

1. Let us remain that we may ask Aemilius concerning the customs of the Gauls. 2. He came back (reveniō) to Rome (Syntax 42) in order that his son might have the best teachers. 

3. Very many friends and relatives come to ask help. 4. They will remain in the city that they may have his advice and help. 5. In order that he might not be blamed, he gave aid to all his relatives. 6. In order that he might be moved by pity (misericordia -ae, f.), they said that they were most wretched.

THE CHIEFS ASK CAESAR'S HELP 

Ille princeps nōbilis, qui apud reliquōs maximam auctōritātem habebat, ita monuerat: "Sine auxilio Caesaris nōn poterimus Suēbōs expellere. Dux hostium, ut Galliam occupet, maximās cōpiās iam comparat. Hoc quidem prohibere audeamus! Interim Caesaris auxilium rogēmus 5 et ab eō moneāmur! Cōpiam frumenti interim ad castra 

Rōmāna supportēmus!"

Itaque, ut auxilium rogarent, ad castra Rōmāna trēs principēs nōbilissimī contendērunt. Ubi Caesar illōs ipse conspexit, cum equitibus multīs prōcessit et illōs in castra 10 

duxit. Consilium suum ita proposuit brevissimē: “Prī

mum illud iter occupēmus, quod est inter flumen et montēs. 

Hōc enim modo barbarōs intercludere poterimus." 

Praemissis equitibus, successit deinde decima legiō audācissimē. Interim principes ipsi cum parī virtute processe- 15 rant, ut equitibus darent auxilium.

II

ABLATIVE OF SEPARATION 

Repulsis hostibus, Caesar, convocatis omnibus principibus, qui frumentum ēmerant et supportāverant, ita addidit: "Romānī semper socii erunt Gallōrum.” 

22. EXERCISE. — Express in Latin : 

1. First, let us be advised by our friends. 2. Meanwhile, let us be eager to accept their opinions. 3. That we might have equal prudence, we consulted prudent friends. 4. When his home had been burned (abl. abs.), he sought the aid and opinion of prudent friends. 5. They carried up many things, which they themselves had bought, in order that he might have a supply of food. 

ABLATIVE OF SEPARATION 

23. RULE. The ablative, with or without the prepositions ā (ab), dē, ē (ex), is used with verbs meaning to separate, remove, etc., to express separation. 

1. Gallī Germānōs (ā) finibus suis prohibēbant.

The Gauls were keeping the Germans from their own territory. 

2. Castra ab Rhēnō tria passuum mīlia aberant.

The camp was distant three miles from the Rhine. 

3. Caesar vēnit ut principēs timōre liberāret. 

Caesar came to free the chiefs from fear.

NOTE. With liberō the ablative is regularly used without a preposition. A preposition is used with expressions of distance from, and with a distinct idea of motion from. The construction with absum has been used in previous lessons. 

24. EXERCISE. - Express in Latin : 

1. They will drive back the Suebi from the river. 

2. We shall free the Gauls from wretched slavery. 3. You (sing.) will go away from Caesar, to announce his victory. 4. Those towns, which had been burned, were not far from Caesar's camp. 5. Let us keep the Suebi from this part of Gaul.

30. EXERCISE. 

Express in Latin: 

1. Let us meanwhile give equal services to these friends. 

2. Let us be ruled by the examples of those noble men. 

3. Then the consul spoke with a loud voice, that he might be heard by all the citizens. 4. We ourselves hastened into the Forum that we might hear his voice. 5. Let us explain his plans to all our friends, in order that they may have good hope.

CLAUSES OF RESULT 

31. RULE. The subjunctive is used in a subordinate clause to express the result of an act mentioned in the main clause. A clause of result, if affirmative, is introduced by ut, that, so that; if negative, by ut. . . nōn, that not, so that not.

EXAMPLES OF CLAUSES OF RESULT: 

PRESENT 

1. Iter tam difficile est, ut prōcēdere nōn possimus. 

The road is so difficult that we cannot proceed. PAST 

2. Iter tam difficile erat, ut procedere non possēmus.

The road was so difficult that we could not proceed.

OBSERVE. In the main clause the adverbs tam, ita, or sic, so, are often used to point forward to the subordinate clause of result.

USE OF TENSES IN CLAUSES OF RESULT: 

32. RULE. After a present or future tense in the main clause, the present subjunctive is used in the clause of result. 

After a past tense in the main clause, the imperfect subjunctive is used in the clause of result. 

33. EXERCISE. - Express in Latin: 

1. The terms of peace were so just that the chiefs quickly accepted them. 2. The tenth legion came up so fiercely that the enemy fled. 3. The wounds of the soldier are so many that he remains in the camp. 4. Our leader's advice is so wise that it is accepted by all the citizens. 

5. You (sing.) wrote the letter in Latin (Latinē, adv.) so carefully that I could read it easily. 6. You had replied so quickly that I was not expecting the letter.

ST. PETER'S SEEN FROM THE VATICAN GARDENS LESSON 7 INDIRECT QUESTIONS. DATIVE OF POSSESSOR INDIRECT QUESTIONS 

42. An indirect question is a subordinate clause introduced by an interrogative word; it is used especially with verbs of asking, knowing, telling.

EXAMPLES OF INDIRECT QUESTIONS : 

PRIMARY TENSES (PRESENT AND FUTURE TIME) 

rogō, I ask rogābō, I shall ask rogāverō, I shall have asked a. quid existimēs. what you think. b. quid existimaveris. what you thought. 20

INDIRECT QUESTIONS 

21 

SECONDARY TENSES (PAST TIME) 

rogābam, I was asking a. quid dicerēs. rogāvi, I asked rogāveram, I had asked what you were saying. b. quid dixissēs. what you had said.

Do not confuse an indirect question with an 

OBSERVE. 

indirect statement.

We know where the boy is. (Indirect question.) 

We know that the boy is at home. 

(Indirect statement.) 

43. RULE. In an indirect question the subjunctive is used, and the tense follows the rule of sequence of tenses (pp. 485-487). 

44. EXERCISE. 

Express in Latin : 

1. I shall ask in what city he lives. 2. He told me why (cur) he had come to Rome. 3. He was telling us what cities he had seen. 4. We learned what he thought concerning our city. 5. He told you (sing.) what plans he was undertaking. 

6. Let us ask him when (quandō) his brother came back. 

45. RULE. 

DATIVE OF POSSESSOR 

The dative is used in the predicate with the verb sum to indicate the possessor, the thing possessed being the subject. 

I. Nōbis sunt librī multi.

We have many books (lit., To us are many books). 

46. EXERCISE. - Express in Latin, with dative of possessor: 

1. This farmer has a large field. 2. He also has many horses. 

3. He has a larger farmhouse than his neighbors. 4. All farmers have large gardens. 5. Many also have very many beautiful trees. 6. That boy, who has very many books, is studying very diligently.

(A Restoration) LESSON 9 CUM-TEMPORAL CLAUSES. CUM-CAUSAL CLAUSES 51. PRELIMINARY REVIEW. Review the subjunctive of vocō, moneō, regō, capiō, audio, sum, and possum. Write, side by side, the active and passive synopses of each verb. Give each synopsis orally, testing your speed. 

Cum-TEMPORAL CLAUSES 

52. RULE. A temporal clause introduced by cum, when, while, takes its verb in the indicative to express an action as a definite fact and at a definite time. With the imperfect and pluperfect tenses the subjunctive is regularly used; the clause is then called a cum-circumstantial clause. 26

CUM-TEMPORAL CLAUSES 

27 

EXAMPLES OF Cum-TEMPORAL CLAUSES : 

1. Cum eum vidēbō, dē patre eius rogābō. When I (shall) see him, I shall ask about his father. 

2. Cum haec diceret, reliqui discesserunt. saying this, the rest went away. 

While he was 3. Cum haec dixisset, ipse discessit. When he had said this, he himself went away.

OBSERVE. 

A cum-circumstantial clause (2 and 3) is felt merely as giving an idea of the circumstances (not with the emphasis of a definite fact taking place at a definite time). 

53. RULE. 

Cum-CAUSAL CLAUSES 

A causal clause introduced by cum, since, because, has in all tenses the subjunctive.

EXAMPLES OF Cum-CAUSAL CLAUSES : 

1. Cum in urbe sim, templum videre possum. Since I am in the city, I can see the temple. 

2. Cum templum vīderim, tibi dē eō nārrābō. Since I have seen the temple, I shall tell you about it. 

54. EXERCISE. 

Express in Latin : 

1. When I had asked him where he lived, he replied that he lived in Italy. 2. Since I had not been in Italy myself, I asked him about Rome. 3. When he was telling about Rome, he showed me many beautiful pictures. 4. While we were looking at these, his little brother came to see the pictures. 5. When I asked the little boy what pictures he had seen, he said that he had often seen all those pictures. 6. When he had said this, he went away. 7. I had not asked him why he was going away. 8. When he had come back, we looked at the rest of the pictures. 9. He did not know that Rome was so beautiful. 

10. He said that he also was eager to see Italy.

Caesar Fabio suo S(alutem) D(icit)

Dē Mānliō amīcō tuō pauca scrībam. Magnam fortitudinem ostendit in negōtiō difficillimo quod ipse susceperat. 

Cum enim portam custōdīret, hostēs plūrimī subitō adpropinquabant.

In castris erant pauci militēs nostri: multi in agrōs 5 finitimōs, ut frumentum compararent, missī erant. Princeps barbarōrum, qui suōs longē antecesserat, prō portā constitit et tēlum coniecit. Tum Manlius laudis cupidissimus, gladiō correptō, scūtō prōtēctus (protected) sẽ prōiēcit et in ducem hostium impetum fecit. Pugnabat cum tantā 10 fortitudine et cupiditāte ut princeps ad suos, qui iam accēdēbant, fugere inciperet. Instābat (pressed on) Mānlius nē princeps ā suīs ēriperētur. Tēlum, quod forte (by chance) 29

Since he knew that three cohorts had been sent across the river, he decided to attack the camp. 5. He hoped that in this way he would rescue his hostages.

SUBJECTIVE GENITIVE AND OBJECTIVE GENITIVE 

59. RULE. With a noun expressing action or feeling a subjective genitive indicates the doer of the action or the one subject to the feeling. An objective genitive indicates the object toward which the action or feeling is directed. 

1. Auxilium amīcōrum quaesīvimus. We sought the aid of friends. 

2. Timor mortis non omnēs tangit. Fear of death does not touch all.

LESSON 11 CLAUSES OF PURPOSE. GENITIVE AND ABLATIVE OF QUALITY OR DESCRIPTION. DEPONENT VERBS 

RELATIVE CLAUSES OF PURPOSE 

61. RULE. A clause of purpose may be introduced by a relative pronoun. The antecedent of the relative is usually the direct object in the main clause. 

EXAMPLES OF RELATIVE CLAUSES OF PURPOSE: 

1. Amicum, qui me rogaret, misit. He sent a friend to ask me (lit., who should ask me). 

2. Hic amicus mihi epistulam, quam legerem, dedit. This friend gave me a letter to read (lit., which I should read). 

3. Servos duos dēligebat, qui domum custōdirent. choosing two slaves to guard the home. 

He was 4. Eis scuta dabit, quibus se defendant. To them he will give shields with which to defend themselves. 

5. Ego unum servum mittam, qui reliquos admoneat. I shall send one slave to warn the rest.

GENITIVE AND ABLATIVE OF QUALITY OR DESCRIPTION 

62. RULE. A noun with an adjective may be used in the genitive or ablative as an adjective phrase to describe a noun or pronoun. 32 32

DEPONENT VERBS 

33 

EXAMPLES OF PHRASES OF DESCRIPTION : 

1. vir magnae fidei (or magnā fidē), a man of great loyalty. 

2. puer magnō capite, a boy with a large head. 

3. iter paucōrum diērum, a journey of a few days. 

4. mūrus magnae altitūdinis, a wall of great height. 

OBSERVE. 

Physical characteristics are usually expressed by the ablative; measure, size, and weight, usually by the genitive. 

DEPONENT VERBS 

63. Certain verbs are active in meaning, but have the form of the passive. Such verbs are called deponent since they put aside (deponere) most of the active forms.

EXAMPLES OF Deponent Verbs : 

verēri, to fear proficisci, to set out 

I. cōnor, cōnāri, I attempt, I try to attempt 

II. vereor, I fear 

III. proficiscor, I set out 

III-iō. patior, pati, I allow, I suffer to allow 

IV. experior, experiri, I try, test to try

A LOYAL FRIEND 

Ad hanc urbem profectus amicum misisti, qui mihi persuādēret (61). Cum ad mē vēnisset, ego eum rogavi quid ipse sciret. Dixit të cupere auxilium meum habere. Tum rogāvī cur nōn tū ipse Rōmam profectus essēs. Sciēbam enim te multōs amīcōs familiārēs in urbe habere. Respondit s tē iam ab eīs omnibus auxilium petīvisse. Nesciēbam ego cuius auxilium iam experīrī possēs. Tu quidem scīs mē magna fidē esse. Ut amīcōs tuōs ipse rogarem, Rōmam proficisci constitui. Cum proficiscerer, mātrem rogāvī quã in parte urbis amīcī tui habitārent. Respondit sẽ 10 ipsam scire et saepe in illa parte urbis fuisse. Māter existimābat amīcōs tuōs tē sine auxilio esse nōn passūrōs esse. "Cum illi," inquit, "sint amīcī magnā fidē, nōn patientur eum sine auxiliō esse.” Deinde mäter mihi dedit hanc epistulam, quam illīs darem (61). Tū vērō ipse es magnae 15 virtutis et amīcōrum fidem saepe experiēris.

LESSON 12 SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF PURPOSE SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF RESULT SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF PURPOSE 

67. RULE. After verbs meaning to urge, persuade, command, the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause to express the action willed. The clause is introduced by ut, that, if affirmative; by nē, that . . . not, if negative.

EXAMPLES OF SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF PURPOSE: 

1. Mīlitibus imperat ut maneant. to remain. 

2. Militibus imperāvit nē manērent. not to remain. 

He orders the soldiers 

He ordered the soldiers 3. Principibus persuādēbit ut pacem accipiant. He will persuade the chiefs to accept peace. 36

SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF PURPOSE AND RESULT 37 

4. Caesaris consilium accipient, ut reveniant. They will accept Caesar's advice, that they come back (or to come back). 

NOTE. - Iubeō, I order, takes an accusative with the infinitive :

Mē manĕre iussit. He ordered me to remain. 

In examples 1, 2, and 3 the subordinate clause serves as the thing object of the main verb; in 4 the subordinate clause is an appositive, explaining the noun consilium. A clause used like a noun (or substantive) is called a substantive clause. 

68. EXERCISE. Express in Latin : 

1. He ordered the soldiers to set out from the camp at daybreak and attack the town. (a) With imperō, (b) with iubeō. 2. They could not persuade the leader to accept terms of peace. 3. They beg (implōrō) the leader not to burn their homes.

SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF RESULT 

69. RULE.With the impersonal verb accidit, it happens, it happened, also with certain verbs of making (faciō, make, efficio, bring about, accomplish), the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause of result, introduced by ut, that, so that. The negative is nōn.

EXAMPLES OF SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF Result: 

1. Accidit ut pater eius absit. His father happens to be away (lit., It happens that his father is away). 

2. Accidit ut pater eius abesset. His father happened to be away (lit., It happened that his father was away). 

3. Efficiet ut hostes fugiant. He will cause the enemy to flee (lit., He will accomplish that the enemy flee). 

4. Effectum est ut hostes fugerent. The flight of the enemy was brought about (lit., It was brought about that the enemy fled).

LATIN READING 70. EXERCISE. 

Express in Latin : 

1. It happens that we fear war. 2. He brought it about that the unfaithful citizens did not remain in the city. 

3. He made the bad citizens obey the laws of the state.

THE VESTALS (From a painting by LeRoux) LESSON 13 GERUND. FUTURE PASSIVE PARTICIPLE GERUNDIVE PHRASES A VERBAL NOUN 

72. In English a verbal noun is formed from a verb by adding -ing; thus, from the verb read, we have the verbal noun reading, as in the following sentences: 

1. Reading gives great pleasure. 

2. We learn many things by reading. 

3. These books are suitable for reading. 

OBSERVE. 

- A verbal noun in -ing should not be confused with the present participle of the verb: I saw him reading.

THE GERUND 

73. In Latin most verbs have in the active voice a verbal noun called the gerund. The gerund is a neuter noun of the second declension; it has no nominative and no plural. For the nominative the infinitive is used. 39

THE GERUND 

74.

DECLENSION OF THE GERUND 

NOM. 

GEN. vocandi monendi regendi capiendi audiendi

DAT. vocando monendō regendō capiendo audiendo 

Acc. vocandum monendum regendum capiendum audiendum

ABL. Vocandō monendō regendō capiendō audiendō 

OBSERVE. The gerund is formed by adding -ndi to the present stem with certain changes in quantity and spelling.

EXAMPLES OF THE GERUND: 

GENITIVE 

DATIVE Voluptatem cognoscendi habēmus. We have the pleasure of learning.

Amīcī idōnei sunt monendo. Friends are suitable to advise (lit., for advising). 

ACCUSATIVE Ad laudandum vēnērunt. They came to praise (lit., to (or for) praising).

ABLATIVE 

Multa cognovimus audiendo. We learned many things by listening.

NOTE. Instead of the dative of the gerund, ad with the accusative is generally used.

FUTURE PASSIVE PARTICIPLE 

75. 

I. vocandus, -a, -um, II. monendus, -a, -um, to-be-called to-be-advised 

III. regendus, -a, -um, to-be-ruled 

III-iō. capiendus, -a, -um, to-be-taken 

IV. audiendus, -a, -um, to-be-heard

FUTURE PASSIVE PARTICIPLE AS GERUNDIVE 

76. If a future passive participle is used as an attributive adjective with a noun or pronoun in the genitive, dative, accusative,

THE GERUNDIVE 

41 or ablative case, the adjective often carries the leading idea of the phrase, and is then called a gerundive. The phrase is usually translated in English by a verbal noun with an object. 

EXAMPLES OF THE GERUNDIVE : 

GENITIVE 

DATIVE 

ABLATIVE 

Spem Romae videndae habēmus. We have hope of seeing Rome (lit., of Rome to-be-seen). 

Vix armis capiendis tempus dabātur. Scarcely was time given to seize arms (lit., for arms tobe-seized). 

ACCUSATIVE Ad urbem videndam veniēmus. We shall come to see the city (lit., to the city to-be-seen). 

Manlius de castrīs mūniendis cognoverat. Manlius had learned about building a camp (lit., about a camp to-be-built).

NOTE. 

Instead of a gerundive phrase in the dative, ad with the accusative of the phrase is generally used.

INDEFINITE PRONOUNS 

81. The indefinite pronoun aliquis, m. f., aliquid, n., some one, something, is declined in the singular like the interrogative pronoun quis, m. f., quid, n., who? what? (p. 408). The adjective is aliquis (aliqui), m., aliqua, f., aliquod, n., some (p. 408). The nominative singular feminine and the nominative and accusative plural neuter have -qua. 

Other indefinite pronouns (and adjectives) are quidam, m., quaedam, f., quiddam (quoddam), n., a certain (one), something, pl. certain ones, some; indef. adj. certain, some; quisque, m., quaeque, f., quidque (quodque), n., each, every; neuter, each thing, everything (p. 408).

LESSON DATIVE WITH COMPOUND VERBS. DATIVE OF PURPOSE DATIVE WITH Compound Verbs 

86. If a preposition is prefixed to a Latin verb, the construction used with the compound verb should be carefully studied. 

Certain compound verbs are transitive and take a direct object (accusative). Other compound verbs are intransitive and take only an indirect object (dative).

EXAMPLES OF THE DATIVE WITH COMPOUND VERBS: 

I. Amicus mihi occurrit. 

A friend met me. 

2. Finitimis bellum inferebant.

They were bringing war upon their neighbors. 

3. Manlius decimae legiōnī praepositus est. 

Manlius was placed in command of the tenth legion. 

OBSERVE. If the simple verb is transitive, the compound verb may take a direct object (accusative) in addition to the indirect object taken by the compound as a whole (as in 2). 

87. RULE. The dative of the indirect object is used with many (but not all) verbs compounded with ad, ante, circum, con-, in, inter, ob, post, prae, prō, sub, and super. 

DATIVE OF PURPOSE 

88. RULE. The dative is used to indicate the purpose for which something is done, or the thing for which something serves, often with another dative indicating the person with reference to whom the statement is made. 45

DATIVE OF PURPOSE EXAMPLES OF THE DATIVE OF PURPOSE: 

1. Caesar duas legiōnēs praesidiō misit.

Caesar sent two legions as protection (lit., for-a-protection). 

2. Amicitia est magnō auxiliō.

Friendship is a great help (lit., for-a-great-help). 

3. Militēs praesidiō oppidānīs mittet. 

He will send soldiers as a protection to the townsmen. 

4. Amicitia nostra erit mihi auxiliō. 

Our friendship will be a help to me.

OBSERVE. — In English a predicate appositive, describing the direct object, is used (as in 1 and 3); with the verb sum, a predicate noun, describing the subject, is used (as in 2 and 4).

1. Accidit ut Manlius decimae legiōni praeesset. 

2. Huic legiōnī ā Caesare ipso praepositus erat. Paulō post Caesar in Italiam ad alias legiōnēs cōnscribendās profectus erat. 4. Nē Helvetii flūmen transirent, Labiēnum mūnītiōnibus praefēcerat. 5. Helvetii quasdam gentēs virtute praecēdēbant. 

6. Amīcī duo mē hortabantur ut sibi auxilio essem. 

Cuique amicō saepe aliquod auxilium dederam.

THE PERSIAN INVASIONS 

47 

THE PERSIAN INVASIONS 

O sons of Greeks, go set your country free! 

Robert Browning, translating Aeschylus. 

91. The conquest of Asia Minor drew the Persians on to attempt that of European Greece. It seemed easy, for the 

Greeks were disunited, and Persian In resources were gigantic. In 492 B.C. 

King Darius sent the first expedition, which merely reached Mount Athos, where the fleet was wrecked. 

490, a second fleet landed troops at

Marathon, only to be terribly defeated by the Athenians, led by 

Miltiades. In 480 came a third effort; Darius' son and successor, Xerxes, appeared with an enormous army and fleet; the latter was crippled at Salamis, and that part of the army which remained in Greece was annihilated at Plataea by the Spartans. Persia was foiled.

ΠΕΡΙΚΛΗΣ 

ZANBINDY ΑΘΗΝΑΙΟΣ PERICLES 

(During the rule of Pericles the civilization of Greece entered its golden age.) 

Had she won, Greece would have been enslaved and submerged. As it was, the Greeks were elated by their triumph, and at Athens literature and the arts burst into enduring bloom; noble temples rose, supreme sculptors chiseled, comedy and tragedy flourished, while wise interpreters of life, scholarly historians, and profound philosophers taught or wrote. All this has become our inheritance.

SUGGESTED READINGS: 

Morey, Outlines of Greek History, pp. 168-192.

Davis, A Victor of Salamis (an historical novel).

ACTIVE PERIPHRASTIC CONJUGATION 

94. The future active participle and the verb sum form a verb phrase expressing immediate futurity or intention:

I. Dictūrus est. He is about-to-say; is going-to-say; intendsto-say. 

2. Dictūrus erat. He was about-to-say; was going-to-say; intended-to-say.

OBSERVE. The participle expresses the action of the verb as future in relation to the time (past, present, or future) indicated by the form of sum (Syntax 68–71).

PASSIVE PERIPHRASTIC CONJUGATION 

95.

The future passive participle and the verb sum form a verb phrase expressing necessity or obligation: 

I. Urbs defendenda est. The city is to-be-defended; must (or should) be defended. 48

PASSIVE PERIPHRASTIC 

49 

2. Urbs defendenda erat. The city was to-be-defended; must be defended; had to be defended.

OBSERVE. - The participle expresses necessity or obligation in relation to the time indicated by the form of sum.

DATIVE OF IMPLIED AGENT 96. RULE.With the passive periphrastic conjugation the dative (of reference) is used to indicate the implied agent. 

I. Urbs nōbis dēfendenda est.

The city must (or should) be defended by us; We must (or should) defend the city. 

2. Urbs nōbis defendenda erat.

The city must (or should, or had to) be defended by us; 

We had to defend the city. 

NOTE. The passive periphrastic conjugation of intransitive verbs is used only impersonally : 

3. Mihi nōn manendum est. I must (or should) not remain. 

4. Vōbis manendum erit. You will have to remain.

CICERO (106-43 B.C.) 

Rome's least mortal mind. Byron. 

99. Marcus Tullius Cicero, greatest of Roman orators, and one of the greatest of all men of letters, was born at Arpinum in Latium. He studied with fa- mous teachers, became an advocate, and rose to the consulship in 63 B.C., when he suppressed 

Catiline's conspiracy, and was saluted as Pater Patriae. When 

Caesar formed the First Triumvirate, Cicero left politics for literature. After Caesar's death, he entered the struggle against Antony, who took mortal offense and demanded his destruction. Cicero met his fate with firmness, and was slain by one whom he had defended.

Of his amazing literary output there survive some translations, seven works on oratory, eleven on philosophical subjects, seventy-seven orations, and about eight hundred letters. 

CICERO 

In the words of Augustus, he was vir eloquens et patriae amāns, an eloquent man, and loved his country well."

Collins, Ancient Classics for English Readers, " Cicero." 

Mackail, Latin Literature, pp. 62–77. 

Dryden, Plutarch, "Cicero."

Strachan-Davidson, Cicero, chapters i-v.

LATIN READING: Why Titus Manlius Was Called "Tor quatus," p. 80.

CHARACTER TRAINING BY EXAMPLE 

104. The Romans in their family life emphasized for the children certain definite qualities, which they believed were distinctly Roman: patientia, patience; constantia, steadfastness; gravitas, seriousness; dignitas, dignity; fortitudō, courage; virtus, manliness; fides, loyalty; pietas, devotion. 

These qualities, developed in the difficult days of Rome's early history, remained in the Roman ideal (the mōs maiōrum, the custom of the ancestors).

The Scipionic Circle, named for the Younger Scipio 

(d. 129 B.C.), comprised distinguished men who loved Greek literature for its own sake, and lent the weight of their influence against the sentiment that the pursuit of Greek culture tended to luxury and affected the simple virtues of the Romans. 

Aristocratic homes cherished the family traditions, and by stories of heroism and fidelity to duty, taught the young to be worthy of their forefathers. 

SUGGESTED READINGS: 

Johnston, Private Life of the Romans, pp. 67-86.

Wilkins, Roman Education. 

Fowler, Social Life at Rome, pp. 168–203. 

A GREEK COIN

LESSON CLAUSES WITH VERBS OF FEARING. CLAUSES WITH NEGATIVE EXPRESSIONS OF DOUBT CLAUSES WITH VERBS OF FEARING 

105. RULE. With expressions of fear the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause introduced, if affirmative, by ne, that, lest; if negative, by ut, that . . . not.

EXAMPLES OF CLAUSES WITH VERBS OF FEARING : 

I. Vereor nē ille absit. 

I fear that he is (or may be) absent. 

2. Verēbar nē ille abesset. 

I feared that he was (or might be) absent. 3. Verēbāmur ut ille revenisset. 

We feared that he had not come back. 

4. Timēbātis ut principēs auxilium ferrent. 

You feared that the chiefs might not bring aid.

OBSERVE. After verbs of fearing në loses its negative force and is translated that. This is because the clause was expressed in early Latin as an independent sentence (a negative wish): 

5. Vereor. Nē ille absit! 

I fear. May he not be absent!

CLAUSES WITH NEGATIVE EXPRESSIONS OF DOUBT 55 CLAUSES WITH NEGATIVE EXPRESSIONS OF DOUBT 

107. RULE. With negative expressions of doubt or questions of doubt implying a negative answer, the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause introduced by quin, that. 

EXAMPLES WITH NEGATIVE EXPRESSIONS OF DOUBT : 

1. Nōn dubito quin ita sit. 

I do not doubt that it is so. 

2. Non dubitabam quin ita esset. 

I did not doubt that it was so. 

3. Dubitasne quin vēra dixerit? 

Do you doubt that he spoke the truth? 

4. Dubitābāsne quin vēra dixisset? 

Were you doubting that he had spoken the truth?

LIVY LIVY (59 B.C.-A.D. 17) 

Livy's pictured page. - Byron. 

110. Like Catullus, Vergil, and Nepos, Titus Livius was born in Northern Italy. After a careful education at his birthplace, Patavium (Padua), he went to Rome, where he met the most eminent men of the day, including Augustus. He lived for his literary work, and died in his native town. His History of Rome, Ab Urbe Condită (to the Death of Drusus, 9 B.C.), was in no less than 142 books, of which thirty-five remain. Livy is an unequaled story-teller, rather than an accurate historian. 

His purpose was to rekindle Roman patriotism by the examples of the glorious past.

SUGGESTED READINGS: 

Collins, Ancient Classics for English Readers, "Livy."

Laing, Masterpieces of Latin Literature, "Hannibal Cross ing the Alps," pp. 362-372. 

Mackail, Latin Literature, pp. 145-155.

THE ANCIENT HARBORS OF CARTHAGE LESSON 19 ADVERSATIVE CLAUSES. SUBORDINATE CLAUSES IN INDIRECT DISCOURSE ADVERSATIVE CLAUSES 

111. RULE.- Adversative clauses introduced by cum, although, take the subjunctive.

EXAMPLES OF ADVERSATIVE CLAUSES : 

1. Cum utrumque iter difficile sit, (tamen) ībimus.

Although each road is difficult, (yet) we shall go. 

2. Quae cum ita essent, Caesar in Britanniam transiit. 

Although these things were so, Caesar crossed into Britain. 

OBSERVE. An adversative clause states a circumstance that tends to oppose the statement of the main clause. The adversative relation of the cum clause is often indicated by tamen, yet, still, in the main clause. 57

SUBORDINATE CLAUSES IN INDIRECT DISCOURSE SUBORDINATE CLAUSES IN INDIRECT DISCOURSE 

112. RULE. A subordinate clause in indirect discourse has its verb in the subjunctive. The verb of the subordinate clause follows the law of sequence of tenses.

Examples of SUBORDINATE CLAUSES IN INDIRECT DIscourse : 

I. DICIT Belgās fortissimōs esse, quod cum Germānīs bellum gerant. 

He SAYS that the Belgae are the bravest, because they are waging war with the Germans. 

2. DIXIT Belgās fortissimōs esse, quod cum Germānīs bellum gererent.

He SAID that the Belgae were the bravest, because they were waging war with the Germans. 

113. EXERCISE. 

Read and translate : 

1. Liber quem legō est bonus. 2. Dīcit librum quem legat esse bonum. 3. Dixit librum quem legeret esse bonum. 4. Dixit se librum totum legisse quem ego sibi dedissem. 5. Dixit sẽ, cum aegerrimus esset, tamen prō patriā suā labōrāre. 

114. EXERCISE. - Express in English : 

1. Although many friends were present, yet no one dared to defend Catiline. 2. They feared that they might be blamed by the consul. 3. Cicero said that he would prevent the plans of those who had remained in the city. 4. He did not doubt that their plans could be prevented. 5. He thought that all who had resisted Catiline should be praised.

59 

THE PUNIC WARS "Dēlenda est Carthagō."-Cato. 

115. Carthage, a rich and powerful Phoenician (Punic) colony near Tunis in Africa, realized that Rome was dangerous to her trade and empire, - and hostilities arose from a trifling pretext. 

The First Punic War lasted from 264-241 B.C. Rome won by 

COIN OF CARTHAGE 

the strength of her fleet and the initial mistakes of Carthage. 

The latter lost Sicily, which became a Roman province. Presently Rome took away Corsica and Sardinia also. 

But Carthage had now found a great commander, Hamilcar Barca, who, foreseeing another war, developed Southeastern 

Spain as a military base.

From this base his son Hannibal in the Second Punic War (219-202 B.C.) crossed Gaul and the Alps and attacked the 

Romans in Italy, inflicting terrible defeats. But the city of Rome was impregnable, and the allies did not desert her. 

Hannibal had to cross to Africa, where his weakened army was defeated by Scipio at Zama (202 B.C.). Carthage lost her power and her commerce.

The Third Punic War (146-143 B.C.) was due to the great 

Italian landowners, who wished to end Carthaginian trade rivalry and to possess estates in Africa. For three years Carthage offered a heroic resistance, and then was "blotted out." 

Our modern world has profited by the defeat of Carthage, for, in spite of this great wrong, Carthaginian supremacy would have meant an inferior culture for the West; and Rome might 

SLC.-5

THE PUNIC WARS 

never have been able to perform her supreme service, keeping back the barbarians of the north till the best of the ancient civilization had penetrated human life so deeply that nothing could destroy it.

LESSON RELATIVE CLAUSES OF CHARACTERISTIC RELATIVE CLAUSES OF CHARACTERISTIC 

116. RULE. The subjunctive is used in relative clauses of characteristic, which describe or classify the antecedent by stating a characteristic or tendency.

EXAMPLES OF RELATIVE CLAUSES OF CHARACTERISTIC: 

I. SUNT itinera duo, quibus exire possint. 2. 

THERE ARE two roads by which they can go out. 

ERANT itinera duo, quibus exire possent.

THERE WERE two roads by which they could go out. 

3. VĒNERANT multi qui cum Catilīnā cōnsentirent. 

THERE HAD COME many who sympathized with Catiline.

117. EXERCISE. 

Read and translate : 

1. Quis est qui aquilam in proelium prōferre audeat? 

2. Nēmō fuit quem Caesar saepius laudāret. 3. Suntne alii ducēs quibus cōnfīdere possimus? 4. Nihil aliud est quod homines melius gubernet (guides) quam cōnscientia (conscience). 5. Plūrimi erant qui ex urbe iam exissent. 

118. EXERCISE. Express in Latin : 

1. I have many other books which I wish to read often. 

2. There is one book which has brought pleasure to many friends. 3. Although many praise that new book, yet I do not wish to read it. 4. I fear that this book would not be a help (88) to me. 5. I intend to read only books that can help (prōsum) me (86), 61

LESSON EXPRESSIONS WITH VERBS OF HINDERING EXPRESSIONS WITH VERBS OF HINDERING 119. RULE. 

With verbs of hindering and preventing, the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause introduced by nē, quominus, or quin. Nē is used when the main verb is affirmative, quin when it is negative, quominus in either case.

EXAMPLES WITH VERBS OF HINDERING: 

1. Mē prohibuit nē (or quōminus) irem.

He prevented me from going (lit., that I should not go). 

2. Eum prohibēre nōn poterāmus quōminus (or quin) iret.

We could not prevent him from going. 

3. Haeduōs dēterrēbit nē frūmentum conferant. 

He will prevent the Haedui from collecting grain. 

NOTE. 

With prohibeō the infinitive is often used: 

4. Mē ire prohibuit. 

He prevented me from going (lit., me to go). 

120. EXERCISE. Read and translate : 

1. Caesar Germānōs tōtam Galliam occupare prohibuit. 

2. Caesar eōs prohibuit nē Galliam occuparent. 3. Gallī ipsī Germānōs impedire non poterant quominus (or quin) 

Rhēnum transirent. 4. Hostēs prohibēbimus patriam nostram occupare. 5. Cīvēs infidōs prohibeamus (Syntax 

73, a) nē (or quōminus) hostibus auxilium dent. 62

THEREBY YOU HAVE LAID THE ROMAN PEOPLE UNDER A MIGHTY OBLIGATION, AND YOU HONOR YOUR FATHER- LAND. YOU HAVE GAINED THE HIGHEST GLORY AND A TRIUMPH WHICH IS TO BE PREFERRED TO THE TRIUMPH OF THE GREATEST GENERAL: FOR IT IS A NOBLER THING TO ENLARGE THE BOUNDARIES OF THE MIND THAN THOSE OF AN EMPIRE." PART ONE LATIN READINGS 1. THE CRESTED LARK (After Lesson 8) 

Avicula parva nōmine cassīta nīdum in segete posuerat. Frumentum iam mātūrum erat, sed pulli (fledglings) cassītae adhūc minimī et involucres erant. Prō pullīs māter timēbat, nam existimabat agricolam paucīs diebus in segetem ventūrum 5 esse ut frumentum colligeret. Itaque pullōs convocavit et admonuit: "Hodiē sī agricola ad segetem veniet, audīte eum et mihi, cum revēnerō, nārrāte omnia." Deinde ad flūmen āvolāvit, ut pābulum pullīs inveniret et ad nīdum reportaret.

NEPOS (about 100-24 B.C.) 

Cornelius Nepos, born in Northern Italy, wrote in sixteen books a work entitled Dē Viris Illustribus, Illustrious Men. 

It consists of biographies of famous Greeks, set side by side with Romans of similar characters and careers. The author writes interestingly, and with fairness and sympathy. 

Nepos was a friend of Cicero and of Atticus, and also of Catullus, who dedicated a volume of poems to him. 

THERMOPYLÆ 

Of those who at Thermopyla were slain, Glorious the doom and beautiful the lot; 

Their tomb an altar men from tears refrain 

To honor them, and praise, but mourn them not.

Such sepulchre, nor drear decay Nor all-destroying time shall waste; this right have they. 

Within their grave the home-bred glory

Of Greece was laid this witness gives Leonidas the Spartan, in whose story 

A wreath of famous virtue ever lives. 

- Simonides (translated by John Sterling)

8. WHY TITUS MANLIUS WAS CALLED "TORQUATUS" 

The mighty chief of the invading 

(After Lesson 17) 

Rōmāni cum Gallis, qui in Italiam descenderant, bellum gerebant (361 B.C.). Iam ad pontem, à quo non longe Galli constiterant, pervenerant. Cum essent proelium initūrī, Gallus quidam Gauls. 

THE CONQUERING ROMAN EAGLE 

scutum longum gerens et gladiōs duos atque aurō multō 5 ōrnātus, in prīmam aciem prōcessit. Ille quidem reliquōs 

Gallōs magnitudine corporis superabat. 

He defies the single com- bat. 

Commissō proelio, iam utrimque summō Romans to studiō pugnābātur (Syntax 15, b), cum ille et Gallis et Rōmānīs manu significare coepit. 10 "Desistite pugnare," inquit. Silentio facto, ille vōce magna dixit, "Procedat (Syntax 73, b), si quis mēcum dē victoria pugnare vult." Audēbat nēmō propter illīus magnitudinem. Deinde Gallus Rōmānōs rīdēre coepit.

Manlius

Id Titō Manlio, nōbili iuvenī, magnō dolōrī 15 accepts the erat. "Cūr nēmō," inquit, "ex tantō exercitū challenge. audet contra illum prōdire?" Cum hoc dixisset, statim ipse processit neque passus est virtutem Rōmānam ā Gallō spoliārī. Scutum pedestre et gladium 

Hispanicum habebat; contra Gallum in medio ponte 20 

cōnstitit in cōnspectū utriusque exercitūs. . 

He overthrows the 

Gaul, and despoils him of his necklace.

Gallus cum magnō clāmōre scūtum prōtendit. 

Manlius autem, animo magis quam viribus cōnfīsus, scūtum scūtō percussit. Dum Gallus locum suum recuperare vult, Manlius iterum 25 scutum percussit et illum de locō iterum dēiēcit. 

Hōc modō sub gladium Gallicum successit et gladiō suō hostem occidit, torquem rapuit et in collō suō imposuit. 

Quo ex facto Manlius ipse posterīque (descendants) eius appellabantur "Torquati."

AULUS GELLIUS 

A certain man of letters, Aulus Gellius, lived in or near Athens some time in the earlier part of the second century A.D. He spent the long winter evenings in composing the Noctes Atticae, or Evenings in Athens; his purpose was to amuse his children. 

The work is in twenty books, most of which have come down to us, a huge scrapbook of anecdotes, fragments of history, poetical quotations, etc., much of which is valuable.

HADRIAN'S VILLA, AT TIBUR (TIVOLI) SUETONIUS 

Gaius Suetonius Tranquillus, historian and scholar, is famous for his Lives of the Twelve Caesars, namely, of the first twelve emperors, from Julius Caesar to Domitian. These biographies, filled with interesting anecdotes, present a lifelike and intimate portrait of each emperor, often with sensational exaggerations. 

Only a part of his encyclopaedia remains. It was devoted to poets, orators, philosophers, historians, rhetoricians, and grammarians; nearly the whole of the last section survives, together with three lives of poets, Terence, Horace, Lucan, and fragments of the life of the elder Pliny. Suetonius was a friend of the younger Pliny, who secured for him important favors, and did him the honor of addressing him in several of his published letters. Later the historian became the private secretary of the emperor Hadrian.

(Written about 130 B.C.)

ROMAN WOMEN 

Roman women were far superior in dignity and in social importance to their sisters in Greece. We note a difference, however, between the old-fashioned woman of the early centuries and the emancipated woman of the time of Cicero.

Roman life long remained simple, and Roman women were expected to be serious-minded and old-fashioned in their virtues and strength of character. Gradually, however, the influence of Greek culture was strongly felt in their life and manners. The early ideals for Roman women continued to be expressed in memorial inscriptions, which are very numerous. 

Roman women commanded respect, and were expected to have certain of the old-time qualities: frugalitās (thrift), dignitās (dignity), gravitās (seriousness), and sānctitās (purity). They had important responsibilities in the home, especially the education of the children and the developing in them of the qualities

ROMAN WOMEN 

95 

BASILICA JULIA IN ROME 

(Its construction was begun by Julius Caesar) of character in which the family took pride. These qualities were vividly presented in the history of the family; if it was aristocratic, the birthdays of ancestors were observed, and the family portraits in the atrium were the object of reverential regard.

In the time of Cicero, a Roman woman shared her husband's intellectual, political, and financial interests, and, on social occasions, met his friends on a basis of equality. Many women, like Cornelia, mother of the Gracchi, and Aurelia, mother of Julius Caesar, were conspicuous for their culture, and not a few were distinguished in literature.

THE YOUNGER PLINY (A.D. 62-114) 

The younger Pliny, nephew and adopted son of Pliny the 

Elder, was born at Novum Comum (Como) beside the loveliest lake of Northern Italy. He became an advocate and statesman, held the consulship under the emperor Trajan, and subsequently governed the province of Bithynia. 

His fame rests upon ten books of letters, the first nine on topics. of general interest and published by himself, the tenth consisting 20

LATIN READINGS 

of official correspondence with Trajan and only given to the world after Pliny's death. One memorable letter tells how his uncle died during the eruption of Vesuvius which destroyed 

Pompeii (A.D. 79); another asks the advice of Trajan concerning the treatment of the Bithynian Christians, - one of the earliest references to Christianity in classical literature. 

The letters give a clear picture of Pliny's own kindly personality, of his studies and his friendships, and of the public and social life of his time. 18.

A poet employs word-groups, and arranges them, as far as possible, according to the principles that govern special emphasis in prose.

Before reading the poetry selection (4), pronounce and translate the prose version (B). Before translating the poetry, read it also aloud, and endeavor to let the elements of the thought come in their order.

(4) A Fable in Verse, by Phaedrus

PHAEDRUS 

(Early in the first century A.D.

) 

Phaedrus was a Macedonian, who, early in life, was brought to Rome as a slave, and was probably set free by Augustus. He was the first of Latin poets to write fables, imitating Aesop in his stories of talking beasts, birds, and trees, each with its "moral." To these he added tales from history. 

In the reign of Tiberius his writings brought him persecution from the government, but during the Middle Ages they were widely read. He often makes us think of " Uncle Remus."

AESOP (about 560 B.C.) 

Under Aesop's name multitudes of stories were told, teaching the lessons of life under the guise of fables about animals. Some of these stories came originally from India. This kind of literature is represented in Latin by Phaedrus.

LATIN READINGS 

103 

Of Aesop we know little but his name.

The tradition is that he was a Phrygian, who, after release from slavery, lived at the court of the wealthy and generous monarch, Croesus, until, sent as an ambassador to Delphi, he was murdered there.

CATULLUS Tenderest of Roman Poets. - Tennyson 

Gaius Valerius Catullus (84-54 B.C.), the greatest lyric poet of Rome, was born at Verona near the Alpine lakes of Northern Italy. He spent most of his short life at Rome, in the midst of friends and foes, and died during the year when Caesar crossed to Britain. This wonderful writer has left us a hundred and sixteen poems (most of them brief), full of clearness, beauty, warmth, and strength. His love poetry has been compared to that of Burns.

"His broad, dome-like head; his calm and penetrating eyes; his aquiline nose; his massive yet finely-moulded jaw, expressed, like no other human countenance, a rich and harmonious nature - - intellect, passion, will, moving in accord. PART TWO INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL GAUL AND THE GAULS 

1. Gaul (Gallia) to the Romans meant the region where the 

Gauls (Galli) lived. This was divided by the Romans into 

Gallia Cisalpina, " Gaul this side of the Alps," and Gallia Trānsalpīna, "Gaul beyond the Alps." Gallia Cisalpina, now northern Italy, occupied the great valley of the river Po (Padus) between the Apennines and the Alps. By Caesar's time it had been conquered, conciliated, Romanized, and it served him as a vast reservoir of man power for his armies. South of the Po 

River the inhabitants had received Roman citizenship; hence this part of the valley was called Gallia Togata, "Gaul where the toga is worn." North of the Po the inhabitants were less thoroughly Romanized; and this part of the valley was called 

Gallia Comāta, "Gaul of the long hair." The general name given to the country beyond the Alps was Gallia Brācāta, " Gaul where trousers are worn." Gallia Trānsalpīna at first included only the Province (Provincia, see sections 3 and 4), but through Caesar's conquests it came to include not only modern France, but also western Switzerland, Germany west of the Rhine, Belgium, and southern Holland. 

2. Through the interior of the country runs the watershed between the south and the north, its most important portion being the Cevennes (Cebenna mons). The southern section has one large river, the Rhone (Rhodanus), which, rising in Switzerland and joined by the Saône (Arar), flows to the Mediterranean. The northern section has five large rivers, the Garonne (Garumna) and the Loire (Liger), flowing to the Bay of Biscay; the Seine (Sequana), which receives the Marne (Matrona) and 109

IIO INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

flows into the English Channel; the Meuse (Mosa), which unites with the Rhine near its mouth; and the Moselle (Mosella), which joins the Rhine far inland. 

3. The country south of the watershed was by Caesar's time Roman and was known as the "Province" (Provincia, cf. modern Provence). The northern portion of the country was divided into three parts: first, that occupied by the Belgae (Belgica, or Belgium, cf. modern 

Belgium), extending from the

Rhine to the Seine and Marne; second, that held by the Celtae (Celtica), stretching from the 

Belgae to the Garonne and the 

Province; and third, Aquitania (cf. modern Aquitaine), occupied by the Aquītānī, and lying between the Province, the Garonne, the Bay of Biscay, and the Pyrenees. 

THE CATHEDRAL OF AMIENS IN THE COUNTRY OF THE AMBIANI 

(6 

4. "The aspect of this region," says T. Rice Holmes,¹ was very different from that of the beautiful France with which we are familiar. The land of gay cities, of picturesque old towns dominated by awful cathedrals, of cornfields and vineyards and sunny hamlets and smiling chateaux, was then covered in many places by dreary swamps and darkened by huge forests." 

5. In this region of Transalpine Gaul, with an area of about 

240,000 square miles, inhabited to-day by fifty or sixty million people, dwelt perhaps six or seven million Gauls. The country thus had about the population of New England with about four times its area. The Gauls were divided into fifty or more tribes. Dissension and inter-tribal war made the whole country ripe for conquest. "Every tribe, every hamlet, nay 

1 Caesar's Conquest of Gaul, pp. 4, 5.

GAUL AND THE GAULS III 

every household," says T. Rice Holmes, "was riven by faction . . . all, in short, were for a party; and none was for the state." 

6. In some cases the cities of modern France are called by names derived from their ancient Gallic names: Vienne from 

Vienna; Alise-Sainte-Reine from Alesia. Sometimes the name of a Gallic tribe has passed into that of the modern city; thus, Paris was originally Lutetia Parisiōrum, "Lutetia of the Parisii." 

So Soissons (Noviodunum) is named from the Suessiōnēs, Amiens (Samarobriva) from the Ambiānī, Rheims (Durocortorum) from the Rēmi, Bourges (Avaricum) from the Biturigēs. 

7. The Gauls did not speak Latin, and Caesar could converse with them only through interpreters. Their language was related to the Latin, but, as a Celtic" language, more closely to the Gaelic of Scotland and Ireland, and still more closely to Welsh. Not much is known of the Gallic language, but we are informed that rix means king (cf. Latin rēx); thus the Caturīges are "battle kings." Brīva means ford; Samarobriva is the ford on the Somme. Dunum is a hill fortress; Noviodūnum is Newcastle. 

8. The Gauls in general were far above the condition of barbarians, while the Celtae had even attained a certain degree of luxury. "Their many-colored tartan shirts and cloaks excited the astonishment of their conquerors. The chiefs wore rings and bracelets and necklaces of gold; and when those tall, fairhaired warriors rode forth to battle with their helmets wrought in the shape of some fierce beast's head and surmounted by nodding plumes, their chain armor, their long bucklers and their clanking swords, they made a splendid show." The Gauls had walled towns, large and well-built houses, painted pottery, fields yellow with grain, roads suitable for wheeled traffic. Rude bridges spanned rivers, along which floated barges laden with merchandise. Ships sailed to Britain. Under the influence of the Greek trading city of Massilia, the arts of coinage and of writing were familiar.

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

9. The common people were serfs. The influential classes were the Knights (equites) and the Druids (Druidēs). The former were the military class, and the great nobles among them could muster thousands of retainers. The Druids were the priests, educators, and judges. They were exempt from taxes and military service. They constituted a religious hierarchy under one Chief Druid. They wielded the terrible power of excommunication. They taught that the soul is immortal. Of their mysterious ritual, which was thought to be best learned in Britain, human sacrifice was an important part. 

10. "The Gauls were an interesting people," says T. Rice

Holmes, "enthusiastic, impulsive, quick-witted, versatile, vainglorious and ostentatious, childishly inquisitive and childishly credulous, rash, sanguine and inconstant, arrogant in victory and despondent in defeat, submissive as women to their priests, impatient of law and discipline, yet capable of loyalty to a strong and sympathetic ruler." 

11. To Gaul the conquest by Caesar was a blessing in disguise. What really occurred was that a brilliant and naturally progressive people entered the school of a superior civilization.

ROME AND THE GAULS BEFORE CAESAR 

12. Three centuries before the birth of Caesar, the Gauls first encountered their predestined conquerors. For more than a hundred years these wanderers had been pouring in resistless stream down the Alpine passes into Italy. At length they overthrew a Roman army in the battle of the Allia (390 B.C.) and marched unopposed through the gates of Rome. From time to time during the next century they came to plunder; but their incursions were repelled, and the champion of Italian civilization was Rome.

ROME AND THE GAULS BEFORE CAESAR 

113 13. At length the tide fully turned. By 223 B.C. Rome had fought her way to the Po and established a chain of forts along that river; but before the country was completely conquered, Hannibal had descended from the Alps and the Gauls rallied around him. Even when he had been defeated, they still fought on, till at last, after terrible disasters, they yielded (191 B.C.) and the survivors were lost among Italian settlers. 

14. Rome now looked westward beyond the Alps. There on the southern coast of Transalpine Gaul was the Greek city of Massilia. As champion of Massilia (155 B.C.) Rome crushed the neighboring tribes. She had come to stay. The 

Haedui, a powerful state of central Gaul, were recognized as "Friends and Allies of the Roman people." The Allobroges and Arverni, however, chose to resist 

Rome. Just twenty years before the birth of Caesar they were beaten in a great battle upon the bank of the Rhone, whose bridges broke down beneath the multitude of the fugitives. The victors now proceeded to organize southern Gaul into the "Province." Thither came Roman merchants, and there, when Caesar came upon the stage, the language and civilization of Rome had taken deep root. 

A ROMAN MAUSOLEUM AT ST. REMY, FRANCE GAUL (FRANCE) AFTER CAESAR'S CONQUEST 

15. Gaul became one of the most prosperous portions of the 

Roman Empire, and "the eldest daughter of the Church." 

When the dying Empire could no longer hold the Rhine frontier, Gaul suffered successive invasions, at last falling into the possession of the Franks (whence the name France). They in their turn were Christianized, and their great king Charlemagne

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

(A.D. 800) revived the Western Roman Empire and befriended learning and the Church. However, we cannot here dwell upon the long, tragic, and glorious history of France, though it was for many centuries so closely interwoven with that of English-speaking peoples. In the settlement of America France took a great part. The region along the St. Lawrence was once known as 

New France and much of it is still French-speaking. Lake 

Champlain was named from a French explorer, St. Louis from one French monarch, and Louisiana from another. 

16. France retains the impress of Roman civilization. Paris is to-day sometimes called " the first of Latin cities," and

French is really a modern form of Latin. 

BRITAIN AND THE BRITONS 

a world away 17. Britain seemed to the Romans from

Italy, and exploration was one of the motives for Caesar's two expeditions thither (in 55 and 54 B.C.). With these the history of England really begins, though traders had long visited the 

STONEHENGE ON SALISBURY PLAIN 

(This sanctuary of gigantic stones, built before Caesar came to Britain, is thought to have been associated with sun worship.) country for the product of its tin mines. Caesar landed on the southeast coast not far from Dover and penetrated into the interior beyond the Thames (Tamesis). He found that

BRITAIN AND THE BRITONS 

115 the low hills of the east and south offered few difficulties to his invading force, and that the real natural defenses of Britain were the rough waters around it, which twice occasioned serious disasters to his fleet. 

18. Upon the country and the people he made careful observations, noting, for example, that the climate was milder and the summer night shorter than in Gaul. The Britons themselves he describes as gallant warriors and skillful in the use of chariots. Plainly he thought them more dangerous than the

Gauls; and it is clear that he took decided risks in entering their country. 

19. Civilization was farthest advanced in Cantium (Kent), where the manner of life was like that of the Gauls across the Channel. With the Gauls the 

Britons remained connected by the closest ties, and Belgic kings ruled on both sides of the narrow seas. In the interior and in the north, civilization continued primitive and had hardly entered the agricultural stage. 

WALL AND TOWER AT CHESTER, ENGLAND 

20. A fierce resistance to the advance of Caesar was led by 

Cassivellaunus, who ruled north of the Thames. But here, just as in Gaul, the rivalries of the Celtic tribes paralyzed resistance to the common enemy, and Cassivellaunus was brought to submission after the powerful Trinovantes and other tribes had gone over to the invader. 

21. Caesar's countrymen may well have felt that he had "given them a new world." It was not, however, until a hundred years later, in the reign of the Emperor Claudius, that southern Britain became a Roman province (A.D. 44). Nor

after Rome had laid her mighty grasp upon the tribes of the south did she hasten. It was not until A.D. 85 that, as the result of victories in southern Scotland by Agricola, northern

Britain became a Roman province. 

22. Eventually a wall was built from the Clyde to the Forth, shutting off the wild Highland tribes of Caledonia (northern 

THE ROMAN BATH IN BATH, ENGLAND 

Scotland), and all south of this rampart was firmly under 

Roman control. Eboracum (York) and Londinium (London) 

became the principal centers. Like the railways of modern England, the chief Roman military roads, built to give direct communication with outlying garrisons, radiated from London, which was itself connected, by a highway constructed on the line of an old British trail, with the ports on the coast of Kent, and so with the continent and Rome. 

23. An evidence of Roman occupation of Britain is found in the names of towns that end in chester and cester (that is, castra, "camp"), as Winchester, Colchester, Gloucester; and in such a name as Lincoln, where the second syllable is for colonia, "colony," that is, the colony Lindum.

BRITAIN AND THE BRITONS 

119 

24. Britain, especially in the south and east, took the Roman language, civilization, and spirit, though never on the same scale of splendor as did Gaul. The towns were small and somewhat few, and the country houses indicated comfort rather than wealth. "We have before us a civilization," says Haverfield,¹ which, like a man whose constitution is sound rather than strong, might perish quickly from a violent shock." Such a shock came, when, after holding Britain for 350 years, the weakening Roman Empire withdrew the legions for its own defense. Like beasts of prey the hordes of Angles and Saxons from overseas, of Picts and Scots from Caledonia and Ireland, fell upon the country. Not a town was left undestroyed. 

The Angles and Saxons took possession, except in the west, and their descendants (the Anglo-Saxons), who became Christianized, ruled the land, which came to be known as England, that is, Angle-land, until A.D. 1066. 

25. Then the Anglo-Saxons were conquered by the Normans from across the Channel. Thus the Roman culture came again to Britain; for the Normans brought with them a language (the Norman-French), which was really Latin in a modified form, and a civilization which was in no small degree an inheritance from Roman days. These influences were reënforced throughout the Middle Ages by the universities, which inculcated the classic culture, and by the Church, which used Latin as its official language and reflected the Roman Empire in its organization.

So to this day the English language is full of Latin elements, and at its core the civilization of English-speaking peoples is still largely Roman.

"The Last of the Legions"; Kipling and Fletcher, A History of England, Chap. 1, especially the poem, "The Roman Centurion Speaks"; Kipling, Puck of Pook's Hill, "A Centurion of the Thirtieth," "On the Great Wall," "The Winged Hats"; Tacitus, Annals, xiv, 29–30 (Ramsay's translation).

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL GERMANY AND THE GERMANS 

26. Ancient Germany included not only most of modern 

Germany, but also northern Holland, Denmark, and parts of Norway and Sweden. In addition, German tribes ranged well down the Danube. 

27. Though traders had long brought amber from the south shores of the Baltic, Germany seemed to the Romans of Caesar's 

THE RETURN OF THE GERMANS 

time much as America with its Indian tribes seemed to the first white settlers a vast, wild, mysterious land, covered with forests and full of barbarous and warlike peoples. 

28. The Germans are described by the Roman historian 

Tacitus as having fierce, blue, glaring eyes, reddish-yellow hair, and huge bodies. They had herds and flocks, but paid little attention to agriculture. The glory of a tribe was to have around it the broadest possible belt of devastated country. "When not at war," says Tacitus (Germānia, 15), "they spend their time to some extent in hunting, but mostly in idleness, sleeping, and eating; their bravest and most warlike doing nothing at all, giving over the care of house and home and estate to the women and old men

GERMANY AND THE GERMANS 

I2I and incapacitated members of the household; they themselves are dull and inactive, from a strange contradictoriness of nature, since the same men so love inactivity and so hate quiet. . . ." "It is well known," adds Tacitus (Germania, 16), "that the German peoples do not dwell in cities, nor even permit their habitations to be adjacent. 

They dwell separate and apart, according as a spring, a plain, a woodland has pleased their fancy." 

29. Caesar twice bridged the Rhine (in 55 B.C. and in 53 B.C.), but did not advance far into Germany, for his military resources were inadequate to its conquest. However, he drove the Germans out of Gaul. Among those tribes which he encountered were the Suēbī, a vast confederacy of peoples, whose position among the Germans might be compared to that of the Six Nations, or Iroquois, among the North American Indians. "Examining carefully Caesar's account of this people, we see that he looked upon the Suebi as at the bottom of all disturbances along his new frontier of the Rhine; it was they who set other tribes in motion, by refusing to have them as neighbors, turning them out of their homes, bag and baggage, and destroying their crops wherever the unfortunates settled." 1 

30. It was really a question whether the civilized Romans or the barbarous Germans should possess Gaul, for the Gauls were too disunited to maintain their independence. There was imminent danger that Gaul might become an outpost of barbarism. Caesar made it a bulwark of the Roman Empire for centuries; but "the Roman Empire," as Haverfield says, was the civilized world; the safety of Rome was the safety of all civilization." 2 (6 

THE LIFE OF GAIUS JULIUS CAESAR 

"Caesar was the one man of his time really gifted with scientific intelligence — with the power of seeing the facts before him and adjusting his action to them. This intelligence, combined with great strength of will, made him master of the Roman Empire; and though his char- 

1 W. Warde Fowler, Julius Caesar, p. 188. 

2 The Romanization of Roman Britain, p. 10.

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

acter was by no means perfect, he seems to have used his mastership not like a capricious oriental despot, but with a real sense of responsibility." 1 "Caesar above all men was gifted with the faculty of seizing the right moment." 2 

31. Summary of Caesar's Achievements. Among Caesar's main achievements are the following: He completed the work of frontier defense of the Roman Empire, which Marius began by destroying the wandering German hordes of the Teutoni and Cimbri. He began the recorded history of England and in a real sense founded France. For the Mediterranean world he established an efficient administration, with tranquillity for Italy and more liberal treatment for the subject peoples. He was the first and most illustrious of the Roman emperors, called after him The Caesars." 

CAESAR 

(On a coin of Augustus) 

This many-sided man was also among the most elegant of 

Roman writers, and, as an orator, second only to Cicero.

Echoes of his greatness are the facts that the month of July perpetuates his name of Julius, and the former title Kaiser his name Caesar. 

32. His Appearance and Qualities. Caesar was tall, with a fair complexion, black and piercing eyes, firmly-set lips, an aquiline nose, and a high forehead. The expression of his face was keen, thoughtful, often somewhat stern. tastes.

He was athletic; but he also had marked literary and artistic 

His manner combined dignity with courtesy. Judged by the standards of the time, Caesar showed clemency even to foreign enemies and was remarkably merciful to his conquered
