This and the following translations of Plutarch, the Greek biographer and moralist, are from Plutarch's Lives of Illustrious Men, translated by John 

Dryden and Others, John W. Lovell Company, New York.

fellow countrymen. At the battle of Pharsalus he cried out to his men, "Spare your fellow citizens." "I will be no Sulla," he said at another time. 

33. Birth and Early Education. 

Caesar was born in 102 B.C.

He was fortunate in a good mother, Aurelia, and a good teacher, Gnipho, the Gaul. 

34. Political Situation at Rome. His life was largely influenced by the political situation at Rome, where there had long been two great parties, the aristocratic, represented in legislation by the Senate, and the democratic, represented by the 

Assembly of the People. During Caesar's youth the relations of these factions had been a terrible story of massacre and countermassacre. The democratic leaders were 

Cinna, Marius, and later, Sertorius and

Cacsar. The champion of the nobility was the able general who had defeated King Mithridates of Pontus, Lucius Cornelius Sulla, the Fortunate, or the Dictator. Sulla crushed opposition, slaughtered his opponents, and so arranged the Constitution as to make the people helpless. 

A COIN OF MITHRI- DATES 

Caesar was himself an aristocrat, for the Julian house traced its proud lineage to Iulus (whence the name Julius), son of

Aeneas, the Trojan. Caesar, nevertheless, was to insure the final victory of the people over the Senate, fulfilling the prophetic words of Sulla that "in Caesar was many a Marius." 

35. Early Adventures Abroad. In fear of Sulla, Caesar had left Rome for Asia Minor, where he served in the new war against 

Mithridates. Here he saved the life of a fellow citizen in battle and so won the "Civic Crown," which was like our modern "Distinguished Service Cross." Later he was captured by pirates, whom, after he had been ransomed, he pursued and punished. 

As one of 

36. His "Hat in the Ring" of Roman Politics. the tribunes of the people, at Rome he assisted in the overthrow

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

(70 B.C.) of the constitution of Sulla. In 65 B.C. as curule aedile he gave magnificent theatrical shows. At another time, he feasted the people at 22,000 tables. This lavishness, along with eloquence and tact, made it certain that the people would give him overwhelming support in future elections.

In 63 B.C. he became Pontifex Maximus, the head of the Roman state religion. 

After holding the praetorship, a judicial office, for the year 62 B.C., he served with military success in Further Spain. 

He now 

37. The First Triumvirate. 

formed, in union with Pompey, the distinguished general, and Crassus, the richest man in Rome, the First Triumvirate, a pooling of political interests, in order to secure the ambitions of these three men as against the Senate. The alliance was strengthened by the marriage of Pompey to Caesar's daughter Julia.

POMPEY (Gnaeus Pompeius Magnus) 

Caesar brought to the combination popularity and ability, Pompey contributed prestige, and Crassus " the sinews of war." 

38. His First Consulship. - Caesar was consul in 59 B.C., and, faithful to the coalition, put through a land bill securing for Pompey's soldiers the "bonuses" which Pompey had been unable to obtain. His prestige now began to supplant that of Pompey. 

39. His Conquest of Gaul. The Triumvirate next obtained for him an appointment as governor of Cisalpine Gaul, Illyricum, and the Province, with command of four legions. Now the way was clear for one of the most successful military careers in history." After this," says Plutarch, "he seems to begin his course afresh, and to enter upon a new life and scene of action. The period of those wars which he now fought, and those many expeditions in which he subdued Gaul, showed him to be a soldier and general not in the least inferior to any of the

greatest and most admired commanders who had ever appeared at the head of armies." The necessity of defending the Province against attacks from without drew him into campaigns extending from 58 to 50 B.C., wherein he subjugated central and northern Gaul. Also he twice crossed the Rhine into Germany, and twice the Channel into Britain. 

40. Conference at Lucca. 

In April of 56 B.C. a continuance of the Triumvirate was arranged. Caesar's governorship of Gaul 

THE THEATER AND PORTICOES OF POMPEY 

In a room opening upon these porticoes Caesar was assassinated, March 15, 44 B.C. -"Beware the Ides of March" (Shakespeare, Julius Caesar) 

was to be extended for five years, and Pompey and Crassus, after holding the consulship for 55 B.C., were each to receive a governorship for five years - Crassus in Syria, Pompey in Spain. 

41. Death of Crassus; Estrangement of Caesar and Pompey. 

At the end of 55 B.C. Crassus went away to Syria and was killed next year at Carrhae in battle with the Parthians. The

Triumvirate was now at an end, and Caesar and Pompey were rivals. Also Julia died. In 52 B.C. Pompey found himself

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

champion of the Senatorial party, which desired to bring the power of Caesar to an end. Through the next two years Pompey and the enemies of Caesar in the Senate were intriguing to prevent him from receiving a second consulship for 48 B.C., a vital matter to Caesar, for as a mere private citizen his enemies would have quickly destroyed him.

Pompey failed to provide against the contingency of an armed struggle, although he had boasted that whenever he stamped with his foot, troops would arise in an instant. 

42. The Civil War between Pompey and Caesar. On Janu ary 1, 49 B.C., the Senate demanded that Caesar give up his provinces and dismiss his army. Caesar then crossed the 

Rubicon, a little stream between his provinces and Italy, and with his army advanced on Rome. 66 Since the law forbade a provincial governor to bring armed forces into Italy, to cross the Rubicon " was to declare war upon the government. Caesar's momentous decision on the northern bank became the subject of legend. The Roman biographer Suetonius writes: ¹ ... "Overtaking his cohorts at the river Rubicon . . . he paused for a little time, and, reflecting on the vastness of the undertaking, turned to those near him and said: "Even yet we can go back, but if we once shall cross that little bridge, we must depend on our good swords alone.' "As he stood in doubt, this sign was given him. Suddenly there appeared hard by a being of wondrous stature and beauty, who sat and played upon a reed; and when not only the shepherds flocked to hear him, but many of the soldiers left their posts, and among them some of the trumpeters, the apparition snatched a trumpet from one of them, rushed to the river, and, sounding the war-note with a mighty blast, strode to the opposite bank. Then Caesar cried: 'Take we the course which the signs of the gods and the false dealings of our foes point out. The die is cast."" 

1 Dē Vita Caesarum, I, 31, 32. Most of the quotations from Suetonius are from Rolfe's translation.

Pompey was made commander-in-chief of the Senatorial army. He had about twice as many soldiers as Caesar, but they were scattered in Spain, Italy, and the East, and were in all stages of training, while Caesar's men were veterans. 

By October of 49 B.C. Caesar had won Italy, Sicily, Sardinia, and Spain. In August of 48 B.C. Pompey was completely defeated at Pharsalus in Thessaly. He fled to Egypt where, as he landed, he was murdered by order of the chief adviser of the boy king. This was the melancholy end of one who had captured 800 ships in one campaign, had taken 900 cities, and had triumphed thrice, first over Africa, then over Europe, and lastly over Asia, so that he seemed in these three triumphs to have led the whole world captive." ¹

Caesar followed Pompey to Egypt with only a small force, and presently found himself besieged in Alexandria by the Egyptians. After his rescue by reënforcements, he conquered 

Egypt and placed the famous Cleopatra on the throne. Marching into Asia Minor, he won a victory over the king of Pontus. 

September of 47 B.C. found him setting affairs in order at Rome.

In the following December, Caesar crossed to Africa where the illustrious Cato, Scipio, and other adherents of Pompey had gathered considerable forces, and had the support of Juba, King of Numidia. In April, 46 B.C., Caesar annihilated the enemy at 

Thapsus. Cato took his own life rather than survive the Republic or accept the mercy of the conqueror. 

Caesar returned to Rome and celebrated four triumphs. "The first and most splendid," says Suetonius, "was the

Gallic triumph, the next the Alexandrian, then the Pontic, after that the African each differing from the rest in its equipment and display of spoils. . . . He mounted the Capitol by torchlight, with forty elephants bearing lamps on his right and his left. In his Pontic triumph he displayed . . . an inscription of but three words, Veni, vīdī, vīcī."

The survivors of the Pompeians had gathered yet another army in Spain, led by the sons of Pompey, and by Labienus, Caesar's second in command in the Gallic wars, but now among the bitterest of his enemies. Again Caesar sought out the enemy and defeated them with terrible slaughter in the battle of Munda

CAESAR 

on March 17, 45 B.C.; Labienus and one of Pompey's sons were among the slain. So desperate was the struggle that the day was saved only by Caesar's personal entreaties to his soldiers as they fought, and he told his friends that he had often fought for victory, but that this was the first time he had ever fought for life. 

Caesar made a mild use of his victory, restoring the statues of

Pompey, and even giving honors and offices to some who, like (From a bust in the Palazzo dei Conserva- Brutus and Cassius, had fought (C tori, Rome) against him. 

His 

43. Caesar Becomes Dictator His Program. He was now made Dictator for Life," and was virtually supreme. policy not only contemplated the concentration of power in his own hands, but also looked toward the welfare of the provinces as well as Rome and Italy. Soldiers and the poor were settled on farms; free labor was encouraged as against slave labor; a juster system of taxation was enacted; citizenship was given to the Gauls between the Po and the Alps. He began the erection of splendid public buildings, and projected such works as a public library and an improved harbor for Rome, a canal through the isthmus of Corinth, and the rebuilding of Corinth and Carthage. His reformation of the calendar, with some slight modifications, is in use to-day.

44. The Murder of Caesar. - As Caesar was about to set forth on an expedition against the Parthians, he was stabbed to death at a meeting of the Senate on the " Ides of March" (March 15, 44 B.C.) by a band of about sixty conspirators, among them several of his intimate friends. The story of the plot, the assassination, and the retribution upon the chief conspirators, Brutus and Cassius, has been made familiar to the world in Shakespeare's Julius Caesar.

The event that struck down "the foremost man of all this world" could not fail to become the subject of story and legend. 

ROMAN ARCH AT ORANGE, IN SOUTHERN FRANCE 

The death of Caesar was said to have been attended by flood and by volcanic eruption, by yawning of the earth and trembling of the mountains, by comets and darkened sun, and by the sound of arms in heaven. His wife dreamed that she was weeping over Caesar and holding him murdered in her arms.

According to Plutarch, "A soothsayer bade him prepare for some great danger on the Ides of March. When this day was come, Caesar, as he went to the Senate, met this soothsayer,

and said to him by way of raillery, The Ides of March are come.' 'Yes, they are come,' was the calm answer,' but they are not gone.' 222 

A ROMAN TRIUMPHAL PROCESSION (Approaching the Temple of Jupiter) 

"Caesar died in his fifty-sixth year," says Plutarch, "not having survived Pompey above four years. That empire and power which he had pursued through the whole course of his life with so much hazard, he did at last with much difficulty compass, but reaped no other fruit from it than the empty name and invidious glory. But the great genius which attended him through his lifetime, even after his death remained as the avenger of his murder, pursuing through every sea and land all those who were concerned in it, and suffering none to escape. . . . Cassius killed himself with the same dagger which he had made use of against Caesar, Brutus, . . . laying himself down one night, . thought he heard a noise at the door of his tent, and looking that way, by the light of his lamp, which was almost out, saw a terrible figure, like that of a man, but of unusual stature and severe countenance. . . . He asked it what it was. The specter answered him, ' Thy evil genius, Brutus, thou shalt see me at 

Philippi.' . . . The night before the second battle (of Philippi), the same phantom appeared to him again, but spoke not a word. . . .

Brutus did not die in the fight, but seeing his men defeated, presenting his sword to his naked breast, met his death."

Caesar in the Field. "He was highly skilled in arms and horsemanship, and of incredible powers of endurance. On the march he headed his army, sometimes on horseback, but oftener on foot, bare-headed both in the heat of the sun and in rain. "He covered great distances with incredible speed, . . . swimming the rivers which barred his path or crossing them on inflated skins, and very often arriving before his messengers." ... 

Loyalty of his Soldiers. "Throughout the long struggle not one of his soldiers deserted, and many of them, on being taken prisoner, refused to accept their lives, when offered them on condition of consenting to serve against Caesar. 

They bore hunger with such fortitude that when Pompey saw in the works at Dyrrachium a kind of bread made of herbs on which they were living, he said that he was fighting wild beasts. Indeed one cohort of the sixth legion, when set to defend a redoubt, kept four legions of Pompey at bay for several hours, though almost all were wounded by the enemy's showers of arrows, of which 130,000 were picked up (British Museum, London) within the ramparts. THE ROMAN ARMY IN CAESAR'S TIME 

46. The Infantry. 

ORGANIZATION 

The infantry of the Roman army was organized into legions (legiōnēs) and auxiliaries (auxilia). 

47. The Legions. The legions were the mainstay of the army; they met the enemy in hand-to-hand encounter and bore the brunt of the battle. At first they were a citizen militia who served for the emergency only and without pay. But after

Marius a standing army was established, the citizen becoming a professional soldier and taking an oath to serve for twenty years. Men of wealth and social position entered the army only as officers; the ranks. were filled by the poorer classes. 

The legions were designated by numbers, in the order of their formation. Caesar employed at various times in Gaul from six to eleven legions, the First, and the Sixth to the Fifteenth inclusive. The famous Tenth Legion was his first command. 

Jameson

THE BEARER OF THE EAGLE 

These legions are mentioned only by number. Later, under the Empire, some legions had special names, as Victrix, the Victorious." In the same way special names are taken by bodies of troops to-day, as, the "Black Watch," the "Yankee 

Division," the "Legion of Death," the "Foreign Legion," the "Rough Riders."

The legion was divided into cohorts (cohortes), maniples (manipuli), and centuries (centuriae) as follows:

I legion (6000) 48. The Auxiliary Infantry. The auxiliary infantry was recruited from the Roman provinces and from allied and independent nations. The very name "helps" indicates that it was not relied upon for the brunt of the battle. Often these men were posted upon the wings.

The auxiliaries included slingers (funditōrēs), chiefly from the Balearic islands, bowmen (sagittarii), chiefly from Crete and

CALLARIM CAESAR IMPLERATOR] 

Sling Missile (glāns) of Lead (In the Museum at Vienna) 

Numidia, and other lightarmed foot soldiers who may be called lancers on foot. 

Their light javelin (hasta) was longer than the pike (pilum) of the legionaries. 

Auxiliaries had their own organization, used their native weapons, and were commanded by native or 

Roman officers. The bowmen had no protective armor. The slingers and light-armed soldiers carried a small round shield (parma). 

49. The Cavalry (equites, or equitatus). Caesar's cavalry was non-Roman and formed part of the auxiliaries. At first it came from Gaul, later contingents were brought from Spain and Germany. The largest tactical unit, the squadron (āla), was divided into troops (turmae) of about thirty men each, the troops into decuries (decuriae) of about ten men each. The squadron was commanded by a prefect (praefectus), the troops by a decurion (decurio). The whole body of cavalry was in the charge of a Roman officer.

50. Additional Elements. A Roman army in the field also included: 

1. Reënlisted veterans (ēvocati), who received extra pay, were exempt from routine camp duties, and rode horses on the march. In emergencies they were called out by the general, and formed a select troop, or were distributed in the legions to build up the morale of the troops. 

2. Spies (speculātōrēs), often natives knowing the country and the language, and scouts (explōrātōrēs), usually mounted. 

3. Standard bearers of the legions (aquiliferi), the color sergeants of a modern regiment - standard bearers of the cohorts (signiferi), and trumpeters (tubicinēs). or 

4. Tent companions (contubernālēs, contaberna, tent), "bunkies" "buddies," an "officers' training camp of young men who through influence had secured the privilege of accom- 

A STANDARD BEARER 

panying the general. They thus acquired the science of war without the hardships of the enlisted soldier. 

5. Engineers (fabri) and artisans under a chief engineer (praefectus fabrum). These had charge of all construction work - the building of bridges, siege works, camp fortifications, etc. 

6. Noncombatants. Army slaves (calōnēs) performed menial camp duties and acted as body servants of the officers. To prevent confusion in case of an attack they were under military discipline. The pack animals (iumenta) were driven by muleteers (mūliōnēs). Sutlers (mercātōrēs) also went along, who sold to the men extra food and clothing, and in turn purchased booty from them. Sutlers were not a part of the army and pitched their tents within the camp only in times of danger.

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL THE EQUIPMENT OF THE LEGIONARY SOLDIER 

51. Dress. The dress of the legionary included a shortsleeved woolen tunic (tunica) or shirt, extending nearly to the knees and bound around the waist by a girdle (cingulum); a thick woolen cloak (sagum) for inclement weather (but never worn in action), secured on the right shoulder by a clasp so that the arms were left free; heavy hobnailed sandals (caligae) with numerous leather straps, which could be easily loosened if the feet became swollen. Experience has shown that proper footwear is necessary to the efficiency of an army. 

The commander-in-chief wore a scarlet cloak (palūdāmentum), and the subordinate officers, scarlet tunics. This color, a mark of distinction in civil life, made the officers conspicuous to the soldiers. 

52. Armor (arma). A legionary soldier wore a helmet (galea, cassis) and a coat of mail (lōrica), and carried a shield (scutum) on his left arm. Helmets were of bronze, or of leather reënforced with bronze, and were adorned with a crest. The helmet protected the head and the back of the neck, and was held in place by the chinpiece; the face was not covered. The coat of mail was, as a rule, of leather strengthened by bands of metal across the shoulders and around the breast and back. 

Sometimes it was covered with overlapping metal scales (squamae) or made entirely of chain mail (lōrīca hāmāta). The shield was rectangular, with a slightly cylindrical curvature, and was about forty-eight inches long and thirty inches wide, completely protecting the vital parts of the body; it weighed from sixteen to twenty pounds. It was made of wood, covered with leather, and bound around the edges with a metal rim. A metal boss (umbo) attached to the center of the outside strengthened and ornamented it. The boss served to deflect blows, and on occasion might be driven into the face of the foe. Shields were often ornamented with various designs, among the most common of which was the winged thunderbolt. When not

in use the shield was carried in a leather case (tegimentum). A legion usually had shields with distinctive colors and symbols. 

53. Weapons. - The 

Roman legionary used the light spear (hasta), the javelin (pilum), and the sword (gladius). The javelin had a round or square wooden shaft, about four feet long, to which was attached a head of soft iron some two and one half feet long. 

The tip of the metal was hardened. After piercing the shield of an enemy, the iron bent, thus making the javelin difficult to extract and useless for hurling back at the foe. The javelin could be thrown about eighty feet; at close range it could be used as a bayonet. too much burdened to carry more than one. An extra supply was carried in the baggage train. The heavier "wall javelin was used by the defenders of fortified places. It was sent crashing down upon the assailants. The sword was about two feet long, straight, pointed, and two-edged. It was used more for thrusting than for cutting. It was suspended on the right side by a strap (balteus) passing over the left shoulder, and was kept in a leather-covered scabbard (vāgīna). 

ROMAN LEGIONARY SOLDIER (Model in the Musée Saint Germain, Paris) 

The soldier on the march was

55. Individual Packs. 

BAGGAGE 

While on the march each soldier carried in addition to his armor and weapons whatever personal effects were needful for his comfort, such as extra clothing, food, and cooking utensils. He also carried his part of the equipment for constructing the camp fortifications a saw, hatchet, spade, basket, and one or more stakes for the rampart. All these were made into a compact bundle (sarcina), weighing about fifty pounds, which was fastened to a forked stick and carried over his shoulder. 

With his pack the soldier was said to be encumbered (impedītus), without it, unencumbered (expeditus). A Roman soldier was prepared for activity when encumbered by a rigorous. drill that included running, jumping, wrestling, swimming, and long forced marches with heavy loads. Before battle the packs were stacked in a heap and a strong guard was left to protect them. The pack of the modern soldier, weighing about sixty-five pounds, is carried in part rolled in his blanket and strapped to his shoulders; his other effects are contained in his "duffel bag." 

56. Heavy Baggage. The heavy baggage (impedimenta) consisted of extra supplies of arms, food, and clothing, tents, siege artillery, hand mills for grinding grain, etc. Most of this was carried either on the backs of beasts of burden (iumenta) or in carts (carri). Each legion had its baggage train. A legion was said to be encumbered (impedita) when followed immediately by its baggage train, unencumbered (expedita) when not. The baggage of modern armies is transported largely in packs on mules, the heavier baggage being placed in carts or motor trucks. The 

American Expeditionary Forces had with them overseas, December 25, 1918, a total of 191,631 animals.

THE SOLDIER'S PAY AND FOOD 

57. Pay (stipendium). - Caesar raised the pay of his legionaries from 125 to 225 denarii a year. A dēnārius was nominally equal to about twenty cents, but had a purchasing power many times as great. The soldier's pay was about equivalent to the wage of a day laborer at Rome. It was supplemented by gifts distributed by the commander after a victory. A centurion had twice the pay of a common soldier; higher officers were paid according to the appropriation made by the Senate. Soldiers could deposit their A ROMAN COIN pay with the quaestor (paymaster), who kept it with the standards in the headquarters. At his discharge the soldier received a bonus in money or a plot of land or both. Dēnārius 

58. Food. Caesar's soldiers subsisted almost entirely on a diet of bread and porridge, both made of wheat (rarely of barley), which had to be ground in hand mills. The daily allowance was about two pounds, and rations were issued every fifteen or sixteen days. Caesar's commissary department was in the charge of young officers from civil life (lēgātī, tribūnī). 

DISCIPLINE 

59. Work. At the end of the day's march each soldier did his part in fortifying the camp, erecting the tents, preparing the meals, mounting guard, etc. In time of peace he might be employed in the construction of canals, bridges, buildings, and roads. 

60. Punishments. - The Roman military discipline in time of war was strict. Recognized punishments were: shortening of rations, flogging by the centurion, imprisonment in the "guardhouse," loss of pay and booty, degradation in rank,

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

dismissal from the service, or death after court-martial. In military life the citizen had no appeal from the death penalty. 

61. Rewards. Special acts of merit were rewarded by gifts of money, promotion, or decorations of honor, such as medals, wreaths, necklaces, armlets. Whole cohorts often received these badges of honor.

STANDARDS AND MUSICAL INSTRUMENTS 

62. Standards. Each legion, cavalry division, and cohort had its standard. That of the legion was a silver eagle (aquila) 

SPOR STANDARD BEARERS 

surmounting a staff, and was borne by the eagle bearer (aquilifer), under the personal supervision of the chief centurion (primipilus) of the legion. 

Cohort standards (signa) were of various designs, often displaying the figure of an animal or an open hand, sometimes inclosed in an oak wreath. Below was indicated the number of the cohort, and metal decorations for bravery in battle were attached. So in modern times decorations for bravery are awarded to bodies of troops and displayed with the colors. The standard of a cohort was probably intrusted to its first maniple. 

The standard bearer (signifer) was made conspicuous by a wolf or bear skin which he wore over his head and shoulders. It was

necessary that each standard be easily recognizable in the confusion of battle.

The cavalry and infantry auxiliaries had a special standard (vexillum), usually a crimson flag on a horizontal bar upheld by a staff. A red flag was displayed above the general's tent before the call to battle. 

The standard bearer received the orders for his division. To lose a standard in battle was as disgraceful as to lose the flag in modern war. 

Instruments.

The musical instruments were horns and trumpets to sound the various signals. The cornů, a curved horn with deep, loud Jansen sound, called the troops to form

ROMAN TRUMPETERS 

for battle. It was the thunder preceding the storm. The tuba, a straight trumpet with a sharp, shrill sound (like the fife), gave the signals in battle. 

The bucina (bugle) was sounded for reveille, the noon rest, and formations preliminary to battle. The lituus, a modified form of the tuba, was the signal instrument of the cavalry. 

OFFICERS 

64. The regular officers of the Roman army were the centurions (centuriōnēs), military tribunes (tribūni militum), lieutenant commanders (lēgātī), quaestors (quaestōrēs), prefects (praefecti), and, finally, the commander-in-chief (dux, imperātor). 

65. Centurions. The centurions were the noncommissioned officers, corresponding in a general way to our corporals and sergeants. The higher military officers were Romans of social distinction. The centurions were common soldiers promoted

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

Expert from the ranks, and of conspicuous ability and courage. swordsmen, they were the drill sergeants of the cohorts. There were two for a maniple, six for a cohort, and sixty for a legion. 

CENTURIO LICTOR LEGĀTUS Dux (Imperator) 

The "centurion of the first rank " (the senior centurion of the first maniple of the first cohort), stationed at the right of the line, had under his especial care the legionary standard, and was thus the actual leader of his legion in battle. Centurions of the first rank were admitted to the general's council in emergencies. 

66. Tribunes. To each legion were assigned six military tribunes. Caesar assigned his tribunes to small detachments sent out on special service, and employed them as judicial and administrative officers. They also formed a part of his councils of war. 

67. Lieutenant Commanders. 

Lēgāti belonged to the senatorial class. Caesar sometimes assigned the command of the legions to the lēgātī, who thus became the highest strategic officers under the commander-in-chief.

A lēgātus had various duties. He might have charge of one or more legions in battle. He might also be put in command of a winter camp (hiberna), be dispatched on an independent expedition, or be commissioned to levy troops, to build a fleet, or to hold court as the representative of the general. A lēgātus often performed the duties of adjutant. 

68. Quaestors. Quaestōrēs in the army were paymasters and quartermasters. A competent quaestor was sometimes put in command of a legion in battle. 

69. Prefects. The auxiliaries, both infantry and cavalry, were commanded by praefecti. The chief engineer (praefectus fabrum) had charge of all construction work. 

70. The Commander-in-Chief. After the first important victory of the commanding general his soldiers hailed him as imperator. The title was officially conferred by the Senate, if the victory had added territory to the Roman state. assumed it as a result of his victories in Gaul. Caesar

THE CAMP 

71. A Roman army constructed a fortified camp (castra) at the end of each day's march. A Roman general seldom went into battle without such a camp to retire to in case of defeat. 

A surprise attack by night was impossible. 

72. Site of the Camp. A special detachment under the direction of the chief engineer was sent ahead of the army to choose a suitable location. An ideal spot was a sloping hillside, not too steep, with an abundance of wood, water, and forage near by. It should not be too near a dense wood which might conceal an enemy. 

73. Construction of the Camp. - When the main army arrived, a part of the legionaries was detailed to construct the fortifications (or, as we say, " to dig in "); the necessary tools they carried in their packs. These fortifications consisted of

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

a trench (fossa) and a rampart (agger) formed of the dirt thrown inward from the trench. Perhaps the average dimensions of the trench were nine feet wide at the top, three feet at the bottom, and seven feet deep. The rampart would be about five feet 

VALLUM FOSSA 

M

AGGER VERTICAL SECTION OF A WALL AND DITCH 

ABCD represents a ditch (fossa fastīgāta), nine feet wide and seven feet deep LMNO, a wall or rampart (agger), five feet high and six feet wide at the top, furnished with steps on the inside, that is, on the side MRSN 

PP, palisades (vāllum) 

FF, fascines, imbedded in the work to strengthen it high, and wide enough on top to afford comfortable standing room for the defenders behind the palisade (vallum). The outside of the rampart was faced with sod and bundles of sticks (fascinae), so that it might be made steeper than the natural slope of the earth and not be washed down by the rains. The inner side had a gentle slope, surfaced with horizontal logs, which gave the soldiers a foothold.

The palisade, on the outer edge of the agger, was about four feet high, low enough for the soldiers to fight over it, and high enough for them to crouch behind it. The task of building a camp, great though it was, could be finished within three or four hours.

A Roman camp for five legions was about 2100 feet long and 1400 feet wide. The interior was laid out in blocks like a modern city, with two main streets crossing each other at right angles, and other streets parallel to them. At the ends of the main streets were the four gates, so constructed that when the foe approached, his right side, unprotected by the shield, was exposed. The rampart could be reënforced by towers, redoubts, and other defenses.

The soldiers slept in leather-covered tents accommodating five men each. In winter, there was a permanent camp (castra stativa); the troops were housed in huts thatched with straw or covered with skins. 

74. Watches. The night, from sunset to sunrise, was divided into four equal watches (vigiliae). The guard, a cohort at each gate and sentries on the rampart, was relieved at the end of each watch. The watchword (signum) for the night was 

A ROMAN CAMP 

written on tablets (tesserae) and circulated among the guards. 

The buglers sounded tattoo at nightfall, reveille at dawn, and signals at the end of each watch.

SLC. IO

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL THE MARCH 

75. A marching army (agmen), if not near an enemy, was preceded by a vanguard (primum agmen) of cavalry. The main army came next, each legion followed by its baggage. Each cohort was ordinarily marshaled in a column of centuries, one directly behind the other. The length of the legion thus formed was about 1400 feet, its width some 40 feet. A legion's baggage train was about 650 feet long. The combined length of legion and baggage train was about one third of a mile. 

When near the enemy, a strong vanguard of several legions went first; after them came all the baggage trains assembled into one compact body. The remaining legions came last, forming a strong rear guard (novissimum agmen). This name, "the newest line," was given because new recruits usually marched in the rear. 

Since most of the afternoon was devoted to camp construction, troops did most of their marching in the morning, starting at daylight or even before. The day's march averaged about sixteen miles, though forced marches (magna itinera) might be as much as twenty-five miles, or more. Caesar once marched fifty miles in twenty-four hours.

THE BATTLE 

76. Disposition of Troops. — The ten cohorts of a legion were regularly drawn up in a triple line (triplex aciēs); the first two lines being often armed with light spears (hastae), while the third line bore the heavy javelin (pilum). Occasionally they were arranged in a double line (duplex acies), when no reserve was needed and it was desirable to extend the front. 

In the first line were placed side by side four cohorts, the tried veterans. In the first cohort, on the extreme right, were the finest of these. The first line began the attack. The second line, composed of three cohorts, was so arranged that

the cohorts blocked the intervals between those of the first line. The third line, also of three cohorts, contained the least experienced soldiers. The second line stood ready to relieve the first, while the third line was used to meet any emergency.

The normal depth of each line was probably eight men. 

The cavalry, which was used for preliminary skirmishing, for guarding the flanks, and for pursuing the enemy, was stationed on the wings of the battle line. 

77. In the Battle. - A Roman general attached great importance to the initial charge, which was made preferably from a hillside (ex superiore loco). ADLOCYT CONH ADLOCVT[IO] COH[ORTIS] 

A Roman Officer Address ing a Cohort 

(On a Roman Coin) 

Before battle the leader addressed to each legion a brief speech of encouragement (cohortatio). After the signal to advance (signa inferre), the first line moved forward with even step (certo gradu) until within about 125 paces of the enemy. It then charged on the run (concursu), the first ranks with javelins poised (pilis infestis). At about forty feet the legionaries of the front rank hurled their javelins and drew their swords. 

Then, in succession, each rank advanced and sent its volley. 

The attack of the legions with a volley of javelins followed by hand-to-hand fighting with swords may be compared to a volley of musketry followed by a bayonet charge. 

The battle became a series of duels. The first and second lines of cohorts relieved each other, while the third line of cohorts remained in position to move forward, if it should be necessary.

When hard pressed, the Romans sometimes employed two other formations: the cuneus," wedge," and the orbis, a square with rounded corners. The first was a mass formation to break through the ranks of the enemy; the second was designed to repel attacks from several directions at once.

ROMAN ARTILLERY 

(An onager drawn by horses;

in the Musée Saint Germain) 

THE CAPTURE OF WALLED TOWNS 

78. Artillery. The engines of war were called tormenta (from torquere, to twist, hurl).

The catapulta (catapult) was a huge crossbow of immense power, for sending heavy javelins or other missiles horizon- 

AN ONAGER IN THE MUSÉE SAINT GERMAIN 

tally from a grooved track. The arms of the bow were sticks of timber inserted between tightly-twisted ropes. The cord was drawn back by a windlass. The range was perhaps 400 yards.

The ballista (ballista) was a modifica- tion of the catapulta. Its grooved track was tilted upward at about forty-five degrees. It could hurl huge javelins and stones weighing perhaps 130 pounds.

The onager (wild ass) had a vertical arm, which was pulled to a horizontal position by a windlass. When released, the

arm flew back with great violence. The missile described an arc like that of a projectile from a mortar. The onager could hurl stones weighing 160 pounds, 500 yards. Modern heavy artillery has a range of more than 30 miles. 

The scorpio (scorpion), a mounted crossbow smaller than the catapulta, corresponded to the rapid-fire Maxim.¹ 

79. Protective Apparatus. - During siege operations soldiers were protected as follows:

The vinea (grape arbor) was a shed, some sixteen feet long, seven feet wide, and eight feet high. It had open ends and was mounted on rollers. Several vineae placed end to end would afford a safe passageway to the enemy's wall. The vinea was built of heavy timbers covered with hides. 

The musculus (little mouse) was smaller, with one end open and 

VINEA 

the other covered; it was intended for use under the very walls of the enemy, especially to protect sappers. 

The testudo arietaria, the "tortoise" with the battering ram, was a strongly built shed which carried, suspended from the roof, a battering ram (ariēs), a heavy beam some sixty feet long, armed with a head of bronze or iron. When swung by a number of men, the ram could inflict blows of great violence on a wall or gate.

MUSCULUS 

66 The pluteus was a movable shield about eight feet high, mounted on three 1 A vivid picture of Roman artillery fire before Avaricum is given in Caesar, De Bello Gallico, VII, 25: Standing before the town gate was a Gaul. 

A bolt from one of the mounted pieces of artillery struck him on his right or unguarded side, and he fell dead. wheels, and supplied with loopholes for the shooting of arrows. 

It corresponded to a modern "tank."

The turris ambulātōria (movable tower) was an imposing structure of several stories; it was built out of range, then pushed forward on rollers to the wall. Drawbridges could be let down on the wall, affording a passage for an attacking party ascending from the lower stories. The structure was about twentyfive feet square at the base and twenty feet square at the top, and might reach the height of 180 feet. The 

TESTUDO ARIETARIA 

lowest story usually contained a battering ram, while above were artillery, archers, and slingers, who laid down a "barrage' of missiles in front of those attacking by the drawbridge. 

80. Methods of Assault and Siege. Walled towns could be taken by assault (repentina oppugnatio), siege (oppugnatio), and blockade (obsidio). 

Assault. If a trench surrounded the enemy's wall, this was first filled in with earth and bundles of brush (crātēs). 

Meanwhile the archers and slingers tried to protect their men. 

Shock troops then advanced to scale the wall with ladders (scalae), or to batter down the gates. Sometimes the first rank held their shields in front, vertical and overlapping; the succeeding ranks held theirs overhead, forming an unbroken roof like the back of a tortoise (testūdō).

Siege. The ground before the wall of a besieged town was leveled, the ditch filled. The wall might be undermined,¹ battered by the ram, or torn down by wall hooks (falcēs mūrālēs) attached to long poles. 

1 The advancing gallery was supported by timbers which, after the miners withdrew, were set on fire, so that the gallery caved in, wrecking the enemy's fortifications above. This is "mining" without explosives.

UNITED TI 

HIINHAMIL HGAMAHI ROMANS STORMING A CITY 

Strong defenses were captured by means of the agger (siege mole), a great causeway of logs and earth built at right angles. to the wall and begun about 500 feet away from it, out of range. 

Eventually the agger was extended to the wall, which was scaled from it. The materials were brought through a passageway formed of vineae, in front of which the soldiers were protected by plutei. The agger was forty or fifty feet wide, and often of several stories. If the agger was not so high as the wall, access to the latter was afforded by a tower on the agger itself.

Blockade. Towns impregnable by assault or siege were starved into submission. A ring of entrenchments, crowned with palisades, towers, and redoubts (castella), was made around the town. A counter ring of fortifications of greater circumference and facing in the opposite direction might be constructed as a defense against an army coming to the aid of the besieged. 

Blockade was too slow to be used except as a last resort. Caesar used it but once in the Gallic War, at Alesia (VII, 68-89).

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL THE FLEET 

81. Fleets were constructed as occasion required and manned by legionary soldiers, who corresponded to modern marines. 

Each ship was commanded by a tribune or centurion.

The Roman war ship (navis longa) was propelled by both sails and oars; in battle, oars were the main reliance. The war ship of the usual type, trireme, had three banks of oars.

Under the prow was a metal beak (rōstrum). On the deck a tower was sometimes erected for legionaries and the artillery (cf. the "fighting tops" of battle ships). A trireme was about 150 feet long, twenty feet wide, and of about 235 tons burden. Its speed, without sails, was about ten knots. 

COIN OF ANTONIUS 

(A Roman war ship) 

The enemy might be rammed midships, or a glancing blow might shear away the oars. In boarding a ship the Romans held it with grappling hooks (copulae). A gangplank was dropped to the opposite deck, and soldiers swarmed over this and engaged the foe hand to hand.

Freight vessels and transports (nāvēs onerariae) conveyed men and supplies. They were propelled mainly by sails, and were convoyed by war ships. Vessels for scouting purposes (speculātōria nāvigia) corresponded to the light cruisers of to-day.

CAESAR'S CAMPAIGNS (58-50 B.C.) AND THE WORLD WAR (A.D. 1914-1918) 

82. The German army, entering Belgian territory from near 

Aix, pressed westward up the Meuse (the Mosa described by 

Caesar, B. G. IV, 10), and reduced the fortified city of Liége.

Here they were near Aduatuca, from which the ill-fated army of Sabinus and Cotta had been lured by Ambiorix, and where the despairing fugitives of that army had slain themselves to a man

CAESAR'S CAMPAIGNS AND THE WORLD WAR 155 

(V, 26 ff.). Here, too, Quintus Cicero had successfully withstood the attack of the raiding German tribe of the Sugambri (VI, 35 ff.).

Continuing up the Meuse from Liége the invaders now passed Mont Falhize, the supposed site of the stronghold of the Aduatuci, who acted treacherously after surrendering to Caesar, and were all, to the number of 53,000, sold as slaves (II, 33). 

Now the Germans approached 

Namur, where Quintus Cicero had heroically held his camp against the victorious Ambiorix until the arrival of Caesar (V, 38 ff.). Under the storm of shells the fortresses around Namur quickly crumpled, and the victors marched on to defeat the French army at Charleroi, and to force the terrible retreat of the British contemptible little army" from Mons. 

ROMAN SHIP ENTERING A HARBOR (A Roman Lamp in the British Museum) 

The next great obstacle was the fortress of Maubeuge near the scene of Caesar's crushing defeat of the Nervii (II, 15 ff.).

Maubeuge was unexpectedly able to hold out for almost a month against the great siege guns, long enough to contribute materially to the Allied Victory of the Marne. The main body of the invading host pressed on toward Paris (ancient Lutetia Parisiōrum), where Caesar had once held a council of Gaul (VI, 3); Paris, which the Gauls had burned rather than have fall into the hands of Caesar's lieutenant Labienus, who won a battle here (VII, 57 ff.). The German cannon were brought up within twenty miles of Paris, but the city could not be occupied until the French and British armies were disposed of. 

Three million men now confronted one another in the Battle of the Marne (ancient Matrona, I, 1), on the plain where the

INTRODUCTION TO CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

Roman general Aëtius in the bloodiest conflict of antiquity had defeated Attila the Hun (cf. Encyclopaedia Britannica). In the greatest battle of all history the Germans were now defeated, and retired to prepared positions of immense strength along the heights north of the Aisne (ancient Axona), where Caesar had fortified his lines with especial care, had fought at the fords, and had inflicted great loss on the retreating Belgae (II, 5 ff.). Along the crest of the heights was the famous road 

THE OLDEST PARIS - L'ÎLE DE LA CITÉ, THE ANCIENT LUTETIA 

Chemin des Dames, not to be captured until more than two years later; until then the battlefront stood here. This road, however, was again in German hands at the time of their great drive of 1918, when, at the high-water mark of their advance, they reached Château Thierry, only to find their final effort broken against the "Rock of the Marne," the Americans who held the famous bridge.

Thereafter the invaders retreated eastward across France. 

They asked and obtained an armistice, whereby they agreed to withdraw their troops beyond the Rhine, even as the ancient Germans had been forced by Caesar to withdraw (I, 53). The 

Allies occupied the country to that river along with certain

157 bridgeheads. American troops in 1923 were still stationed just east of the Rhine at the great fortress of Ehrenbreitstein opposite Coblenz (confluentes), not far from the scene of the defeat of the Usipetes and 

Tencteri (IV, 1 ff.), and the sites of Caesar's bridges (IV, 17; VI, 9). 

The mouth of the Loire, from which Caesar's fleet sailed to attack the Veneti (III, 9, 14), received into its modern port of St. 

Nazaire many American troop ships. American converted yachts patrolled this coast, and almost two score 

American destroyers convoyed the troop ships in.

Sea by a great mine field. Two hundred submarines with crews of at least 4000 men lie on the ocean floor around the British Isles. To the west of the Straits where the Gallic merchantmen made their voyages to Britain (IV, 20) the Channel was alive with ships transporting millions of men to and from 

England. Over England as far as Caesar advanced (V, 18), and indeed as far as Scotland, hovered the Zeppelins raining explosives.

In the early part of the war the country of the Morini saw the giant Battle of Flanders, when the Belgians, British, Canadians and other colonials checked the German drive for the Channel. The Allies turned the swampy nature of the coun-

try to the same good account as did the Morini (III, 28 ff.;

Zeebrugge, the German submarine base where British volunteers blocked the harbor channel.

One of the bloodiest battles of the war, that of the Somme, in which the Canadians took a great part, was under way for months somewhat to the east of Amiens (ancient Samarobrīva), where Caesar once had a military base (V, 53). The noble

Cathedral of Amiens itself suffered somewhat from the hostile guns.

As the battlefront stood at the Aisne, Rheims (named from the Rēmi, ancient Durocortorum, VI, 44), and Soissons (named from the Suessiōnēs, ancient Noviodunum, II, 12) received their "baptism of fire."

The decisive battle with the Helvetii (I, 24 ff.) and the culminating campaign of the Gallic War (VII, 68 ff.) were in the heart of France, and so not in the region of the field operations of the World War. The country of the Helvetii (I, 2 ff.) also escaped, since Switzerland was a neutral power.

SPECIAL VOCABULARIES : (11) populor; vāstō; liberi ;

BOOK I 175 

SUMMARY OF BOOK I, CHAPTERS 13-22 

The Helvetii sent an unsuccessful embassy to Caesar, who insisted on hostages and compensation to his allies, as essential to any peace. 

The Helvetii continued their march, closely followed by Caesar's cavalry, which presently suffered a mysterious defeat. The Haedui now failed to send Caesar the promised supplies. In reply to his complaint, Liscus, the Haeduan chief magistrate, laid all responsibility upon Dumnorix, a powerful Haeduan noble friendly to the Helvetii, and the author of the recent reverse. He was the brother of Caesar's friend Diviciacus! Dumnorix was spared for his brother's sake, but sternly warned. Caesar now planned an attack on the Helvetii, first sending Labienus to seize the hill in their rear. The error of Considius, who commanded the scouts and mistook Labienus for the enemy, spoiled the design. Caesar continued to follow the Helvetii. Caesar marches for 

Bibracte, to 

23. THE WAR WITH ARIOVISTUS, THE GERMAN 

1 

SUMMARY OF BOOK I, CHAPTERS 31-49 

Shortly after, the same chiefs obtained a confidential interview with Caesar. Their spokesman, Diviciacus, explained conditions in Gaul. There had been rival factions, one led by the Haedui, the other by the Arverni and Sequani. The latter had called in from 

Germany the chieftain Ariovistus and his "wild and barbarous men."

This, though the Haedui were reduced to subjection, proved a fatal mistake, for a cruel oppression of the Sequani was begun by the Germans. Already 120,000 had crossed the Rhine, and threatened to drive all the Gauls from the country. Ariovistus, after crushing the combined armies of the Gauls, was showing himself a bloodthirsty tyrant. The Gauls looked to Caesar for help. 

After Diviciacus had concluded, his associates implored aid from Caesar with tears, except the Sequani, who sat silent with bowed heads. Diviciacus explained that the Sequani did not dare to complain, being so thoroughly in Ariovistus's power that they could not even flee. Caesar pledged the Gauls his support. 

An invitation by Caesar to a conference was haughtily refused by 

Ariovistus. He wondered, he said, what business Caesar had in his

Gaul, which he had conquered in war. Caesar replied with the following ultimatum: 

1. Ariovistus must bring no more Germans into Gaul. 

2. He must return the Haeduan hostages. 

3. He must refrain from further injuries. Otherwise Caesar "would not overlook the wrongs of the Haedui.” 

1 This and the following summaries owe much to Caesar's Conquest of Gaul (T. Rice Holmes).

MANEUVERS OF ARIOVISTUS

SCALE OF ROMAN MILES

BOOK I 185 

Ariovistus replied arrogantly. The Haedui must continue submissively in his power; otherwise the title, "Brothers of the 

Roman People," would avail them little. Caesar might fight him any time he wished. The German warriors had not been under a roof in fourteen years. 

Now came reports from the Haedui that the Germans were laying waste their fields, and from the Tréveri, on the frontier, that new swarms of them were attempting to cross the 

Rhine. Caesar wished to encounter Ariovistus before he could be reenforced, and, marching night and day, anticipated him in occupying 

Vesontio (Besançon), the chief stronghold of the

Sequani. Here Caesar's difficulties were increased by a panic among his soldiers, due to exaggerated rumors of the prowess of the Germans. Caesar rose to the occasion in a speech before these half-mutinous men; he reminded them how the Germans had gone down before Marius (102 B.C.) and in the Slave Rebellion (71 B.C.); he predicted that the crude strategy by which Ariovistus had defeated the Gauls would be futile against the Romans. If no one else would follow him, he would go forward with his trusty 

Tenth legion alone.

HEF CAULOIS A GALLIC CHIEF 

The confidence of the commander restored the morale of the troops; the Tenth legion thanked him, the others apologized. He now advanced against Ariovistus. His approach drew from Ariovistus the offer of a parley. This was appointed for the fourth day. Ario- SLC. 12

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

vistus insisted that the leaders should bring with them only a cavalry escort. Caesar was too wise to intrust his safety to Gallic cavalry, but he checkmated the German chief by mounting his Tenth legion on the horses of the Gauls. He then led this extemporized cavalry to the place of meeting, a knoll in the middle of a plain. 

Caesar reminded Ariovistus of the unearned honors conferred on him by the Roman Senate, and that the Romans were morally bound to befriend their old allies, the Haedui. He then repeated his ultimatum. Ariovistus replied insolently; he had come, he said, at the request of the Gauls themselves; they had given him territory and hostages of their own accord; they had attacked him and not he them; he was ready to defeat them again as he had before; if "Roman friendship" was to be a detriment to him, he was ready to repudiate it; he was bringing his tribesmen across the Rhine not to attack 

Gaul, but to defend himself. Why was Caesar invading his dominions and interfering with his rights? Caesar must withdraw from Gaul at once, or take the consequences.

Caesar replied that he would keep faith with his allies, and that the Roman right in Gaul was prior to that of the Germans. The interview was abruptly ended by a treacherous attack of the attendants of Ariovistus on Caesar's escort. 

Two days later Ariovistus sought to renew the conference. Caesar sent his interpreter, Procillus, and Metius, a friend of Ariovistus. 

The Germans flung them into chains.

Ariovistus now moved southwards and cut the Roman line of communication. Caesar restored it by marching past the Germans and fortifying a second camp. Ariovistus declines a general battle, but

Caesar establishes his winterquarters at Vesontio (Besançon), 54. Hoc proelio trans Rhenum nuntiātō Suēbi, qui ad rīpās Rhēni venerant, domum reverti coeperunt; quos Ubii, qui proximi Rhēnum incolunt, perterritōs sēnsērunt, insecūti magnum ex iis numerum occiderunt. Caesar ūnā aestāte 5 duōbus maximis bellis confectis, mātūrius paulō quam tempus anni postulabat in hiberna in Sequanōs exercitum deduxit; hibernis Labienum praeposuit; ipse in citeriorem Galliam ad conventus agendos profectus est. and returns to Cisalpine Gaul.

SUMMARY OF BOOK II, CHAPTERS 19-35 

Six legions were building the camp and the Roman cavalry were engaging the enemies' pickets, when the Nervii swept across the

BOOK II 207 river and up the slope against the half-formed Roman line. Caesar had barely time to say a word of encouragement to his favorite Tenth legion. (See map facing p. 204.) 

Some of the legions drove back their foes, but the men of the Twelfth were huddled together so closely that they could hardly use their swords, and nearly every officer was killed or wounded. Seizing a shield from a man in the rearmost rank, Caesar pushed his way through to the front; he called to his centurions by name; he told the men to open up their ranks so that they would be able to use their swords better and charge. At the sound of his voice their spirits rose. Then three legions came up to the aid of the Twelfth and equally hard-pressed Seventh. The Nervii in their turn were hemmed in; man by man, beneath the javelin and the thrust of the short sword, their front ranks fell. Higher rose the heap of prostrate bodies; and leaping upon them the survivors snatched up the fallen javelins and flung them back, till they too fell. 

Caesar then laid siege to the stronghold of the Aduatuci. At first these descendants of the Cimbri and Teutones laughed; but later, when the great moving tower began to approach their walls, they were alarmed and sent envoys to Caesar, who insisted on their unconditional surrender. They appeared to submit, and pretended to surrender all their weapons, but really kept a third of them. In the middle of the night they sallied out, but were driven back into the town with heavy loss. Next day Caesar took the place and sold all within it, 53,000 in number, as slaves.

In Italy after Caesar's dispatches had been read, it was decided to hold a thanksgiving service of fifteen days an honor which no 

Roman citizen had ever received before. 

A ROMAN COIN

BOOK III SUMMARY OF BOOK III, CHAPTERS I-12 GALBA'S CAMPAIGNS IN THE ALPS 

Caesar had sent Servius Galba (57 B.C.) to open up a road across the Great St. Bernard Pass into Italy. The tribes involved were the 

Nantuátes, the Véragri, and the Sedúni. Galba's force consisted of the Twelfth legion and a body of cavalry. He posted two cohorts near St. Maurice, and himself occupied Octodúrus (to-day, Martigny). 

The mountaineers pretended to submit; but, before the Roman fortifications were completed, they attacked Galba. After six hours of vigorous fighting, the enemy were about to win the camp, when the Romans charged simultaneously from the four gates and drove them from the field. But Octodúrus was plainly untenable. 

Next day, therefore, Galba burned the houses in the village, and returned to winter among the Allobroges. 

THE NAVAL VICTORY OVER THE VENETI 

(56 B.C.)

The legion under Publius Crassus had been quartered near the Veneti, who dwelt in the storm-beaten tract of western Brittany. 

Like the modern Bretons, they were the most skillful seamen in Gaul; they had a numerous fleet of vessels, clumsy indeed, but of extraordinary size and strength; and their prosperity depended upon the carrying trade with Britain. They professed to submit; and Crassus sent officers to arrange with them for a supply of grain. However, they detained the officers as prisoners, allied themselves with neighboring tribes, and sent an embassy to Crassus to demand the restoration of the hostages.

Caesar sent instructions to have a fleet built at the mouth of the Loire, to raise oarsmen from the Province, and to collect as many pilots as they could. Meanwhile he himself arranged with Pompey 208

BOOK III 

209 

THE DAUPHINE ALPS 

and Crassus for a five years' extension of his command in Gaul. He then rejoined his army. 

His first step was to distribute the legions more widely. Labienus was sent to the country around Trèves, to keep an eye upon the Belgae and prevent the Germans from crossing the Rhine. Sabinus was directed to disperse the allies of the Veneti on the northern seaboard; while Crassus marched for Aquitania. (See map p. 204.)

The Veneti were equally active. They provisioned their fortresses, assembled their ships, and even sent across the Channel for help. 

The Roman fleet was soon assembled, under Decimus Brutus, at the mouth of the Loire, but the weather was too stormy for it to put to sea. Meanwhile Caesar tried to capture the enemies' strongholds, but with little success. They were at the ends of promontories, connected with the mainland by shoals, which at high tide¹ were submerged. Caesar constructed dikes across the shoals, but before 

1 Caesar speaks (III. 12) of the difficulties presented by the tides to his attack on the Veneti. The narrowing bays on the French coasts cause some remarkable tides. At Mont-Saint-Michel the tide rises 45 feet, then recedes to a distance of 7 miles, leaving exposed 100 square miles of the floor of the bay. On its return it rushes in faster than a horse can gallop, covering the sands with an angry sea.

SUMMARY OF BOOK III, CHAPTERS 17-29 CAMPAIGN OF SABINUS AGAINST THE VENELLI 

The allies of the Veneti had mobilized their forces. Certain tribes, in their feverish eagerness for war, had massacred their senators, simply because they counseled peace.

Sabinus, who could not be enticed from his camp, bribed a Gaul to go over to the enemy, as if a deserter, and tell them that Caesar was hard pressed, and that Sabinus was about to march to assist him. The Gauls were deceived into attempting a surprise attack; but the disciplined cohorts of Sabinus fell upon the flanks of the pant- ΙΟ 

ROMAN SHIPS IN HARBOR

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

ing multitude and sent them flying. The cavalry allowed few to escape, and the hostile league now fell to pieces.

CAMPAIGN OF CRASSUS IN AQUITANIA 

Meanwhile the Aquitani had obtained reënforcements and leaders from their kinsmen, the Spaniards of the Pyrenees. They fortified their encampment in the orthodox Roman fashion, and hoped to starve out the invader. But Crassus forced them to a battle by assaulting their camp. They resisted stoutly, but they had neglected to secure the rear gate; and some fresh cohorts managed to steal in unobserved while the battle was raging at the opposite end. The imprisoned Aquitani and Spaniards made a desperate effort to flee; but the country was one vast open plain, and they were ridden down and slaughtered in thousands. All except the remoter tribes now submitted.

CAMPAIGN OF CAESAR AMONG THE MORINI 

The Morini and the Menapii, two Belgian tribes, allied with the Veneti, alone held out. The summer was nearly over, but Caesar marched to their country, joining Sabinus on the way. Soon violent storms made it impossible to remain in the field. The country was devastated, and the legions returned to winter between the Seine and the Loire. 

A SCENE IN THE ALPS

BOOK IV SUMMARY OF BOOK IV, CHAPTERS 

I-19 

MASSACRE OF THE USIPETES AND TENCTERI 

The Usipetes and Tencteri, two German tribes, crossed the Rhine in spite of the resistance offered by the Menapii, and settled in the territory of the Menapii on the west bank of the river. Caesar marched against the invaders. As he approached them, they sent envoys. Caesar refused their request for lands in Gaul, but suggested that they settle east of the Rhine, among the Ubii. German cavalry, in violation of a flag of truce, attacked Caesar's Gallic cavalry and drove them back to the main army. Next morning the German chiefs came to Caesar's camp to apologize, as they said, for the unauthorized attack. Caesar refused to hear the chiefs, ordered them put under arrest, and annihilated the whole leaderless host. (See p. 157.)

THE BRIDGING OF THE RHINE 

Caesar determined to make assurance doubly sure. As the 

Germans thought so little of crossing the Rhine, he would cross it too. 

Within ten days the great river was spanned by a firm bridge, and the legions were encamped on the German bank. 

Leaving a strong guard at either end, Caesar marched northward against the Sugambri. He had neither the forces nor the inclination to undertake the conquest of Germany. Having accomplished every object for which he had entered the country — punished his enemies, reassured his friends, and made the name of Rome respected — he recrossed the Rhine and destroyed his bridge. Caesar's reasons for crossing to 

Britain so late in the year:

22. Dum in his locis Caesar nāvium parandārum causā morātur, ex magna parte Morinōrum ad eum lēgātī vēnērunt qui se de superiōris temporis consiliō excusarent, quod homines barbarī et nostrae consuetudinis imperīti bellum populo Romānō 5 fecissent, seque ea quae imperāsset factūrōs pollicerentur. 

Hoc sibi Caesar satis opportūnē accidisse arbitrātus, quod neque post tergum hostem relinquere volebat neque belli gerendi propter annī tempus facultātem habēbat neque hās tantulārum rerum occupatiōnēs Britanniae antepōnen- 10 dās iūdicābat, magnum eīs numerum obsidum imperat.

Quibus adductis eōs in fidem recipit. Caesar gathers eighty transports for two legions and eighteen for the cavalry.

Caesar id quod erat suspicātus, aliquid novi 10 ā barbaris initum consili, cohortēs quae in statiōne erant secum in eam partem proficisci, ex reliquis duas in statiōnem succedere, reliquas armārī et cōnfestim sēsē subsequi iussit. Cum paulo longius à castrīs processisset, suōs ab 15 hostibus premi atque aegrē sustinere et cōnsurround the fertā legiōne ex omnibus partibus tēla conicī animadvertit. Nam quod omni ex reliquis partibus dēmessō frumentō pars ūna erat reliqua, suspicātī hostēs hūc nostrōs esse ventūrōs noctū 20 in silvās dēlituerant; tum dispersōs, depositīs armis, in war chariots ranks of the reapers.

How the 

Britons use 33. Genus hoc est ex essedīs pugnae. Primō their chariots per omnes partēs perequitant et tēla coniciunt in war. Their atque ipso terrōre equōrum et strepitu rotārum ōrdinēs plerumque perturbant; et cum sẽ inter equitum turmās insinuāvērunt, ex essedīs dē-5 skill in driving over broken ground. siliunt et pedibus proeliantur. Aurīgae interim paulātim ex proeliō excedunt atque ita currūs conlocant ut, sī illī ā multitudine hostium premantur, expeditum ad suōs receptum habeant. Ita mōbilitātem equitum, stabilitātem peditum in proeliis praestant, ac tantum 10 ūsū cotidianō et exercitatione efficiunt, uti in declīvi ac praecipiti locō incitātōs equōs sustinēre et brevi moderārī ac flectere et per tēmōnem percurrere et in iugō insistere et se inde in currūs citissimē recipere cōnsuĕrint.

Caesar has come just in time. 15 

34. Quibus rebus perturbātīs nostrīs novitāte pugnae tempore opportūnissimō Caesar auxilium tulit; namque eius adventu hostes constiterunt, nostrī sē ex timōre recepērunt. Quo facto, ad lacessendum hostem et committendum proelium aliēnum esse tempus 5 arbitrātus, suō sẽ loco continuit et, brevi tempore intermissō, in castra legiōnēs reduxit. Dum haec geruntur, nostrīs omnibus occupātīs, qui erant in agrīs reliqui discesserunt.

IN FLANDERS, IN THE WORLD WAR 

Dollman 

(By Courtesy of Frost and Reed, Ltd., Fine Arts Publishers, Bristol and London, England)

BOOK V 

SUMMARY OF BOOK V, CHAPTERS I-II THE SECOND EXPEDITION TO BRITAIN 

(54 B.C.)

Caesar had about 600 transports and twenty-eight war ships built in Gaul. His attention was next required by the Treveri. As a point of departure for Britain, Portus Itius (Boulogne) was chosen. Caesar determined to take with him as hostages for the tranquillity of Gaul most of the Gallic chiefs in his camp, particularly Dumnorix. The latter seized an opportunity of flight, but was overtaken by Roman cavalry, and while crying out that he was “a freeman and a citizen of a free state," was, according to Caesar's order, slain. 

Caesar decided to leave behind him Labienus with three legions and 2000 cavalry, in order to secure his base upon the continent. Setting sail at sunset with five legions and the rest of the cavalry, he disembarked next day at noon where he had landed the year before unopposed, because the enemy, seeing more than 800 ships at once, had fled. Leaving the fleet at anchor, he immediately marched against the Britons, crossed a stream in face of resistance, and captured a forest stronghold. The next day a message from the fleet announced that a storm had shattered the ships. Forty of these were hopelessly wrecked; the others were drawn upon the shore and surrounded by a fortification, the soldiers working day and night. 

After ten days Caesar returned to his furthest point of previous advance, only to find the Britons gathered in larger numbers than before, under a very able leader, Cassivellaunus, whose territory lay just north of the Thames. 12.

TRANSLATION OF BOOK V, CHAPTERS 12-14 

"And here a word or two on the island of Britain and its people may not be out of place. Its inland parts are inhabited by a race pronounced by their own tradition to be aboriginal; whereas the population 234

BOOK V 

235 

CLIFF BETWEEN DOVER AND DEAL 

of the south along the coast are Belgic immigrants, who, at first crossing for the sake of war and plunder, afterwards remained to settle and till the soil, and are still known in the large majority of cases by their earlier tribal names. The island is densely populated and everywhere houses meet the eye, very similar in type to those of Gaul, while flocks and herds abound. "The coinage of the country is usually copper, but gold also is current, and bar-iron, weighed in definite quantities, is likewise used as a substitute for money. The two principal metals produced in the island are tin in the midlands, and iron along the coast, although of the latter the supply is extremely small; copper, on the other hand, they have to import. "Of timber, apart from the fir and beech, the species found are just those of Gaul. Of their animals, they have a conscientious scruple against eating the hare, domestic fowl, or goose, though all of these are kept for amusement or as pets. The climate is more temperate than that of Gaul, and the frosts not so severe. 

13. "The island is triangular in form, and on one of its sides looks out upon Gaul. This side is terminated eastwards by the corner of Cantium (Kent), the usual place of landing for ships from the continent, and at the further end by a point that turns toward the south. The length of this side is about 460 miles. The second or western side leans more toward Spain,

BOOK V 237 and off it is situated the island of Hibernia (Ireland), generally supposed to be about half the size of Britain, and separated from it by a sea-passage equal to that from Britain to Gaul. “Halfway across this channel lies an island called Mona (Man), in addition to which, if report speaks true, there are other small islands near the coast, about which certain writers have alleged that for thirty days round the winter solstice there is unbroken night. Though we ourselves inquired carefully into the matter, we could glean no information; and the only evidence we can adduce is that according to the accurate measurements of the water-clock the nights there were seen to be shorter than on the continent. The length of this western side, to borrow again the reckonings of the same authority, is 640 miles. The third side faces north, and is without any adjacent land. Its extreme point may perhaps be said to face towards Germany, and its length is computed at 735 miles. We thus get a grand circumference for the whole island of 1835 miles. 

14. "Of the inhabitants, those of Cantium (Kent), an entirely maritime district, are far the most advanced, and the type of civilization here prevalent differs little from that of Gaul. With most of the more inland tribes, the cultivation of grain disappears, and a pastoral form of life succeeds, flesh and milk forming the principal diet, and skins of animals the dress. On the other hand, the Britons all agree in dyeing their body with woad, a substance that yields a bluish pigment, and in battle greatly increases the wildness of their look. Their hair is worn extremely long, and with the exception of the head and upper lip the entire body is shaved.". LONG. 

SUMMARY OF BOOK V, CHAPTERS 15-39 CAESAR FIGHTS HIS WAY NORTHWARD 

The British cavalry and charioteers proved elusive and dangerous to the Roman legionaries, who were weighted down by their armor and accustomed to fight merely in compact masses. "Encompassed by those rushing chariots, assailed by those nimble groups of skirmishers who would not come to swords' points with them, they found themselves helpless." However, an attack made by the Britons on the Roman army while it was engaged in building camp was repelled. 

At noon next day Caesar sent out three legions and all the cavalry to forage. A daring attack upon these ended in a headlong rout of the Britons. Most of the survivors scattered to their homes, and never thereafter encountered the Romans in a general action.

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL THE BRITONS SURRENDER. CAESAR RETURNS TO GAUL 

Caesar then crossed the Thames, though stakes were set in the river and the enemy was on the further shore. Cassivellaunus, recognizing his inferiority in the field, dismissed all his force but 4000 charioteers, removed his people with their flocks and herds from the path of Caesar's advance, and waged a guerrilla warfare. Caesar was forced to be satisfied with devastating the country. 

Here, as before in Gaul, the mutual antipathies of the Celtic tribes operated to break down their common defense. Caesar received the submission of the Trinovantes, and other tribes followed their example. 

A stronghold of Cassivellaunus was stormed, many of his men were killed, many cattle captured. Meanwhile the allies of the British chieftain had failed in an attack upon the naval camp. Thereupon the Britons gave up the struggle, brought hostages, and agreed to pay tribute. Caesar returned to Gaul, making the passage of the dangerous straits with wonderful good fortune.

THE FATE OF THE ROMAN GARRISON AT ADUATUCA 

A poor harvest in Gaul compelled the Romans to scatter the legions in winter-quarters unusually far apart. Of special interest were the events that befell the legion quartered among the Nervii and commanded by Quintus Cicero, brother of the great orator; also the troops commanded by Sabinus and Cotta and quartered among the

Eburones at Aduatuca, near the site of modern Liège, were treacherously lured from the camp and met a tragic fate. 

About a fortnight after Sabinus and Cotta had taken up quarters for the winter, Ambiorix and Catuvolcus, chieftains of the Eburones, attacked the Roman camp. Repulsed, the Gauls asked a parley, at which the artful Ambiorix, posing as a true friend of the Romans, tried to induce them to abandon their camp and make their way to 

Cicero or Labienus. He meant to draw them outside their works and attack them in the forests. Cotta wished to remain, Sabinus to depart. Sabinus won his point, and the Romans, after a sleepless night, left their fortifications only to fall into an ambush. Then 

Sabinus seemed dazed, while Cotta commanded capably. Hard pressed, the Romans formed a hollow square. Many completely

BOOK V 239 lost their nerve, filling the forest valley in which they had been trapped "with shouting and weeping." Ambiorix commanded skillfully, ordering his lightly armed soldiers to avoid a hand to hand struggle, but to hurl their spears from a distance. Many of the Romans fell, and Cotta himself was wounded. Sabinus then attempted to discuss terms with Ambiorix, who first guaranteed his safety and then murdered him. Cotta and most of the Romans were killed fighting.

Those who escaped to the camp held out with difficulty for the rest of the day, but during the night slew themselves in despair.

The elated Ambiorix next added the Nervii and Aduatuci to his forces, and attacked the camp of Cicero with great fury, so that the Romans held out with difficulty through the first day.

SUMMARY OF BOOK V, CHAPTERS 53-58 GENERAL RESTLESSNESS OF GAUL. DEATH OF INDUTIOMARUS 

Though Labienus was sixty miles away, near the site of modern Trèves, the news of the relief of Cicero reached him in less than nine

BOOK V 247 hours. An attack upon his camp was abandoned by Indutiomarus. 

Caesar determined to winter in Gaul, for the destruction of the command of Sabinus and Cotta encouraged new efforts at revolt. Partly by fear, partly by exhortation, Caesar held most of the Gallic chiefs in obedience. The Senones, however, expelled the king whom Caesar had imposed upon them, and now that a popular leader had at last arisen, the Romans had no friends in Gaul but the Haedui and the Remi.

The Treveri, in the valley of the Moselle, long sought in vain for allies among the Germans. Indutiomarus, nevertheless, gathered his forces. His plan was, first to dispose of Labienus; but that wily opponent lured him to his death by a pretense of fear. While he incautiously lingered at the gates of the Roman camp, the cavalry was sent out with orders to strike a blow at no one till Indutiomarus had been slain. At the fords of the river the Gallic chief perished; and "Gaul was more peaceful." SLC.-15

BOOK VI 

SUMMARY OF Book VI, CHAPTERS 1-8 CAESAR STRIPS AMBIORIX OF ALLIES 

(53 B.C.)

Caesar had reason to believe that the chiefs of Gaul were forming a still more formidable conspiracy. He raised two new legions and obtained a third from Pompey. Meanwhile the Treveri were planning war and were purchasing support of some distant German tribes; the Nervii, Aduatuci, Eburones, and Menapii were in arms; and the Senones and Carnutes were defying his authority. He decided to strike at once. A raid in overwhelming force against the

Nervii disposed of resistance in that quarter; a rapid movement southward from Lutetia forced the Senones and Carnutes to submit; and Caesar was free to devote himself to Ambiorix. The intended victim was stripped of allies. First, the Menapii were followed into the marshes of the Scaldis (Scheldt), and forced to surrender; then reënforcements were sent to Labienus among the Treveri, who were likewise compelled to submit; and their German allies returned to their homes. 

Caesar again bridges the 

Rhine and allies of Ambiorix.
