SUMMARY OF BOOK VI, CHAPTER 24 

The Gauls formerly were more warlike than the Germans and made conquests east of the Rhine on the borders of the Hercynian Forest. 

At present the Gauls, because of nearness to the Roman provinces and of acquaintance with the luxuries of sea-borne commerce, have become weakened by civilization and accustomed to defeat by the 

Germans in war.

TRANSLATION OF BOOK VI, CHAPTERS 25-28 

25. "This Hercynian Forest, to which reference has been made, has a width, north and south, equal to a nine days' march for an ordinarily fast traveler; than this no more accurate computation can be given, and in it the length of a day's journey is left purely conjectural. From west to east it starts from the latitude of the Helvetii, Nemetes, and Rauraci, and follows the line of the Danube as far as the frontiers of the Daci and Anartes. 

There it turns northwards and leaves the neighborhood of the river, skirting in the course of its immense length a large number of different tribes, until it finally loses itself at some remote point never penetrated, even after sixty days' journey, by any German of these parts, with whom, therefore, its eastern limit remains still shrouded in mystery. The forest itself is well known to exhibit a fauna in many respects peculiar; and therefore to set down the most typical and interesting examples may not be thought altogether inappropriate. 

26. "The first species is that of an ox shaped like a deer.¹ From the center of the forehead, between the ears, springs a single horn, higher and 

1 Generally identified with the reindeer.

BOOK VI 259 straighter than those of the deer in our country, and at its summit widely palmated and branched. The females closely resemble the males, and both are to the same extent antlered. 

27. "Another is that called the elk. In outward form and in the particoloration of its skin, this has much in common with the Italian wild goat, though in build it is slightly heavier. Its horns are mutilated and its legs marked by an absence of any natural protuberance or joint; it never lies down to rest, and if accidentally cast, is powerless to recover its position or to raise itself from the ground. Its lairs are the trunks of trees, leaning against which, with its body slightly out of the perpendicular, it will go off to sleep. On discovering from their tracks any of these animals' favorite haunts, the huntsmen either undermine all the trees of the immediate neighborhood, or else cut them through just far enough to leave them apparently still firmly standing. The elks then return and lean against these as usual, and their weight proving too much for the weakened trunks they and their supports come crashing to the ground. 

28. “The third species is that called the urus (aurochs), in size a little below the elephant, in appearance, color, and shape resembling a bull. Their strength and speed are equally remarkable, and if once discovered, neither beast nor man is safe from their attack. The trapping in pits and the killing of these fierce animals forms a favorite pastime with the natives, who find in it not only an admirable training school for the nation's youth, but also a highly popular form of sport; and those who succeed in killing the largest number are, upon the public exhibition of the horns as proof, acclaimed the heroes of the hour. Unfortunately the species is quite useless for domestic use, and all attempts to tame them, even when they have been taken quite young, have hitherto proved unsuccessful. Their horns differ in size, shape, and general appearance from those of Italian domestic cattle; they are always in great demand, and, decorated with a silver band at the mouth, are used as drinking cups at all their more imposing banquets." — LONG. 

SUMMARY OF BOOK VI, CHAPTERS 29-44 CAESAR RESUMES HIS PURSUIT OF AMBIORIX 

It was impossible for a Roman army to live upon the country of the Germans. So Caesar refused to follow them into the forest fastnesses. But in order to keep them in fear of invasion he cut down only the further end of the bridge, and left a strong force to guard the rest of it. He next sent the cavalry in hot pursuit of Ambiorix. The hunted chieftain was almost captured, but was saved

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

by the devotion of his followers and disappeared into the vast forest of the Ardennes. Thence he sent word to his people to escape as best they might. His fellow-chief, Catuvolcus, could find refuge only in the grave.

Caesar followed his cavalry until Aduatuca (Tongres) was reached. 

There he left much baggage guarded by a legion under Quintus Cicero, with strict orders to allow no one to stir from the camp until his return a week later. The Eburones, scattered in the woods and swamps, could be really reached only by small flying parties. Caesar invited the surrounding tribes to join the man-hunt. Along with a great multitude of others came the German tribe of the Sugambri.

THE SUGAMBRI ATTACK THE CAMP OF QUINTUS CICERO 

The Sugambri planned to plunder the camp of Cicero, who had sent half the legion to forage. They burst upon the panic-stricken camp, which was saved by the splendid courage of the centurion, Publius Sextius Baculus, who rose from a sick-bed and led the defense until he fainted where he stood. The Sugambri then turned upon the foragers. Some of these cut their way back to camp, but many perished. Then the Germans rode away. Caesar, arriving somewhat later, found his young soldiers wild with fear. He contented himself with gently rebuking Cicero for disregard of orders. 

He now destroyed every house and field of grain among the Eburones, in order that any who escaped the sword might perish of hunger.

But Ambiorix escaped into the still deeper recesses of the forest. 

CAESAR HOLDS A COUNCIL AND RETURNS TO ITALY 

Foiled here, Caesar held an assembly at Durocortorum (Rheims), at which he executed Acco, the chieftain responsible for the attempted revolt of the Senones and Carnutes; then the legions were quartered for the winter, and Caesar set out for Italy.

SUMMARY OF BOOK VII, CHAPTERS 11-62 EVENTS PRELIMINARY TO THE SIEGE OF AVARICUM 

On the second day Caesar reached Vellaunodunum, which surrendered. Two days later he came to Cenabum. At midnight the inhabitants attempted to escape across the Loire; but the legions were on the watch, the fugitives were taken prisoners, and the town was captured and burned. 

Vercingetorix, learning of Caesar's approach, gave up the siege of 

Gorgobina and advanced against his enemy. The Gallic cavalry arrived at Noviodunum just as the city was about to surrender, but the Gauls were routed by Caesar's German cavalry, and the surrender was completed. Caesar now marched against Avaricum (Bourges), the chief city of the Bituriges.

Vercingetorix in the council advised a change of tactics. Instead of meeting the Romans in battle, the Gauls must starve them out of the country, and therefore must burn their own houses, villages, IO 15

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

and towns, with the stores of grain in them. "If this seems grievous and bitter, it is much worse to have your children and wives dragged away into captivity, to be slain yourselves; which, of necessity, befalls the conquered."

AVARICUM BESIEGED AND TAKEN BY STORM 

To this wise design all agreed. The Bituriges burned many of their towns, and other states did likewise; but when it was proposed to burn Avaricum, the Bituriges pleaded that so fine a city and one so easily defended should be spared. At length Vercingetorix inadvisedly yielded, and so just saved the Romans from being starved out of Farther Gaul. He camped sixteen miles from Avaricum, and, while 

Caesar laid siege to that city, attacked the Roman foraging parties. 

When supplies failed, the Roman soldiers bore privations cheerfully "without a word unsuited to the dignity of the Roman people and to their former victories," and, when their commander offered to withdraw, insisted on continuing the siege, saying that it was better to endure any hardship than to fail to avenge the massacre of Roman citizens at Cenabum. Vercingetorix approached Avaricum to attack the Roman foragers. Caesar, marching against him, found him so strongly posted on a hill surrounded by an almost impassable swamp 

CAESAR'S SIEGE WORKS BEFORE AVARICUM (Model in the Musée Saint Germain) 

that he refused to permit his men to incur the loss of life involved in storming the position. Ten thousand picked men were added to the garrison of Avaricum.

BOOK VII 

269 As the siege proceeded, great skill and courage were shown on both sides. (See page 153.) Caesar found that neither the battering 

LOOKING NORTH FROM AVARICUM 

ram nor fire would destroy the Gallic walls. Also, on one occasion, the besieged in connection with a determined sally set his works on fire. As one of the Gauls would fall before Caesar's artillery, another would step into his place, but in spite of unflinching heroism, the sally was unsuccessful. The garrison at length attempted to escape, but were prevented by their women, who, rather than permit their men to leave them, reported the design to the Romans. The result was wholesale destruction. Of 40,000 men scarcely 800 escaped to 

Vercingetorix, who received them into the camp silently and late at night.

Even after all these disasters the great-souled and patriotic Vercingetorix did not lose heart. He pointed out that the disasters had occurred from neglect of his advice, and promised to win the assistance of the other states of Gaul. "When the Gauls are once united, the whole world cannot resist them," he said. His army took courage, and adversity, which usually lowers the prestige of commanders, only increased that of Vercingetorix. The Gauls now adopted the strenuous Roman methods of fortification. The losses at Avaricum were repaired by new levies.

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL CAESAR'S UNSUCCESSFUL ATTACK UPON GERGOVIA DEFECTION OF THE HAEDUI 

Caesar recruited the strength of his army on the supplies captured at Avaricum. His plan for the campaign was, however, held in abeyance by the dispute of two Haeduan chieftains over the chief magistracy. Caesar found that Convictolitavis had been duly elected by the priests and therefore gave him the coveted place as against his rival Cotus. Then, after exhorting the Haedui to forget their dissensions, he sent Labienus with four legions against the Senones and Parisii, and himself with six legions marched against Gergovia. 

Vercingetorix followed just across the Elaver, and broke down all its bridges. Since the Elaver could not be forded before autumn, Caesar feared he might be held in check there all summer. He therefore sent forward the main body of his army, which Vercingetorix followed. 

Two legions, however, had secretly remained behind. These, unopposed, repaired one of the bridges and the entire army crossed upon it. 

Outwitted, Vercingetorix pushed on rapidly and encamped on the heights opposite Gergovia, his immense force presenting a fearinspiring sight. Ceasar proceeded more slowly and reached Gergovia on the fifth day.

Convictolitavis now schemed to detach the Haedui from the foreign conqueror. Litaviccus, one of his associates, persuaded a 

Haeduan contingent marching as a reënforcement for Caesar to declare for Vercingetorix. Caesar at once left his camp and overawed the 

Haedui. With equal promptness Vercingetorix took advantage of 

Caesar's absence to attack the Roman camp with desperate fury; but Caesar, after an extraordinary march of forty-six miles in a little over twenty-four hours, returned in time to save it.

Caesar realized that he had scarcely a friend in Gaul; he must unite with Labienus and retire from Gergovia. By a sudden dash he captured three of the enemy's camps. His soldiers, however, followed up their success in disregard of orders. Some even mounted the enemy's wall; but their foes returned, the Romans on the wall were hurled down dead, and the rest were compelled to retreat. 

Caesar reproved his men for their disobedience, but encouraged them to further effort. He then marched away into the country of the Haedui.

BOOK VII 

271 

Viridomarus and Eporedorix, two Haeduan nobles, proved treacherous; and presently all the Haedui had revolted from Caesar. At 

Noviodunum, one of their cities, they possessed themselves of his hostages and of a large part of his treasury, and destroyed his reserve supplies of grain. Caesar, however, was able to cross the swollen 

Loire and obtain necessary supplies. 

EXPEDITION OF LABIENUS AGAINST THE PARISII 

Meanwhile Labienus set out from Agedincum to attack Lutetia

Parisiōrum (Paris). Rather than permit it to be captured, the Parisii burned their city. Labienus was soon in imminent danger between the Parisii and the newly revolted Bellovaci. A sudden storm and clever maneuvering enabled him to cross the river, to defeat the enemy, and to surround and slay them, together with Camulogenus, their aged but capable and valiant leader. He then returned unmolested to Agedincum. 

Even the Haedui, have joined the uprising.

TRANSLATION OF BOOK VII, CHAPTERS 72-89 

(With Omissions) 

72. '. . . The following system of defences was now devised by Caesar.

A large twenty-foot ditch, the sides of which were dug perpendicular, making mouth and base of equal width, was first constructed round the city; behind this the remaining fortifications, for a reason connected with the peculiar condition of the blockade, were then withdrawn for a distance of 700 yards. For inasmuch as a very wide circuit of country had of necessity to be embraced, it was not always possible upon such extended lines to maintain an unbroken cordon of troops; and some means had therefore to be found, both of guarding against surprise by night from superior numbers of the enemy, and of protecting the troops by day when engaged upon the works from the harassment to which they would otherwise have been exposed. "With this interval, then, two parallel trenches, each fifteen feet wide and the same in depth, were next dug round the position; of which the interior, where it traversed the low-lying and open sections of the ground, was filled with water drawn from the neighboring streams. In rear of these came the ramparts formed of mound and palisade, twelve feet high in all, surmounted by a crenellated parapet, with large antler-like branches springing from the juncture of the mound and breastworks to break any rush by the enemy; while at regular intervals of eighty feet, the entire ring of siege works was punctuated with a series of stout wooden towers. 

73. "All this entailed a vast amount of labor, and imposed a multiplicity of duties upon the troops. Day by day timber had to be cut and carried, provisions had to be collected and transported, immense siege works had to be erected; and as these undertakings frequently involved long journeys from camp, it was inevitable that the Roman forces available for the purpose should become greatly reduced. In addition, as was only natural, the Gauls made periodic attempts to test the strength of the fortifications, and, 20

BOOK VII 

279 debouching from several gates at once, would deliver a spirited attack upon the lines. "It was soon plain to Caesar, that, in order to husband his troops, and the better to economize the defence, artificial obstructions of a still more extensive and complex description were required. These he proceeded, CAESAR'S WORKS BEFORE ALESIA, FROM THE SIDE (Model in the Musée Saint Germain) 

therefore, to construct as follows: Trunks of trees or boughs of exceptional strength were cut down and their heads lopped and sharpened to a point. 

Ditches, five feet deep, were then everywhere dug about the ground, into which the timbers so formed were dropped, and after being firmly fastened at the base, to prevent their being wrenched aside, were left to stand with their lateral branches just resting above the soil. These being arranged in rows of five, and tied and lashed together into a single set, any one who was then unfortunate enough to trespass upon what the troops facetiously termed their 'boundary stones,' found himself impaled upon spikes of the most deadly nature. In front of these, set zigzag fashion, a number of pits were next dug out, each three feet deep, with sides slightly converging inwards. 

Into these, after their upper ends had been sharply pointed and hardened off in the fire, smooth stout logs, of about the diameter of a man's thigh, were lowered, with four inches or less left standing above the ground; and a foot or so of each hole having been filled in and stamped down to secure firmness at the base, the rest was overlaid with brushwood and brambles, so as the more perfectly to disguise the trap. The rows of this form of entanglement were placed eight deep, three feet separating each; and the nickname given them by the troops, from a fancied resemblance to the flower, was that of 'lilies.' As a last obstacle the ground in front of these

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

was planted thick with small wooden pegs, dug down to their full depth of one foot, but bearing on their heads a highly formidable iron barb, which once more, in the rough language of the soldiers, were to serve as 'spurs' to their opponents. 

74. "Such, then, was the system of defences that formed the Roman lines of contravallation against the Gauls under Vercingetorix in the town; and as soon as it was completed, a similar system in all respects was at once constructed as lines of circumvallation against the anticipated succors from 

CAESAR'S WORKS BEFORE ALESIA, FROM THE FRONT 

(Model in the Musée Saint Germain) without. These last were drawn in accordance with the natural features of the ground, and embraced a circuit of nearly fourteen miles. In this manner Caesar confidently believed that his rear was sufficiently fortified against any attack that might be made upon it. Yet in spite of this, in view of the possibility of having to abandon the blockade under circumstances fraught with danger to himself, a standing order was maintained that all sections of the army should keep constantly by them thirty days' rations both of food and fodder. 

75. "Whilst the Romans were thus toiling at Alesia, the Gallic nation was preparing for its great counterstroke. A national assembly of all the more distinguished chiefs was summoned, at which it was decided to modify the original proposals of Vercingetorix and, in lieu of a mobilization of the adult population of the country, to make each of the allies responsible for a definite number of combatants. 

76. ". . . The result was shortly seen when 8000 cavalry and some 

240,000 infantry stood ready to march for the seat of operations. In a grand review held in the country of the Haedui the official numbers were checked and the officers were appointed. The supreme command was

divided between four great chieftains, Commius the Atrebatian, the Haeduan leaders Viridomarus and Eporedorix, and an Arvernian named Vercassi- 

THE FINAL EFFORT OF THE GAULS 

vellaunus, a cousin of 

Vercingetorix. These in turn were supported by elected representatives of each tribe, who formed a central and consultative body for the general supervision of the war. In buoyant spirits and lighthearted gaiety the vast assemblage now started for Alesia; nor was there in its ranks a single man who questioned the result. 

Arguing that the Romans would now be caught between two fires, they were satisfied that these could never face a desperate sally from the town on the interior lines of circumvallation, and at the same time endure the sight of squadron after squadron and regiment after regiment deploying from outside. 

77, 78. "Meanwhile at Alesia itself the covenanted day for the appearance of the relieving force had come and gone; supplies were exhausted, and no ray of light as to what was passing among the Haedui had as yet penetrated to the city. A council of war was summoned, and a discussion was opened on the final chapter of the siege. ... 

79. "Meanwhile the Gallic army under Commius and his compatriots, destined for the relief of Alesia, had reached the environs of that city, and seizing an outlying hill, not more than a mile from the Roman works, had entrenched itself upon it. On the morrow after their arrival, their cavalry descended to the open plain (which, as will be remembered, intervenes for a space of three miles between the two converging lines of heights) and filled it from end to end. Their infantry also at the same time moved out in force, taking cover among the adjacent hills. As all the low-lying ground was in full view of Alesia, the garrison no sooner beheld their friends swarming in the valley below, than rushing to get a sight of the auspicious event,

BOOK VII 

283 they abandoned themselves to an ecstasy of joy. The gates were flung open, and once more the fighting forces moved out to a position before the town, where, filling the first of the Roman trenches with fascines and rubbish, they made every preparation for a sally or other similar effort 80. "On the Roman side Caesar had long perfected his arrangement, and every section of the army having had its proper quarters assigned it along both the outer and the inner lines, each man was able, at a moment's notice, to go straight to his individual station on the walls. The hour for action, therefore, having come, the Roman commander ordered his cavalry to advance from camp, and at once to engage the enemy. From their respective points in the huge amphitheater of hills the various Roman camps looked down upon the field of battle, and thousands of legionaries now took up their stand to watch with straining eyes the fortunes of the fight. 

Interspersed at intervals among the Gallic horse were archers and other light-armed skirmishers, valuable alike for the assistance they could give their own men at a critical moment, and for breaking a charge from the opposing cavalry. The Roman troopers suffered heavily from these sharpshooters, and large numbers had to leave the field. "In a little while the Gauls became assured that in sheer fighting qualities they were the superior of their opponents, whilst numerically, of course, as they could see, they far outdistanced them. From both sides, therefore, of the combatants, both from those who were so closely immured within the iron ring of the Roman siege lines, and from those whose work it was to pierce this ring, fierce war cries now rent the air, each answering each, as all parties strove to nerve their champions to the fray. On an arena which was commanded by every eye, and on which, therefore, no act of special gallantry or its reverse could escape detection, the eagerness to win applause and the dread of being branded as a coward were powerful incentives to either army. "All through the afternoon the battle raged blindly, victory inclining now this way and now that, when, towards sunset, the Germans in a certain quarter of the field closed up their squadrons, and charging home with fury upon the enemy, drove them back at the sword's point. Their overthrow at once exposed the archers who had been acting in support, and who were now quickly surrounded and cut down. The whole line thereupon gave way, and following up their advantage, without once permitting any chance of rally, the Roman horsemen pushed their pursuit almost as far as the enemy's intrenchments. "Thus the first round of the great contest was over, and its result was a cruel blow to the new hopes of the besieged; they had moved out to witness a victory, and it was with heavy hearts and premonitions of ultimate defeat that they now regained the town. SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

81. "After a day's interval had elapsed for the adequate preparations of fascines and scaling ladders, the Gauls once more issued from camp, and about midnight noiselessly made their way towards that portion of the Roman works that spanned the low-lying flats. There, startling the night with a shout that echoed up to the besieged and was meant to apprise them of their advance, they suddenly opened upon the Roman guards a tremendous fire of sling bolts, arrows, and stones, intended to sweep them from the ramparts; and then, covered by this, pro- 

Iceeded to throw down their fascines and to make ready for 

IN THE LAND OF THE ARVERNI 

(Auvergne) escalade. At the same instant Vercingetorix's bugle call was heard answering from the town, and the besieged began to move down the hill. "The alarm found the Romans in no wise unprepared; for, since every man from previous practice knew instinctively his position on the walls, they at once advanced to the lines, where, by means of the various kinds of ammunition that had been accumulated for the purpose, they endeavored to keep down the assailant's fire. The darkness being all but impenetrable, the number of wounded men on either side rapidly increased, the greatest damage being caused by the artillery. The part of the lines chosen for the assault by the enemy was under command of M. Antonius and C. Trebonius; and these officers, to relieve the great pressure upon the defenders, now from time to time withdrew battalions from the nearest forts and dispatched them to the point of danger. 

82. "Generally speaking, it was only while at a distance from the works that the fire of the Gauls, owing to their weight of numbers, proved so destructive; at closer range it rapidly died down before the manifold obstructions that barred their way. As they advanced, many, to their dismay, tripped upon the 'spurs' that everywhere studded the ground; others tumbled into the pits, and were forthwith spitted upon the 'lily' heads; others, again, who survived these perils, were thrust through with the great siege pikes wielded from the rampart and towers, and fell back into the ditch. "So it continued till dawn, the enemy's wounded steadily mounting up, and the defences still unbreached. With daylight, however, they became anxious lest their unshielded flank should be turned by a descent in force

BOOK VII 

285 from the camps above, and hastily retreated to their lines. As to the concerted attack from within, the assailants here wasted so much time in filling up the outer trenches and in getting into position the various instruments of breach which they had so carefully prepared beforehand, that before they could come within effective range of the Romans the report arrived that the others had departed. Their coöperation thus proved abortive, and they returned to the town without result. 

83. "Thus the Gauls had twice essayed the task before them and twice they had conspicuously failed. Before entering on a third attempt, they resolved to summon to their assistance the advice of those acquainted with the neighborhood, and to make a careful study of the position and fortification of those Roman camps that lay upon the heights. "On the north side there rose a hill, the formidable dimensions of which had prevented the Roman engineers from completely embracing it within their lines; and the camp here formed had perforce to be left in a somewhat critical position, on a gently falling slope. This camp was occupied by two legions, under command of C. Antistius Reginus and C. Caninius 

Rebilus, two of Caesar's generals of division. Having therefore acquainted themselves through their scouts with the character of the surrounding country, the enemy's leaders proceeded to form a picked body of 60,000 Gallic infantry, drawn from such tribes as were generally considered the hardest fighters; and a plan of action and the method of carrying it out having been secretly agreed upon, it was arranged to make their approach as nearly as possible at the hour of noon. "The leadership of this force was intrusted to the Arvernian chieftain 

Vercassivellaunus, one of their four supreme generals and a near kinsman of Vercingetorix. Leaving camp shortly after sundown, and marching uninterruptedly throughout the night, this division reached its destination with the earliest streak of dawn, and ensconcing itself behind the shoulder of the hill that has already been mentioned, lay down to rest after its long fatigue. As soon as their commander judged it close to midday, he once more set his men in motion for the particular camp that has been described; whilst simultaneously the Gallic cavalry advanced from its main position towards that section of the works that crossed the plain, and their other forces showed themselves outside the lines. 

84. "Each of these movements was plainly discernible by Vercingetorix from his watchtower in Alesia, and accordingly he at once moved out to support the main attack. Fascines, poles, battery sheds, hooks for breaching the rampart, and the various other requisites for a successful sortie, previously prepared for the purpose, were now brought up. Thus, at a

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

single moment, from end to end of the Roman lines the roar of battle broke upon the ear. "The enemy, made desperate by past failure, were resolved to leave no stone unturned, and with sure judgment swooped upon every weak spot in the defences. With such extended lines to guard, the Roman strength was necessarily much divided, nor was it easy to meet adequately the manifold demands that were made upon it. Not the least of the many disconcerting circumstances to which the legionaries were now exposed was the hoarse shout of battle in their rear. They were continually conscious that their own position was dependent upon the steadfast perseverance of others; and of all tests by which human nature can be tried, the most severe is of some peril that obstinately remains unseen. 

85. "Selecting some central point from which to survey the field of battle, Caesar stood ready to reënforce all threatened points. A common instinct told all combatants, friend and foe alike, that the hour for a superhuman effort had arrived. The Gauls knew well that, unless they could pierce and break that fortified ring, their cause was lost. The Romans saw before their eyes, with every stroke they dealt, the longed-for consummation of all their hopes. "The most critical fighting occurred along the upper line of works, against which Vercassivellaunus, it will be remembered, had been dispatched. The additional strain here imposed upon the defence by the inclined position of the camp was tremendous. The enemy dividing their forces, while some threatened the battlements with their fire, others advanced to the breach under the usual formation on such occasions - the 'tortoise' roof of shields; and for both operations a steady stream of reserves stood always ready. 

Every available man brought up his load of earth and rubbish to make a causeway, and soon not only had the Gauls a clear ascent before them to the ramparts, but by the same methods the Roman obstructions hidden in the ground were also neutralized. Strength and ammunition were both fast failing the hard-pressed troops. 

86. "On receiving information of the critical state of things in this quarter of the field, Caesar detached Labienus with six battalions to relieve the pressure. Should that fail to check the advance, then the officer had instructions to withdraw contingents from the surrounding force, and, issuing from the lines, to fight an action in the open. This last measure, however, was only to be adopted in case all others failed. The Roman commander elsewhere strove to animate the defence by a personal visit to the trenches; where, appealing to his sorely harassed men not to succumb beneath the trial, he showed how on that day and on that hour hung the fruit of all their victories.

BOOK VII 

287 "On the town side, the attacking party had decided to abstain from any attempt against the lower works where they crossed the plain, as being too formidable for assault; and directing their energies against the rugged ground above, they proceeded to bring up into position the various engines of offense that they had prepared beforehand. With a murderous fire of spears and other missiles, they drove back the outposts from their place upon the turrets, and then, hastening to fill the trenches with earth and fascines, strove to wrench aside the rampart and parapet with the powerful hooks they had made for the purpose. 

87. "A relieving force was at once dispatched by Caesar under the younger Brutus, quickly followed by a second under C. Fabius; and, the battle still continuing to increase in fury, he himself led up to their support a third body of reserves. This turned the scale in favor of the Romans, and the enemy being here definitely repulsed, he was able to proceed with all haste to that part of the field to which Labienus had previously been sent. 

For this purpose he detached four battalions from the neighboring redoubt, adding also a force of cavalry, part of which was to go with the infantry, and part to make a detour of the outer lines and to take the Gauls in the rear. 

Meanwhile Labienus himself had found it more and more difficult to cope with the severity of the attack, and ditch and earthwork each failing to stop the advance, he rapidly concentrated some forty separate battalions, taking as occasion offered from all the nearest forts, and then informed Caesar of what he conceived the situation now demanded. Caesar thereupon strained every nerve to reach the scene of action. 

88. "From their position on the heights above, the enemy had a wide prospect of all the rolling country at their feet, and recognizing the approach of the Roman commander-in-chief from the conspicuous color of his uniform, usually worn by him in action, and the infantry battalions and cavalry squadrons following at his back, they lost no time in opening the engagement. "As the two hostile lines advanced upon each other, both raised the shout of battle, a shout that was immediately taken up and echoed all down the line of ramparts even to the most distant point of the works. 

Discarding the use of the heavy javelin, the Romans trusted solely to their swords. The enemy maintained a stubborn fight till suddenly in their rear appeared the Roman cavalry, and, fresh battalions coming up at that moment to swell the attack, the Gauls then turned and fled. “The wave of fugitives broke itself upon the serried lines of horsemen waiting to receive them, and a fearful carnage ensued. . . . Among those taken in the rout was the famous Arvernian leader Vercassivellaunus. 

Eighty-four regimental colors were brought as trophies to Caesar; and of the

SELECTIONS FROM CAESAR'S WAR IN GAUL 

once majestic host that had marched to the relief of Alesia, only a tiny fraction struggled back to camp. The sight of this unresisting slaughter of their comrades crushed the last spark of hope from the spirits of the garrison, and a general retreat from the inner face of the lines now took 

VERCINGETORIX SURRENDERS TO CAESAR 

place. This in turn reacted on those without, since immediately upon the news, the entire Gallic encampment broke up in confusion and fled; and but for the exhaustion of the troops caused by the repeated calls made upon their strength throughout an exceptionally trying day, the annihilation of the enemy must have followed. was, the cavalry a little after midnight was sent in pursuit, and catching up with the rear guard, took heavy toll in killed and prisoners. The rest of the fugitives then scattered and melted away to their homes. 

As it 

89. "On the morrow of this disastrous day Vercingetorix convened a meeting of his captains, and, addressing them for the last time as their commander, told them that, as he had not entered on this war from motives of selfaggrandizement or pride, but solely as the champion of a downtrodden people, now that Fortune had proved ungracious, to her decision he meant to bow. Since such was to be the end, he was ready on his side, he continued, to submit himself absolutely to their decree; and they could either kill him, and so satisfy the just demands of the Romans, or they could deliver him alive into their hands. "A message to this effect was sent out by the garrison to Caesar, who returned the answer that all arms must be surrendered and every chief appear personally before him. Taking his seat at a point well within the

BOOK VII 

289 fortified lines, a little distance from his own camp, the Roman governor then formally received their submission. The Gallic chiefs were brought out under escort, Vercingetorix was handed over as a prisoner, and all arms were thrown to the ground. The remaining Gauls, with the exception of the Haedui and Arverni, who were retained in the hope of bringing pressure to bear upon their governments, were then distributed as booty, one to every Roman soldier." - LONG.

Gestārum. C., the early symbol for both C. and G., is the abbreviation of Gaius. Commentării means notes, memoirs. In English this title would be Notes on the Gallic War. By Gaius Julius Caesar.

Interea: the Helvetii had set March 28 as the day for assembling at the Rhone; their departure would have taken place immediately afterwards, but Caesar's refusal to give a definite answer to their petition until April 13 delayed them. legiōne... militibus: abl. of means, although referring to persons (48). 

5. milia: acc. of extent of space (41). in altitudinem: acc. of place to which, depending upon the idea of motion in perducit (42). pedum sēdecim: to the height of sixteen feet (21). Caesar fortified the south bank of the Rhone from Lake Geneva to the Jura Mountains (Le Pas de l'Écluse," the Pass of the Sluice"). Modern engineers have SLC. 18

discovered remains of these defenses; only in certain exposed sections was it necessary to build a complete wall (all together making about three out of the nineteen miles); elsewhere the steep cliffs, scarped and reënforced, sufficed as a rampart. (See map, p. 164.)

CHAPTER IO 

Page 171, line 1. Helvetiis esse in animō: that the Helvetii had in mind. 

3. Santonum: the Santones lived north of the mouth of the Garonne; their name is preserved in Saintonge, the name of a province of medieval France. ... nōn longè . . . absunt: the Santones were between 100 and 200 miles away from the Tolosates and it seems unlikely that the Helvetii could have made raids into the Province from that distance. 

4. Tolōsātium: their chief city was Tolosa (Toulouse).

T. Labiēnum: see p. 128. 

11. magnis itineribus: usually, by forced marches; here, very rapidly. 

12. Aquileiam: founded in 181 B.C. by the Romans as a frontier fortress; it was at the head of the Adriatic. A modern town preserves the ancient name unchanged. 

18. Compluribus his proeliis pulsis: interlocked order: the abl. abs. his pulsis is modified by the abl. of means compluribus proeliis. 

Caesar was in need of soldiers; at first he had only one legion (X); then he enrolled two in Cisalpine Gaul (XI, XII), and summoned three from Aquileia (VII, VIII, IX). Allowing 3600 men to a legion, he had now at his command about 21,600 legionaries and the troops, probably cavalry, which the Province had raised for him. The fighting strength of the Helvetii was 92,000 - almost three times as great. 

Trace on the map (pp. 136-137) the proposed course of the Helvetii and also the movements of Caesar here described. How may the Alps be traversed to-day?

Postrīdie eius diēī: (lit., on the-after-day-of this supererat: quod-causal clause (82); so, too, is quod aberat (1.9) in this same sentence. biduum: notice that the Latin prefers this single word to duo diēs. 

So also triduum takes the place of très diēs. Cf. biennium, chap. 3, line 12. • • 2. cum oporteret: translate cum before the time when. Cum approaches here the meaning of priusquam, and the subjunctive expresses anticipation (84, ƒ). frumentum . . . oporteret: every two weeks the Roman soldier received his allotment of grain, about two pounds for each day. 

4. Bibracte: the form is ablative. Cf. the same word in line 14. 

The site of Bibracte is Mont Beuvray, 12 miles west of Autun. Interesting remains of the Haeduan stronghold have been brought to light by the excavators wall, streets, houses, workshops, and 1500 coins.

19. seu quod . . . existimărent . . sive eo, quod . . . confiderent (1. 23): the subjunctive is used because these are the reasons, not of

Caesar but of the Helvetii. Somewhat illogically the verbs of thinking and trusting are put in the subjunctive, instead of the words which express the thought and the trust. Quod . . . existimārent is a quodcausal clause (82); quod . . . cönfiderent, which has practically the same force, is grammatically a quod-subst. clause of fact, in apposition with eō (92, 110). 

21. eō magis: eō is abl. of cause (so, too, the eō in line 22). quod . . . commisissent: quod-substantive clause in apposition with eō (92); cf. eo, quod . . . cōnfiderent (1. 23). 

22. rẽ frumentāriā: abl. of separation (43). interclūdi posse: understand eōs (i.e. Rōmānōs) as the subject. 

23. commūtātō . . . converso: try rendering these ablative absolutes by finite verbs coördinate with coeperunt.

CHAPTER 26 Page 178, line 1. ancipiti proelio: abl. of manner (51). The origin of this double or two-fold battle was described in the last sentence of the preceding chapter. 

2. pugnatum est: lit., it was fought (15); how can you avoid the use of the impersonal construction in English?

Diutius: emphasized by its position before cum. cum possent: cum-circumstantial (84, b); cause as well as time is expressed by this clause. 

3. alteri . . . alteri: one division . . . the other; each of these words stands in " partial apposition " to the subject of possent. Consult the plan of the battle (p. 181), and you will readily see that the first alteri refers to the Helvetii and the second to the Boii and the Tulingi. 

5. hoc tōtō proelio: in the course of this whole battle; abl. of time within which (58). 

6. cum... pugnatum sit: a cum-adversative clause (86; cf. historical perfect indicative, 70, b); the perfect subjunctive is here used as a secondary tense (113). ab hōrā septimā ad vesperum: the day, from sunrise to sunset, was divided into twelve hours. This battle lasted from one to about eight. 

7. äversum hostem: the back of a foe; what is it literally? If this is to be taken as an unexaggerated statement, then the retreat mentioned in the previous sentence must have been effected by a gradual stepping backward, with no cessation of fighting. 

8. Ad multam noctem: till late at night. etiam: temporal in force, still. Connect it with the verb and translate till late at night the fighting near the baggage still went on. 

10. è locō superiōre . . . inter carròs rotāsque (l. 11): some stood in the carts, others fought between them, standing on the ground.

13. cum esset pugnatum: what kind of clause (84, b)? impedimentis castrisque: syntax (54)? 

14. è filiis equal to a genitive of the whole (22). 

15. captus: agrees with the nearer subject. 

16. milia CXXX: the original number of the Helvetii and their allies was 368,000. If the destruction of the Tigurini had decreased this number by one fourth, there were, approximately, 276,000 before the battle at Mont Beuvray; since 130,000 survive, 146,000 must have perished. Compare with this the loss of men in some battles of modern times. (The loss of the Union army at Gettysburg was 

23,000; 600,000 men were killed at Ypres; Verdun cost the Germans from 250,000 to 500,000 casualties; the losses of the American army in the World War were 255,118 killed and wounded; to the end of 

1918, 2,079,880 men had been transported to Europe; in the battle of the Marne 3,000,000 men were engaged.)

19. quod . . . esset: a quod-substantive clause of fact quoted in implied indirect discourse (92, 110). 

21. ut mātrēs . . . dēclārārent: substantive clause of result (88), in apposition with consuētūdō. 

22. sortibus et vāticinātiōnibus: by lots and prophecies. From 

Tacitus we learn that one way of casting lots was to cut into small bits twigs from fruit trees, mark these with various signs, scatter them on a white cloth, draw three at random, and interpret coming events by the signs upon them. As to the prophecies, Plutarch mentions the eddies and sounds of rushing water as a source of inspiration. 

24. utrum. . . esset necne: whether or not a battle was expedient, a double indirect question (93); the subject of esset is the infinitive phrase proelium committi. ex ūsů: (lit., out of use) advantageous, expedient.

16. trinis catenis: three sets of chains; the distributive numeral is used instead of the cardinal because catenae, although singular in meaning, is plural in form.

20. honestissimum: most honorable. Honestus rarely means honest. 

21. hospitem: guest-friend. Two men living in different places often entered into a highly-prized relationship, hospitium, guest-friendship, which insured the exchange of hospitality and various other kindly services. ēreptum . . . restitūtum: in English we feel the need of a connective between the two participles. 

22. neque ..dēminuerat: this verb, like vidēbat, is introduced by quod (1. 19); neque = et. nōn. Translate and because by disaster to him (Procillus) fortune had not taken anything away from his (Caesar's) great pleasure and rejoicing.

Three has often been thought of as a magic or sacred number. consultum (esse): indirect discourse depending upon dicebat; avoid the impersonal construction in English. utrum . . . necārētur an reservārētur : a double indirect question, dependent upon consultum (93).

And Caesar where was he to "The victory was decisive. go? 

What use was he to make of his victory? It would be fatal to withdraw his legions into the Province. That would be to invite the 

Germans to attempt a new invasion: to confess weakness to Gaul. 

Fortune beckoned him on. Gaul was disunited: her foremost state was on his side; and others felt the spell of his success. To bring those gifted peoples under the civilizing sway of Rome, to open their broad lands to Italian enterprise · that was a work to satisfy the most soaring ambition. For the present indeed he must return to Cisalpine 

Gaul, to conduct the civil duties of his government and watch the politics of Italy; but leaving his legions under the command of Labienus, he quartered them for the winter in the stronghold of Vesontio. In that last act of his we may read the registration of a great resolve; and doubtless he reflected, as he traveled southward, upon the magnitude of the undertaking to which he had committed himself. For to all who had eyes to see and ears to hear he had made it evident that his purpose was nothing less than the conquest of Gaul." (T. R. H.) 

How long did the two campaigns of 58 B.C. last? In what connection has Caesar already mentioned Labienus?

Cum esset Caesar in citeriōre Gallia: notice how closely this opening clause connects Book II with Book I, which ends with ipse in citeriōrem Galliam ad conventus agendōs profectus est. ita... dēmōnstrāvimus: that is, in the words just quoted. Ita strengthens uti; it need not be translated. The first person plural,

subject of the verb, is what we call in English "the editorial we"; so, too, dixerāmus, line 5. 

2. crēbrī: this adjective, together with the imperfect tense of adferebantur and fiebat, shows that the information kept coming to Caesar all through the winter; bring this out in your translation. 

3. Labiēnī: why did he send this news to Caesar? 

4. Belgās coniurare... dare: that the Belgae were forming a league and giving; indirect discourse (108) depending upon both adferebantur and certior fīēbat. Give in direct discourse. quam . . dixerāmus: not, of course, a part of the indirect discourse; quam, standing between an antecedent (Belgās) and a predicate noun (partem) which are unlike in gender and number, agrees with the latter. 6. inter sē: to one another.

Caesar's fortifications by the Axona (Aisne) were elaborate enough for the occasion. In the World War, however, the line of the Aisne was to become the Front and to be defended by one of the strongest lines of fortification ever constructed. 23. Quod si fecerit: and if he should do this; a future more vivid condition in indirect discourse (95, 110). What would fecerit be in direct discourse? 

24. (eum) amplificatūrum (esse): eum is, of course, Caesar. quōrum . . . cōnsuĕrint: by whose aid and resources they were wont to make successful resistance whenever wars broke out.

This people, whom of all his enemies Caesar most respected, and of whom he wrote with one of those rare touches of enthusiasm that here and there relieve the severity of his narrative, were the Nervii." (T. R. H.)

Page 205, line 2. qui . . . dēligant: rel. clause of purpose (76); explain the sequence (72, b). 

3. Cum . . . facerent: this cum-circumstantial clause (84, b) modifies the ablative absolute consuētūdine . . . perspectā (1. 6), rather than the main verb pervēnērunt (1. 7). Either translate the cumclause as a declarative statement (complūrēs . . . faciēbant), beginning a new sentence with quidam, or leave the cum-clause as a clause in English and make the ablative absolute a main statement, beginning a new sentence with nocte. ex ... Gallis: of the Belgae who had surrendered and other Gauls.

inter singulas legiōnēs; between every two legions.

13. ut . . . audērent: a substantive clause of result (88), subject of futurum (esse). 

14. Adiuvābat: its subject is the quod-substantive clause quod effēcerant (lines 15-22). Since this is a long clause, it is advisable to translate adiuvābat as a passive: the plan . was made more feasible by the fact that the Nervii . . . had brought it about. 

15. cum possent: a cum-causal clause (83). equitatu: abl. of specification (56). 

16. nihil: used adverbially; cf. quicquid, line 17, and quid possent, chap. 4, line 2. neque enim: and, in fact, cannot here mean for. not. Enim, as the context shows, 17. ei rei: this division of the army, the cavalry. Syntax (28)? 

18. copiis syntax (56)? quò facilius . . . impedirent (1. 19): (77). 

19. si ... vēnissent: a future more vivid condition in implied indirect discourse, the pluperfect subjunctive standing for the future perfect indicative. 

20. teneris . . . interiectis (1. 22): by cutting into young trees, bending them over, allowing the branches to grow out sideways in great profusion, and by filling in the gaps with brambles and thorns. ...

BOOK III 

"The spacious Bay of Quiberon, where the naval struggle with the Veneti took place, is formed by the projection of a long peninsula. 

Back of the bay is the Morbihan (Little Sea), an island sea or archipelago so thickly beset with islands that a common belief assigns them a number equal to the days of the year. The shores on all sides have a most jagged outline fringed with capes, creeks, and inlets. ... This archipelago is very difficult to navigate a perfect labyrinth of islands, separated by intricate passages which only experienced navigators can thread." 

Murray's Handbook of France, Pt. I, p. 170.

12. sub corōnā vēndidit: sold into slavery. In early times those who were offered for sale as slaves wore a wreath. Captives were regularly sold as slaves, and since they were valuable property, a victorious general might thus realize immense sums of money. This may have been a main motive of Caesar on this occasion, though he doubtless meant to teach the Gauls a terrible lesson.

The least breadth of the Straits of Dover is 21 miles, near Calais, from which the passage by steamer to Dover takes one and a half hours. 

Near the place where Caesar reached the coast of Britain is the Shakespeare Cliff, which rises sheer to a height of 350 feet. It is so named because of the poet's description of it in King Lear, IV, 6.

Page 232, line 4. siccitātēs: translate by the singular, dryness. quo: relative adverb. 

5. quo perfugiō: a place of refuge which. Perfugio, attracted into the relative clause, refers to palūdum. The year before, the Morini had escaped Caesar by a retreat into their swamps. So the Belgians in the 

World War, when hard pressed by the German attempt to break through to Calais, flooded the Yser country, breaking the dams and opening the canal sluices, drowning many of the Germans. "A mile of mud and water now lay between the battle lines."

Page 255, line 1. Dite: syntax? Images of a god with a hammer have been found in Gaul and Britain; this may be the divinity whom

(B) Arranged as Prose Cum imagō trīstissima illīus noctis subit (comes to me), quã mihi supremum tempus in urbe fuit, cum repeto noctem, quā tot mihi căra reliqui, nunc quoque gutta (tear) ex oculis meis labitur (slips). . . . Tum vērō coniunx umeris (shoulders) abeuntis (= mei abeuntis) inhaerens (clinging) haec verba trīstia (cum) lacrimis meis miscuit (mingled): "Non potes avelli (be torn away). Simul hinc, simul îbimus," (illa) inquit, “tē sequar et (ego), coniunx exsulis, exsul erō. Et mihi via facta est, et mē ultima tellus (land) capit: rati (ship) profugae (fleeing) ego, sarcina (burden) parva, accēdam (shall be added). 

Tē īra Caesaris ē patria discedere iubet, mē pietās (devotion) (iubet). Haec pietās mihi erit Caesar." Illa dolōre amēns (frantic), tenebris obortis (in gathering darkness), nārrātur (in) mediā domō sēmianimis (fainting) procubuisse (to have fallen) et mori voluisse, moriendō sēnsūs (grief) pōnere (put away), respectuque (regard) mei (20) tamen non periisse. Vīvat (73, d), et vivat, ut (mē) absentem, quoniam fāta sīc tulērunt, auxiliō suō usque (ever) sublevet. 

OVID (43 B.C.-A.D. 18) 

Publius Ovidius Naso, a gifted poet, was ordered by the emperor Augustus to leave Rome (A.D. 8) and to remain at Tomi, a frontier post on the western shore of the Black Sea. This was cruel punishment, for Ovid, through his early poetry, had become a favorite in the social life of Rome. In exile, Ovid pleaded to be allowed to return to Rome, but Augustus was unyielding. After the death of Augustus (A.D. 14), the poet implored the emperor Tiberius to pardon him, but in vain. He died at Tomi in A.D. 18.

Ovid's masterpiece is the Metamorphoses, stories from mythology, published in A.D. 9. There is evidence in Shakespeare's plays that the great English dramatist felt the enchantment of

LATIN READINGS 

369 Ovid's poetry, with which he was familiar through Golding's translation of the Metamorphoses, which appeared in 1567. 

SUGGESTED READING: 

Mackail, Latin Literature, pp. 135-144.

Dixit, et subitō ex oculis, ceu (as) fūmus commixtus (mingled) in tenuis aurās fugit diversa (apart).

VERGIL (70-19 B.C.) 

Thine ocean roll of rhythm 

Sounds forever of imperial Rome. 

Tennyson, alt.

Publius Vergilius Maro, the greatest epic poet of Rome, was born near Mantua in Northern Italy. In his later years, he lived near the beautiful bay of Naples, enjoying the favor of the emperor Augustus. His works are: the Eclogues, pictures of country life; the Georgics, a farmer's poem, dealing with agriculture and forestry, with vineyards, farm animals, and bee-

LATIN READINGS 

keeping; and the Aeneid, the tale of Aeneas, the Trojan who sailed to Italy, and became the great forefather of the Roman people.

The poetry of Vergil is full of majesty, grace, and pathos. 

SUGGESTED READING: 

Mackail, Latin Literature, pp. 92-105.

SAYINGS OF ROMAN WRITERS 1. Historia est testis temporum, lūx vēritātis. 

History is the witness unto time, the light on truth. Cicero 

2. Nihil est vēritātis lūce dulcius. 

Nothing is sweeter than the light of truth. 

3. Vīta sine litterīs mors est. 

Life without learning is death. Cicero Cicero 4. Nēmō vir magnus sine afflātū dīvīnō umquam fuit. 

Without inspiration of God no man was ever great. Cicero 5. Comes facundus || in via pró vehículo est. 

A witty companion on a journey is as good as a carriage. 

Publilius Syrus 

6. Ímprobé Neptunum accusat, || qui íterum naufragium facit. 

Who suffers shipwreck twice should not blame Neptune. 

Publilius Syrus 

7. Nōbilitas miserias nōbilitat. 

Nobility ennobles miseries. Accius 

8. Accipere quam facere iniuriam praestat. 9.

It is better to suffer wrong than to do it. Cicero Amicus certus || in re incertā cernitur. "A friend in need is a friend indeed." Ennius 

10. Multa petentibus desunt multa. 

Who seek much, lack much. Horace 

11. Ferás, nōn culpēs, || quod vitári non potest. 

Bear, and blame not, what cannot be shunned. 

Publilius Syrus 

12. Fēminīs lūgēre honestum est, virīs meminisse.

It is seemly for women to mourn, for men to remember. Tacitus 377

An heir's grief is laughter under the mask.

When immortal death has taken the mortal life. Lucretius

Great love o'er passes e'en the coasts of fate. Propertius

Human am I, and every human interest is mine. Terence

XXII 

SPINNING

OBJECTIVES OF EXERCISES IN LATIN COMPOSITION 

1. To make more thorough the student's knowledge of Latin grammar and thereby to strengthen his power to read Latin. 

2. To increase the pupil's interest in Caesar's story, as the exercises repeat many of its facts in different form. 

SUGGESTIONS FOR STUDY 

1. Study the sections of Latin Syntax referred to, noting the place of these in the "Finding Table" (pp. 379–381). 

2. Review the text on which the exercise is based. 

3. Study the Special Vocabularies given with this part of the text. 

4. Take a mental inventory of the "building materials" required for the sentence, then build the sentence, arranging the word-groups in their order; finally, write the sentence, and verify its "plan," the constructions used. 382

5. Orgetorix thought (108) that he could get possession of all 

Gaul; he persuaded the Helvetii to wander, because their territory did not extend many miles. II 

Book I, Chaps. 3, 4 CLAUSES OF RESULT: PURE, 80, 81; SUBSTANTIVE, 88 

1. The common people were so stirred up that they were eager for departure. 2. Orgetorix brought this to pass, that Casticus should get possession of his father's kingdom. 

3. He likewise will persuade another chief to attempt the same thing. 4. The whole state will be stirred up by suspicions, so that the magistrates will compel Orgetorix to plead his case in chains. 5. He had collected so many followers that he was able to escape.

Book I, Chaps. 7, 8 PLACE RELATIONS, 42, 44, 59, 59 NOTE 

1. When the envoys came to the Roman camp, Caesar did not grant what they wished. 2. After a short time had elapsed, in accordance with Caesar's wish (51), the soldiers who were in the province came together near Geneva. 3. When Caesar had completed the fortifications, an embassy came from the mountains. 4. There were many Romans in Geneva; when (84, b, end) they heard that war was at hand, they wanted to go home. 

5. Meanwhile the Gauls did not give up their attempt, but tried to cross the river by means of the fords.

VII Book I, Chaps. 23, 24 INDIRECT DISCOURSE: SIMPLE SENTENCES, 105-108 

1. The soldiers knew that on that day grain ought to be measured out for the army. 2. The day before, Caesar had said that the Helvetii would try to cut off the Romans from the grain supply. 3. After Caesar noticed that the enemy had changed their route, he drew up all his forces under the hill. 

4. On the next day the Helvetii, who had gathered all the carts into one place, saw that the hill was filled with Roman soldiers. 

5. We read that the Helvetii, after forming a phalanx, came up under the first crowded line.

retreated to a hill which was two miles away. 3. The Helvetii, having caught sight of the Romans surrounded by the Boii, said to themselves: "We must push forward again, that the 

Boii may not surpass (praestō) us in valor." 4. The Boii, who were next to the Tulingi, asked (use rogō, then petō) them for aid; they persuaded them to attack our men on the exposed side. 5. The two armies fought until evening; then the Gauls fled; although they had (33) no food (22), they did not interrupt their march for many hours. IX 

Book I, Chaps. 27, 28, 29

PARTICIPLES, 99. Iubeō, Impero, 87 AND NOTE 

(Use a participle in every sentence.) 

1. After the Helvetii in tears had asked for peace, Caesar demanded hostages. 2. While (72, d) these were being sought, about two thousand Verbigeni, led on by hope of safety, went out of the camp. 3. These were brought back and punished. 

4. Caesar ordered (use first iubeō, then imperō) the conquered 

Helvetii to return into their own territory, and since they had nothing, he commanded (use both iubeō and imperō) the Allobroges to restore their towns. 5. Caesar will allow the Haedui, who are making this request, to settle the Boii in their own territories.

Book II, Chaps. 7, 8 INDIRECT QUESTIONS, 93 

1. I do not understand how the archers and slingers can be of aid (34) to the townsmen. 2. We did not know why eagerness for fighting had come to the Remi and hope of getting possession of the town had departed from the enemy. 3. The 

Belgae, having delayed a little while in the fields of the Remi, asked whether they might be permitted (licet) to hasten to 

Caesar's camp. 4. Daily by cavalry battles Caesar tries (to find out) what his men dare (to do). 5. This hill was naturally fitted for drawing up the line, for on one side it sloped gradually to the plain. XIV

Book II, Chaps. 9, 10 SIMPLE CONDITIONS, 95. FUTURE LESS VIVID CONDITIONS, 96 

1. If our men are beginning to cross the swamp, the enemy ought to be ready. 2. If neither side should begin the crossing, Caesar would lead his forces back to camp. 3. If the Belgae call a council, they will decide that it is best to return home (42). 

4. If they should fight to a finish in their own rather than in foreign territories, they would be able to use their own grain. 

5. If the Gauls throw their spears very boldly, Caesar will not lead his men down to the river.

Book II, Chaps. 13-15 THE PERIPHRASTIC CONJUGATIONS, 100, 101 

1. Caesar was about to reduce the Bellovaci into slavery. 

2. According to the custom (51) of the Gauls, the women are about to indicate with their hands that they seek peace. 

3. Since Caesar has come up to the town, we need not now endure insults. 4. If any wars occur, we are accustomed to support our friends with all our resources. 5. The Nervii have said that they are not going to send envoys to Caesar, but nevertheless they will have to send envoys to him. 

XVII Book IV, Chaps. 20, 21 Antequam, Priusquam CLAUSES, 84, f. ADVERSATIVE CLAUSES, 85, 86 1. Although (etsi) only a small part of the summer was left, Caesar wished to perceive what customs the nations of Britain had (utor) and which harbors were good. 2. Before Caesar set out for the island, he summoned many traders. 3. He sent the faithful Volusenus on ahead before he himself should attempt to cross. 4. Although Volusenus explored those regions for five days, he did not bring back from there much (news). 

5. Although Commius is urging the Britons to be friendly to the Romans, he is not persuading them. XVIII

2. Caesar waited until he found suitable weather for sailing. Caesar

waited to find good weather for sailing. 3. As long as Caesar remained on land, the horsemen did not advance to the farther harbor. 4. The tide prevented (impediō, 90) the Romans from hurling their spears. 5. The Britons waited for the first ship to touch the shore; they wished to catch sight of Caesar himself.

XIX Book IV, Chaps. 24, 25 SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF HINDERING, PREVENTING, 90 

1. The chariot fighters, whom the Britons generally use, will prevent (use both impedio and the two constructions permitted with prohibeo) the Romans from taking a firm stand on the shore. 2. Our men, oppressed at the same time by the great waves and the weight of their arms, kept fighting bravely. 

3. Caesar, who had noticed that the barbarians were being disturbed by the war galleys, ordered that these be driven on by the oars. 4. The engines of war, which were of great use to the Romans, did not, however, prevent the enemy from beginning battle. 5. The soldier who carried the eagle especially urged his friends not to incur disgrace. XX

Since the Roman horsemen did not hold their course, Caesar complained that he was not enjoying his former fortune. 5. On that very day when Commius arrived along with many envoys, the Britons intrusted themselves to Caesar.

2. Caesar

Book IV, Chaps. 34, 35 ABLATIVES (continued), 43-60 

1. While Caesar for several successive days was keeping his men from fighting, the barbarians sent out messengers in all directions. 2. These messengers, more bold than the scouts, proceeded with very great speed and returned within a few days. 3. Because of their desire for booty and their hope of freeing themselves forever, the Britons had heard with eagerness the words of the messengers. 4. Caesar, who on that day was afraid (89) that the enemy would escape danger by their speed, had stationed his legions in battle line before the camp. 

5. After the enemy had turned their backs, the Romans killed many of them.

All the war galleys reached the port safely (use adjective), but some transports were carried down below. 3. Hearing the shout (abl. abs.), the horsemen persuaded Caesar to send themselves as aid to the infantry. 

4. The sight of our cavalry caused the enemy to hide themselves in the woods which were very near the towns. 5. But afterwards they had to go out of these woods, because Caesar burned their towns and established winter-quarters there.

Book V, Chaps. 40-44 INDEPENDENT USES OF THE SUBJUNCTIVE, 73 

1. Let us prepare whatever (plural) is necessary for making about one hundred and twenty towers. 2. In the night time let the soldiers of this cohort put in place the wall-javelins, which even now the work still lacks (see text). 3. Would that 

Cicero had not heard the things which they recalled! 4. What is Pullo to do, since the enemy all together are throwing spears upon him? 5. No one would say that Pullo ought not to have brought (non ferre debuisse) aid to Vorenus after the latter (hic) had fallen down. XXVII 

Book V, Chaps. 48-52 IMPERATIVE MOOD, 74. NEGATIVE COMMANDS, 73, b 

I. Persuade this horseman, Caesar, to carry to Cicero a letter written in Greek characters. 2. Let Caesar instruct him to cast his spear at a distance. 3. Do not report to us, scouts, that our men have given up the siege! 4. Fight bravely, soldiers, especially since with difficulty can Caesar now bring aid. 5. Do not, Romans, wonder at the fortifications of the enemy.

us. 4. There is no doubt that this man has enjoyed the training of the Druids. 5. Very many Romans feared that the guilty chiefs had not perished in that slaughter.

XXIX 

Book VI, Chaps. 15-24 Quod SUBSTANTIVE CLAUSES OF FACT, 92. COMPARE Quod CAUSAL 82, AND Quod AS A RELATIVE PRONOUN, 78 

1. The fact that the Gauls were given to superstitious rites did not please Caesar. 2. They gave the life of a man in return for the life of a man, because they thought that the gods wished this. 3. The Gauls differ from the Romans in this, that they do not allow their children to come to them in public (palam). 

4. The desire for waging war that the Germans have, makes them very brave. 5. The Germans will not harm you because you are a guest.

XXX Book VII, Chaps. 63-68 INDIRECT DISCOURSE: COMMANDS, 109; CONDITIONS, 111; 

QUESTIONS, 112 

1. The Haedui said to Vercingetorix that they were friends; let him impart the reasons for waging war. 2. They said that they were the bravest of all the Gauls; why was the chief power not being given to them? 3. Vercingetorix said that if he should allow the Romans to flee into the province, they would return with greater forces. 4. The messengers said that our men were hard pressed; let Caesar send aid immediately. What legions could be sent? 5. Vercingetorix said that he would have conquered if Caesar himself had not been present.

antecedō sēcēdō remittō dispellō a. These suffixes, when added to verb stems, make nouns: -tor, (-sor), which denote the agent or doer of the action; -tor is the English equivalent. -ium, which denotes the action or the result of the action; -y is this suffix in English. b. These suffixes, when added to noun stems, form adjectives: -ālis, -īlis, -āris, -ārius, which all denote pertaining to; -al, -il(e), -ar, -ary are the equivalents in English. 

Show how the above suffixes are used in the following nouns : mōtor populāris servilis spectator defensor poenālis inimīcālis cōnsilium nārrātor pecuniārius vitālis convivium

PART TWO 1. SUFFIXES 

a. Many nouns are made from verbs by means of these suffixes: -iō, -tūra, which denote action in progress or the result of an action; -ion, -ture are the equivalent terminations in English. -men, -mentum, which denote the means of an action or the act itself; -ment is the English equivalent. 

Show the force of these suffixes in the following words: temptätiō impedimentum conjecture exposition actiō mūnītiō mission adventure scriptūra positio persuasiō instrumentum regiment regimen possession tradition b. Some nouns are formed from adjectives or nouns by the addition of: -ia, which makes abstract nouns denoting condition or quality; -y is the equivalent in English. 

Study the effect of this suffix in these nouns: memoria victoria iniūria inopia

EXERCISES IN DERIVATION 

397 c. Some adjectives are made from verbs by means of these endings: -ilis, -bilis, which denote capability, usually passive; -ile, -ble are the English equivalents. -ǎx, denoting a tendency, usually faulty; -acious is the form which this suffix assumes in English. -idus, which shows quality; -id is what this becomes in English. 

State the influence of the suffixes in these adjectives: сарӑх laudabilis placid comparabilis rapidus tenacious cupidus mobilis pugnax agilis culpable stable

The stem of the first declension ends in -a, which appears in -ārum of the genitive plural; the stem of the second declension ends in -ō, which appears in -ōrum of the genitive plural.

NOTE. 

genitive singular in -ī

Proper nouns in -ius usually have their so also filius. Thus: (gen.) fili, of the son; (not -ii) and their vocative singular in -ī (not -ie); (gen.) Servi, of Servius; (voc.) Servi! O Servius! (voc.) fili! O son! Nouns in -ium usually have their genitive singular in -i (not -ii). Thus: (gen.) aedifi'ci, of the building. 

The accent of these nouns remains on the same syllable as in the nominative. 399

APPENDIX NOTE. 

THIRD DECLENSION 

Third declension nouns are classified according to their stems: (a) consonant stems, ending in a mute (p, b, labial; c, g, palatal; t, d, lingual) or a liquid (1, n, r); (b) stems ending in i.

I-STEMS NOTE. 

Third declension nouns with i-stems have the genitive plural in -ium, and the accusative plural in both -ēs and is; neuter nouns with i-stems have the ablative singular in -ī, and the nominative and accusative plural in -ia.

LATIN INFLECTIONS 

401 

SINGULAR 

Nom. hostis, m. f. nūbēs, f. animal, n. urbs, f. Gen. hostis nubis animālis urbis Dat. hosti nubi animāli urbi Acc. hostem nubem animal urbem Abl. hoste nübe animāli urbe PLURAL 

Nom. hostės nūbēs animālia urbės Gen. hostium nubium animalium urbium Dat. hostibus nubibus animalibus urbibus Acc. hostės (-is) nūbės (-is) animālia urbės (-is) Abl. hostibus nubibus animalibus urbibus

NOTE. — I-stem nouns may be recognized as follows: 

1. Masculine and feminine nouns in -is or -ēs not increasing in the genitive (hostis, nūbēs). 

2. Monosyllables ending in -s or -x after a consonant (e.g. urbs); also nox, noctis, f. 

3. Neuter nouns in -e, -al, -ar (e.g. mare, animal, calcar). 

4. Nouns in -ns and -rs (e.g. mōns, pars). 

NOTE. A few i-stem nouns (masc. and fem.) have in the ablative singular both -i and -e (ignis, turris, avis, cīvis, fīnis, nāvis).

IRREGULAR ADJECTIVES 

Ten adjectives have in the genitive singular -ius, in the dative singular -i; otherwise they are declined like the regular adjectives of the first and second declensions: ūnus, sõlus, üllus; alius, tōtus, nullus; alter, uter, neuter, and uterque. The rime will help you to remember these.

COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES (Regular) COMPARATIVE POSITIVE SUPERLATIVE 

lātus, -a, -um lātior, -ius latissimus, -a, -um liber, -era, -erum liberior, -ius liberrimus, -a, -um aeger, aegra, aegrum aegrior, -ius aegerrimus, -a, -um fortis, -e fortior, -ius fortissimus, -a, -um similis, -e similior, -ius simillimus, -a, -um audāx audacior, -ius audācissimus, -a, -um acer, acris, acre ācrior, -ius acerrimus, -a, -um 

NOTE. 

Adjectives in -er form the superlative by adding -rimus to the nominative masculine of the positive.

Six adjectives in -ilis (facilis, difficilis, similis, dissimilis, humilis, gracilis) form the superlative by adding -limus to the base of the positive.

405 

COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES (IRREGULAR) POSITIVE COMPARATIVE SUPERLATIVE 

bonus, -a, -um melior, -ius optimus, -a, -um malus, -a, -um peior, -ius pessimus, -a, -um magnus, -a, -um maior, -ius maximus, -a, -um parvus, -a, -um minor, -us minimus, -a, -um multus, -a, -um plūs plūrimus, -a, -um 

COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES (DEFECTIVE) 

(a) Certain adjectives lack the positive: [cis, citra, on this side] citerior, -ius citimus, -a, -um [in, intrā, in, within] interior, -ius intimus, -a, -um [prae, pro, before] prior, -ius prīmus, -a, -um [prope, near] [ultra, beyond] propior, -ius proximus, -a, -um ulterior, -ius ultimus, -a, -um (b) The positive of certain adjectives is not in common use:

[exterus, -a, -um] [inferus, -a, -um] [posterus, -a, -um] [superus, -a, -um] exterior, -ius inferior, -ius posterior, -ius superior, -ius extrēmus, -a, -um infimus (imus), -a, -um postrēmus (postumus), -a, -um suprēmus (summus), -a, -um 

COMPARISON BY magis AND maximē 

Adjectives in -us preceded by a vowel (e.g. dubius, idōneus) form their comparative and superlative by using the adverbs magis, more, and maximē, most.

Pluperfect 2 vocāveram vocāverāmus vocāverās vocāverātis vocaverat vocāverant eram erāmus vocātus erās (-a, -um) vocăti (-ae, -a) erātis erat erant 1 The Latin imperfect indicative (sometimes called the past descriptive), like the English “ past progressive," expresses the action of the verb as going on at a past time; thus, vocābam, I-was-calling, I-kept-calling, I-used-to-call. 

The English "past" tense, or simple past," I-called, and the "emphatic past," I-did-call, express the action of the verb merely as done in the past, and are generally translated by the Latin perfect indicative. 

2 The Latin pluperfect (sometimes called "past perfect ") expresses the action of the verb as completed prior to some past time.

The present infinitive ferre is shortened from fer(e)re. In the present indicative fers, fert, and fertis are shortened forms of fer(i)s, fer(i)t, fer(i)tis, respectively. There is a similar shortening in the forms of the imperative. The following verbs also omit e in the pres. imper. 2d sing. : dicō, dūcō, faciō (dīc, düc, fac). 

DEFECTIVE VERBS 

The most important of these are the perfects memini, I remember, ōdi, I hate, and coepi, I have begun. Notice that meminī and ōdī have the meanings of presents. Their pluperfects and future perfects have respectively the meanings of imperfects and futures.
