JUPITER 

(King of the Gods)

LATIN SYNTAX 

The principles of syntax here treated are restricted to those necessary to the translation of Caesar. Points of especial difficulty are approached from the standpoint of English. The examples taken are usually the first occurrences of the constructions in Caesar. 

1. Definitions. 1. Syntax treats of the use of words in sentences. the 2. A sentence (sententia) is a group of words that expresses a complete thought. A sentence consists of two partssubject and the predicate. 

3. The subject is that which is spoken of. 

4. The predicate is that which is said of the subject. The predicate of a sentence contains a verb or a verb-phrase. 

5. The direct object, a part of the predicate, is the person or thing directly affected by the action of the verb. Thus: "The 

Helvetii burned their towns." 

6. The indirect object, a part of the predicate, is the person or thing indirectly affected by the action of the verb. Thus : "The Helvetii gave Caesar hostages." 

2. Classification of Sentences. A sentence may be classified (1) according to the form in which it is constructed, and (2) according to the nature of the thought which it expresses. 

I. According to the form or structure a sentence is (a) simple, (b) complex, or (c) compound. (a) A simple sentence contains only one subject and only one predicate. Thus: "Caesar built a bridge over the Rhine." (b) A complex sentence contains a main (or principal) clause and one or more subordinate (or dependent) clauses. Thus: "After Caesar came into Gaul (subordinate clause), he defeated the Germans (main clause).” SLC. - 26 427

APPENDIX 

(c) A compound sentence consists of two or more sentences joined together by coördinate conjunctions. Thus: "Caesar hastened into Italy, and Labienus remained in Gaul.” 

NOTE. Clauses are coördinate if they are of the same order or rank.

Conjunctions used to join coördinate clauses are called coördinate, or coördinating, conjunctions. These are and (both . . . and), or (either . or), nor (neither . . . nor), but, for 2. According to the nature of the thought which it expresses, a sentence is (a) declarative, (b) interrogative, (c) exclamatory, or (d) imperative. (a) A declarative sentence declares or asserts something. 

Thus: "Gaul is divided into three parts." (b) An interrogative sentence asks a question. Thus: "Into how many parts is Gaul divided?” (c) An exclamatory sentence expresses surprise, grief, or some other emotion, in the form of an exclamation or cry. 

Thus: "How beautiful is Italy! (d) An imperative sentence expresses a command, a request, or an admonition. Thus: "Citizens, dare to fight for your country."

NOTE. 

Sentences which express a command or a request are sometimes classified as declarative sentences.

AGREEMENT OF NOUNS 

3. An Appositive. — In Latin, as in English,

In Latin, as in English, a noun used to describe another noun in the same part of the sentence, and denoting the same person or thing, agrees with that noun in case, and is called an appositive. Thus : 

1. Orgetorix, princeps dītissimus, coniūrātiōnem fecit, Orgetorix, a very rich chief, made a conspiracy.

An appositive may explain only in part the noun to which it refers. This is called partitive apposition. Thus: 

2. Helvētiī, . . . alii vadīs Rhodani, The Helvetii, • • • some by the fords of the Rhone.

LATIN SYNTAX 

429 An appositive, especially in the predicate, is often best translated by inserting as. Thus: 

3. Līberōs obsidēs ad sē addūcī iussit, He ordered the children to be brought to him as hostages. 

4. Predicate Noun. - In Latin, as in English, a noun in the predicate, describing the subject, and denoting the same person or thing, agrees with the subject in case, and is called a predicate noun: 

1. Divico princeps fuit, Divico was the leader.

A predicate noun is used with sum, be; fio, become; videor, seem; appellor, be called, etc. Thus: 

2. qui . who ... 

Celtae appellantur, . are called . . . Celts.

AGREEMENT OF ADJECTIVES 

5. Attributive Adjective. An adjective used to describe a noun immediately, and placed in the same part of the sentence, is called an attributive (or adherent) adjective. It agrees with its noun in gender, number, and case. Thus : 

1. Ūnam partem incolunt Belgae, One part the Belgae inhabit.

An attributive adjective used to describe two or more nouns of different genders regularly agrees with the nearest noun. Thus: 

2. vir et consili magni et virtūtis, a man of great wisdom and courage. 

6. "Partitive Adjectives." The following adjectives some times designate a part of the noun they describe: prīmus, extrēmus, summus, medius, infimus (imus), reliquus. Thus: 

1. prīmā aestāte, in the-first-part-of the summer; in the early summer.

APPENDIX 

2. extrēma acies, the-end-of the battle line. 

3. summus mōns, the-top-of the mountain. 

4. infimus (imus) collis, the-lowest-part-of the hill; the-footof the hill. 

5. reliqua aestas, the-rest-of the summer. 

7. Predicate Adjective. An adjective in the predicate describing the subject is called a predicate adjective. It agrees with the word to which it refers in gender, number, and case. 

Thus : 

1. Suēbōrum gēns est longe maxima et bellicosissima 

Germānōrum omnium,

The tribe of the Suebi is by far the largest and most warlike of all the Germans.

A predicate adjective referring to two or more nouns of different gender is plural, and usually masculine if the nouns denote living beings, neuter if they denote things without life. 

2. Et filius et filia bonī sunt, 3.

Both the son and the daughter are good. 

Montes et flumina sunt magna,

The mountains and the rivers are large. 

A predicate adjective is sometimes best translated by an adverb. Thus : 

4. qui creātur annuus, who is chosen annually, lit., as an annual (magistrate). 

8. Adjectives Used as Nouns (Substantives). — Adjectives are often used as nouns (substantives), especially in the plural; the masculine denotes men, or people in general, the feminine, women, and the neuter, things; as, omnēs, all men, everybody; liberta, a freedwoman; nostri, our men; bona, good things, goods, property. Thus: 

1. Lēgātōs ad eum mittunt nobilissimōs cīvitātis, They send to him envoys, the noblest of their state.

LATIN SYNTAX 

431 A substantive adjective in apposition with the subject is often used, instead of an adverb, for especial emphasis. Thus: 

2. Caesar primus in Britanniam transiit, NOTE. 

(lit., Caesar, the first, crossed, etc.) Caesar was the first to cross into Britain. - Cf. Primum Caesar in Britanniam trănsiit, First, Caesar crossed into Britain, implying that this act was the first in a sequence of things done by Caesar.

AGREEMENT OF PRONOUNS 

9. Pronouns Used as Nouns. A pronoun used as a noun has the gender and number of the noun for which it stands : 

1. Hunc laudō, I praise him. 

3. Haec est filia mea, This is my daughter. 

2. Hic est filius meus, This is my son. 

4. Id ipsa dixit,

She herself said this. 

Personal and demonstrative pronouns are expressed as subjects only for emphasis. Thus : 

5. Ad eas rēs conficiendas Orgetorix dēligitur. Is lēgātiōnem ad civitātēs suscēpit,

To accomplish these things Orgetorix was chosen. He undertook an official visit to the states. 

10. Pronouns Used as Adjectives. 

A pronoun used as an adjective (pronominal adjective) agrees with its noun in gender, number, and case. Thus: 

1. in hoc ipso oppidō, in this very town. 

2. eadem dē causā, for the same reason. 

11. Possessive Adjectives Used as Pronouns. - A possessive adjective used as a pronoun takes the gender, number, and case of the noun to which it refers: 

1. Hic liber est meus (liber), This book is mine.

APPENDIX 

2. Tuōs (librōs) nōn vīdī, I have not seen yours (single possessor). 3. 

Vestrōs (librōs) nōn vīdī, I have not seen yours (more than one possessor). 

12. The Relative Pronoun. 

The relative pronoun agrees with its antecedent in person, number, and gender, but its case is determined by its use in the relative clause. Thus: 

1. Erant itinera duo quibus exire possent,

There were two routes by which they could go out. 

The antecedent of the relative is often omitted, especially if it is indefinite. Thus: 

2. Tertiam (partem ei incolunt) qui ipsōrum linguā Celtae appellantur,

The third (part they inhabit) who in their own language are called Celts. 

3. Qui decimae legiōnis aquilam ferēbat, (The man) who bore the eagle of the tenth legion. 

4. Qui cognoscerent misit (76), (lit., He sent [men] who should find out) He sent (men) to find out.

Such a relative clause (as in 2, 3, and 4) was felt by the Romans to be equivalent to a noun. sentence is often used at 

A relative pronoun thus "connective relative "; 

13. "Connective Relative." A relative pronoun referring to an antecedent in the preceding the beginning of a new sentence. used to begin a new sentence is called a it takes the gender and number of the noun (or nouns) to which it refers; it is usually best translated by a personal or demonstrative pronoun with and, but, etc. Thus:

LATIN SYNTAX 

433 I. Quae cum ita sint, And since these things are so. 

2. Qua de causā virtute praecedunt,

And for this reason they surpass in courage. 

When a relative clause contains a predicate noun, the relative pronoun usually agrees with this noun rather than with the antecedent. Thus: 

3. Rhenus, quod est flūmen magnum, the Rhine, which is a large river. 

4. Athenae, quod est oppidum magnum,

Athens, which is a large town. 

AGREEMENT OF VERBS 14. Verbs with Personal Subject. 

A finite¹ verb agrees with its subject in person and in number. Thus : 

I. Gallōs ab Aquītānis Garumna flūmen dividit, The Garonne river separates the Gauls from the Aquitani. (a) With two or more subjects the verb is in the plural. 

But two singular subjects are often considered together as one unit, and so take a singular verb. Thus: 

2. Matrona-et-Sequana dīvidit, The Marne-and-Seine divides. (b) With two or more singular subjects connected by disjunctive conjunctions, such as aut . . . aut, neque (nec) . . . neque (nec), the verb is regularly singular. Thus : 

3. Aut Caesar aut Cicero summus erat Rōmānus,

Either Caesar or Cicero was the greatest Roman. 

¹ Finite is from finitus (fīniō), bounded, limited. The finite moods are those limited by person and number indicative, subjunctive, and imperative. 

Infinitive (sometimes called a mood) is from infinitus; that is, the infinitive forms express the verbal idea in a general, unlimited way.

APPENDIX 

(c) A collective noun may take a plural verb if the individuals constituting the group are thought of. Thus: 

4. Cum tanta multitūdō lapides ac tēla conicerent, When such a great number were (or was) throwing rocks and spears. 

15. Impersonal Verbs. Besides the impersonal verbs whose literal equivalents are used in English (oportet, it is necessary; licet, it is allowed; interest, it concerns; etc.), numerous Latin verbs are used only impersonally in the passive voice: (a) Intransitive verbs which in the active voice take an indirect object are used only impersonally in the passive voice. Thus: 

1. Helvetiis persuasum est,

The Helvetii were persuaded (lit., It was persuaded to the 

Helvetii (28)). (b) Certain verbs which in the active voice do not take a direct object are often used impersonally in the passive. Thus: 

2. Ventum est, They came. 

3. Pugnatum est,

They fought (lit., It was fought). 

4. Nōbis non dubitandum est, We must (should) not hesitate (101).

CASES OF NOUNS NOMINATIVE CASE 

16. Subject. The subject of a finite verb is in the nominative case. Thus: 

1. Gallia est dīvīsa in partēs trēs, Gaul is divided into three parts.

The subject of an infinitive is regularly in the accusative (37), but the subject of an "infinitive of animated narration ”

LATIN SYNTAX 

435 (historical infinitive) is in the nominative, the infinitive being used merely as more descriptive of a continuing (unlimited) action or state than the historical present, imperfect, or historical perfect. Thus: 

2. Interim Caesar frumentum flagitäre, Meanwhile Caesar was demanding the grain.

VOCATIVE CASE 

17. Vocative. The vocative case is used in addressing directly a person or thing. It is usually placed after one or more important words. Thus : 

1. Dēsilīte, commilitōnēs, Leap down, comrades.

GENITIVE CASE 

The genitive is used with a noun 

18. Possessive Genitive. to indicate the possessor and is then called a possessive genitive. Thus: 

1. In eōrum finibus bellum gerunt,

They wage war in their territory (lit., in the territory of them). 

2. Ubi erant fīnēs Belgārum? 

Where was the territory of the Belgae?

The possessive genitive often is in the predicate, being connected with its noun by a verb, chiefly by the copula (“connective") sum, be, or by fio, become. Thus : 3. Duae fuerunt Ariovisti uxōrēs, Two were wives of Ariovistus.

Observe that instead of the genitive of the personal pronouns the corresponding possessive adjectives are used. Thus: liber meus, my book.

APPENDIX 

19. Subjective Genitive. With a noun expressing action or feeling a genitive may be used to indicate the doer of the action or the one subject to the feeling. It is then called a subjective genitive. Thus : 

1. Adventus hostium, The arrival of the enemy. 

NOTE. If the phrase (adventus hostium) is expanded into a sentence, the subjective genitive becomes the subject of an active verb. Thus: : 

Hostēs advēnērunt, The enemy arrived. 

20. Objective Genitive. With a noun expressing action or feeling a genitive may be used to indicate that toward which the act or feeling is directed. It is then called an objective genitive. Thus: 

I. Timor noster periculi, Our fear of the peril. 

2. Is rēgni cupiditate inductus coniūrātiōnem fecit, He, (being) influenced by eagerness for royal power, made a conspiracy. 

NOTE If the phrase (timor noster periculi) is expanded into a sentence, the objective genitive becomes the object of an active verb; as, Nōs periculum timēmus, We fear danger. If the noun of action or feeling is replaced by a passive verb, the objective genitive becomes the subject; as, Periculum ā nōbīs timētur, The danger is feared by us. 

A noun accom- 

21. Genitive of Quality or Description. 

panied by an adjective may be used in the genitive as an adjective phrase to describe a noun (cf. ablative of quality, 57). The genitive of quality is used chiefly of essential (especially mental or internal) qualities, and in phrases expressing measure. Thus: 

1. homines magnae virtutis, men of great courage. 

2. levis armatūrae Numidae, the light-armed Numidians. 

3. paucōrum diērum iter, a journey of a few days.

LATIN SYNTAX 

437 The adjective phrase may be in the predicate. Thus: 

4. Flūminis erat altitūdō pedum circiter trium,

The depth of the river was (of) about three feet. With 

22. Genitive of the Whole (" Partitive Genitive "). 

words denoting a part a noun in the genitive may be used to indicate the whole to which the part belongs. Thus: 

1. Quārum (partium) ūnam (partem) incolunt Belgae, One (part) of which the Belgae inhabit. 

2. Hōrum fortissimi sunt Belgae, Of these the bravest are the Belgae.

In place of the genitive the ablative with ē (ex) or dē is regularly used with cardinal numerals (except milia, thousands), generally with quidam, a certain one, and often with adjectives suggesting numbers, as pauci, few. Thus: 

3. Ibi ūnus ē filiis captus est, There one of the sons was captured.

The genitive is used with the neuter singular (nominative or accusative) of many adjectives and pronouns expressing quantity. The genitive in the phrase is usually translated without the preposition of. Thus : 

4. quantum boni, how much (of) good. 

5. satis causae, sufficient (of) reason. 

6. minus dubitātiōnis, less (of) hesitation. 

7. nihil reliqui, (lit., nothing of remaining) nothing left.

APPENDIX 

23. Genitive of Value.

The genitive (neuter) of certain adjectives is used in the predicate with sum and verbs of valuing to express indefinite value. Thus: 

1. Tanti eius gratiam esse ostendit,

He showed that his good-will was worth so much (of so great value).

With the impersonal verbs interest and refert (it concerns) the degree of concern is expressed by an adverb, an adverbial accusative, or a genitive of value. Thus: 

2. Magni interesse arbitrābātur,

He thought it was of great concern. 

24. Genitive with Certain Adjectives. 

A noun or a pronoun in the genitive may be used to limit or define certain adjectives expressing desire, knowledge, memory, fullness, sharing, guilt, and their opposites. Thus : 

1. homines bellandi cupidī, men desirous of warring. 

2. qui rei militāris perītissimus habēbātur, who was considered very skilled in warfare. 

3. hominēs barbarī et nostrae cōnsuētūdinis imperītī, men uncivilized and unacquainted with our ways. 

25. Genitive with Verbs of Remembering and Forgetting. 

The genitive is used with memini (keep in mind, remember), reminiscor (call to mind, remember), and obliviscor (forget), if these verbs are felt as intransitive and equivalent to verb phrases containing adjectives (reminiscor, be-mindful-of; obliviscor, be-forgetful-of). Thus: 

1. Quod si veteris contumēliae oblīvīsci velim, But if I should be willing to be forgetful of the old insult. 

2. Reminiscere veteris incommodi populi Rōmānī!

Be mindful of the old misfortune of the Roman people! 

NOTE. — These verbs, if felt as transitive, are used with a direct object.

LATIN SYNTAX 

439 

26. Genitive with Interest and Refert. 

With interest, it concerns, the person concerned, if a noun, is expressed by the genitive; the thing which concerns is the subject. Thus: 

1. Docet quanto opere rei publicae intersit manūs distinērī, He shows how greatly it concerns the commonwealth that the forces be kept apart.

NOTE. 

The subject of interest may be a neuter pronoun, an infinitive phrase (as in 1), or an indirect question.

DATIVE CASE 

27. Dative of Indirect Object. The dative is used, especially with verbs of giving and saying, to indicate the indirect object, that to or toward which the action of the verb is directed. Thus: 

1. Ei filiam suam dat, He gives (to) him his daughter. 

2. Ea res est Helvetiis ēnuntiata, This fact was reported to the Helvetii. 

NOTE. The indirect object is closely associated in thought with the verb, while the dative of reference (30) is associated in thought with the whole clause in which it stands. 

Observe that with the verb dōnō (give, present) two constructions are used. We may (a) present a thing (direct object) to a person (indirect object), or (b) present a person (direct object) with the thing (ablative of means). Thus: 

3. Multis civitatem dōnāvit, He presented (gave) citizenship to many. 

4. Multōs civitāte dōnāvit, He presented many with citizenship. 

While certain 

28. Dative with Certain Intransitive Verbs. verbs in English are transitive and are used with a direct object, the corresponding verbs in Latin are intransitive and are used

APPENDIX 

66 with a dative of the indirect object. These are: most verbs meaning to favor, help, please, trust and their opposites, also to believe, persuade, command, obey, serve, resist, envy, threaten, pardon, spare." Thus: 

1. Civitāti persuasit, He persuaded the state. 

2. Novis rebus studēbat, He desired a revolution.

In the passive these verbs are used only impersonally. Thus: 

3. Mihi persuāsum est,

I was persuaded (lit., it was persuaded to me). 

4. Sibi quidem persuādērī dīxit,

He said that he at least was-being-persuaded. 

NOTE. With persuadeō a neuter pronoun or neuter adjective in the accusative may be used to indicate the thing contained in the persuading. Thus: 

5. Id eīs persuasit (lit., He persuaded this to them), He persuaded them (of) this. 

29. Dative with Certain Compound Verbs. The dative of the indirect object is used with many (but not all) verbs compounded with ad, ante, circum, con, in, inter, ob, post, prae, prō, sub, super. Thus:

I. cum omnibus praestarent, since they surpassed all.

If the simple verb is transitive, the compound governs a direct object in addition to the indirect. Thus: 

2. finitimis bellum inferre, to bring war upon their neighbors. 

3. Munitioni Labienum praefecit, He put Labienus in charge of the defense.

LATIN SYNTAX 

44I NOTE. Instead of the dative, the preposition is often repeated with its proper case, especially if a place is indicated, or motion is expressed. Thus: 

4. signa inferre in hostes, to advance the standards against the enemy. 

30. Dative of Reference. The dative is used to indicate the person with reference to whom the statement as a whole is made. Thus : 1. Sēsē Caesarī ad pedēs prōiēcērunt, They cast themselves at Caesar's feet.

The dative of reference is also used with statements containing the adverbs satis and bene. Thus: 

2. Si Allobrogibus satisfaciētis, If you (shall) make amends to the Allobroges. 

31. Dative of Separation. The dative of reference is used with certain verbs of taking away, to indicate the person with reference to whom the idea of separation is expressed. 

So used, it is called the dative of separation. Thus: 

1. Rōmāni Haeduis libertatem ēripient,

The Romans will take away liberty from the Haedui. 

2. Hostibus spēs discessit,

NOTE. 

Hope departed from the enemy (lit., with reference to the enemy).

Actual separation (with a distinct idea of motion) requires the ablative, usually with a preposition (43). Thus : 

3. Hostem a pugnā prohibēbant,

They kept the enemy away from battle. 

32. Dative of Implied Agent. The dative of reference is regularly used with the passive periphrastic conjugation (101) to indicate the person with reference to whom the idea of obligation or necessity is expressed. Also with the compound

APPENDIX 

tenses of the passive, especially if impersonal, the dative of reference may be used to indicate the implied agent. Thus: 

1. Sequanis omnes cruciātūs sunt perferendi, All tortures must be endured by the Sequani. 

2. Nōn exspectandum (esse) sibi statuit, He decided that he ought not to wait. 

3. Mihi dubitandum est, I ought to hesitate. 

33. Dative of Possessor. 

The dative of reference is used in the predicate with the verb sum to indicate the possessor, the thing possessed being the subject. Thus : 

I. Nōbis nihil est, We have nothing. 

Here the statement (nihil est, there is nothing) is made with-reference-to-us (nōbīs). 

2. Nullus aditus est ad eōs mercātōribus, Traders have no access to them. 

34. Dative of Purpose. The dative is used to indicate the purpose for which something is done, or the thing for which it serves; often with another dative (of reference) indicating the person with reference to whom the statement is made. Thus: 

I. quem auxilio Caesarī miserant, whom they had sent (lit., for-an-aid to-Caesar) as-aid to-Caesar. 

2. Pollicitus est sibi eam rem curae futuram (esse), He promised that this thing would be (for)-a-care to-him. 

NOTE. 

The dative of purpose is usually best translated as a predicate appositive (cf. 1), or as a predicate noun (cf. 2). 

35. Dative with Adjectives. The dative is used with certain adjectives to indicate that to which the quality expressed by the adjective is directed or referred. These adjectives express

LATIN SYNTAX 

443 nearness, likeness, fitness, friendliness, service, and their opposites. Thus : 

1. proximique sunt Germānīs,

And they are nearest to-the-Germans. 

2. Ac maximē plebi acceptus erat,

And he was most acceptable to-the-common-people. 

3. Castris idoneum locum dēlēgit,

He chose a place suitable for-a-camp 

ACCUSATIVE CASE 

36. Direct Object. The direct object of a transitive verb is indicated by the accusative case. The direct object may be either (a) that which is directly affected by the action of the verb, or (b) that which is produced by the action of the verb. Thus: (a) Lēgātōs misit,

NOTE. 

He sent envoys. (b) Coniurationem fecit, He made a conspiracy. - The direct object of a verb in the active voice becomes the subject of the verb in the passive. Thus: (a) Lēgātī missi sunt, Envoys were sent. (b) Coniūrātiō facta est, A conspiracy was made. 

37. Subject of an Infinitive. The subject of an infinitive is indicated by the accusative case. Thus: 

1. Angustōs sē fīnēs habēre arbitrābantur, They thought that they had narrow territory. 

2. Biennium satis esse dūxērunt,

They thought that two years were enough. 

3. Certior factus est très iam partēs cōpiārum Helvetiōs trādūxisse, NOTE. 

He was informed that the Helvetii had already led three fourths of their troops across. 

The subject of an "historical infinitive" is in the nominative case (16; 72, c). 

SLC. -27

APPENDIX 38. Two Accusatives: Direct Object and Secondary Object.- 

Certain verbs meaning to ask, demand, teach, conceal may have two accusatives: (1) the direct object (the person), and (2) the secondary object (the thing). Thus: 

1. docere puerōs elementa, to teach children their A B C's. 

2. Mē sententiam meam rogavit, He asked me my opinion. 

3. Haeduos frumentum flāgitāre ("historical infinitive"), He was demanding grain (of) the Haedui. 

NOTE. With the verbs rogo, I ask, and ōrō, I implore, beg for, the two accusatives are used; with other verbs of asking (petō, quaerō, postulō, etc.) the person is usually expressed as the source, with ā (ab), ē (ex), or dē with the ablative. Thus: 

4. Eadem ab aliis quaerit ("historical present," 72, c), He asked the same things of (from) others. 

5. Pācem ab Rōmānīs petiērunt,

They sought peace from the Romans. 

The accusative of the thing may be retained with the passive of rogō, and of verbs of teaching. Thus: 

6. Caesar sententiam rogātus est, Caesar was asked his opinion. 

39. Two Accusatives: Direct Object and Predicate Accusative. 

-Certain verbs meaning to name, choose, make, call, regard, show may have two accusatives: (1) a direct object, and (2) a predicate accusative, a noun or adjective referring to the same person or thing as the direct object. Thus: 

1. quem 'vergobretum' appellant, whom they call the 'Vergobret.' 

2. Caesarem certiorem faciunt, (lit., They make Caesar more certain) They inform Caesar.

LATIN SYNTAX 

445 

NOTE. 

With the passive of these verbs the direct object of the active becomes the subject, the predicate accusative becomes a predicate nominative. Thus : 

3. qui 'vergobretus' appellatur, who is called the 'Vergobret.' 

40. Secondary Object of Verbs Compounded with trāns. 

Transitive verbs compounded with trans (chiefly trādūcō and transportō) may take, in addition to the direct object, a secondary object, originally governed by the preposition. Thus : 

1. Trēs partes copiarum flumen trādūxerant,

They had led three fourths (lit., three parts) of their forces across the river.

The preposition trāns may be expressed with the secondary object. Thus: 2. Cōpiās trāns flūmen trādūxit, He led his forces across the river. 

The secondary object without trans may be retained with a passive verb. Thus: 

3. Belgae Rhenum trāductī sunt,

The Belgae were led across the Rhine. 

41. Accusative of Duration and Extent. The accusative is used to express duration of time and extent of space. The noun must be one that indicates time or distance. Thus: 

1. Rēgnum multos annos obtinuerat, He had held royal power many years. 

2. Haec magnam partem aestātis faciebant,

This they continued to do during (or for, or throughout) a large part of the summer. 

3. Fīnēs eōrum milia passuum CCXL (ducenta [et] quadragintā) patēbant,

Their territory extended two hundred and forty miles (lit., two hundred [and] forty thousands of paces).

APPENDIX 

42. Accusative of Place to Which. The place-to-which (the "end of motion ") is regularly expressed by ad or in with the accusative, but with names of cities and towns (also with domus, home, and rūs, the country) no preposition is used. Thus : 

1. Pertinent ad inferiorem partem Rhēnī,

They extend to the lower part of the Rhine. 

2. In Italiam profectus est,

He set out for (lit., into) Italy. 

3. domum reditiōnis spē sublātā, hope of return home having been removed.

NOTE. - Ad is used with names of towns (after an idea of motion) to express the relation to-the-vicinity-of. Thus: 

4. Ad Genavam pervenit, He reached the vicinity of Geneva (72, c).

ABLATIVE CASE 

The different relations expressed by the ablative fall into three main divisions. These relations, which were originally expressed by three cases distinct in form and meaning, are (1) the from-relation (the true ablative, expressing separation), (2) the with- or by-relation (expressing association), (3) the in- or at-relation (expressing place where). 

43. Ablative of Separation. 

The ablative, with or without the preposition à (ab), dē, or ē (ex), is used with verbs meaning to separate, remove, etc., to express separation. Thus: 

1. Gallos ab Aquitānis Garumna dividit,

The Garonne separates the Gauls from the Aquitani. 

2. Suis finibus eōs prohibent,

They keep them from their own territory. 

3. Ab his castris oppidum aberat milia passuum octō,

From this camp a town was distant eight miles. 

NOTE. 

With some verbs the ablative may be used either with or without a preposition; the usage with each verb should be carefully observed.

With a distinct idea of motion a preposition is regularly used.

LATIN SYNTAX 

447 44. Ablative of Place from Which. - The place-from-which is expressed by the ablative, usually with a (ab), away from, from; dē, down from, from; ē (ex), out from, from. But with names of towns and cities (also with domus, home, and rūs, the country) no preposition is used. Thus: 

1. Helvetii de finibus suis exībunt,

The Helvetii will go out from their territory. 

2. ex castrīs ēducere, to lead out from the camp. 

3. domō exire, to go out from home. 

NOTE. - Ab is used with names of towns and cities to express the relation from-the-vicinity-of. Thus: ab Ocelō, from-the-vicinity-of Ocelum. 

45. Ablative of Source. The ablative, with or without the preposition à (ab), ē (ex) or dē, is used to express source or origin. Thus: 

1. Rhenus oritur ex Lepontiis,

The Rhine rises among (lit., out-from) the Lepontii. 

2. Eadem secrētō ab aliis quaerit, He asked ("historical present," 72, c) the same things of (lit., from) others privately. 

3. Amplissimo genere natus erat,

He was born of (lit., from) a most distinguished family. 

46. Ablative of Material. The ablative, usually with the preposition ē (ex) or dē, is used to express the material out of which something is made. Thus : 

1. Nāvēs tōtae factae erant ex rōbore, The whole ships were made of (lit., from) oak.

APPENDIX 47. Ablative of Agent. With a verb in the passive voice the agent or doer-of-the-action is expressed by the ablative with the preposition à or ab. Thus: 

1. Ā senātū amicus appellatus erat,

He had been called friend by the senate. 

2. Ab non nullis Gallis sollicitabantur, NOTE. 

They were being incited by some (of the) Gauls. 

Indirect agency, the person through whom (rather than by whom) is expressed by per with the accusative. Thus: 

3. Per tres potentēs populōs Galliae sēsē potīrī posse spērant, They expect that they can become masters of Gaul through three powerful peoples. 

48. Ablative of Means. The means or instrument is expressed by the ablative without a preposition. Thus: 

1. Ūna pars continētur Garumnā flūmine, Oceanō, fīnibus Belgārum,

One part is bounded by the Garonne river, the Ocean, the territory of the Belgae. 

2. Ipse eōdem itinere contendit, He himself hastened by the same route. 

49. Ablative of Cause. The ablative, sometimes with the preposition ē (ex) or dē, is used to express cause. Cause is also expressed by ob or propter with the accusative. 

1. Mobilitäte et levitāte animi novīs imperiīs studēbant, Because of their inconstancy and fickleness of heart they were eager for a change of (lit., new) authority. 

2. Qua de causā Helvetii quoque reliquos Gallōs virtute praecedunt,

And for this reason the Helvetii too surpass the rest of the Gauls in courage.

LATIN SYNTAX 

449 

3. qua (via) Sequanis invītis propter angustias īre nōn poterant, by which (way) they could not go, if the Sequani were unwilling, on account of its narrowness. 

50. Ablative of Price. 

With verbs meaning buy or sell the Thus: ablative is used to express the price. 

1. vectīgālia parvō pretiō redēmpta, taxes bought at a small price.

The ablative of price is really an ablative of means. Indefinite value is expressed by the genitive (23). An adjective phrase expressing price or value, used to describe a noun, may be either in the genitive or ablative of quality (21, 57). 

51. Ablative of Manner. - The manner of an action is expressed by the ablative with cum; but cum may be omitted if an adjective or limiting genitive is used with the noun. Thus: 

1. Multis cum lacrimis obsecrāre coepit, He began to beseech with many tears. 

2. Id (oppidum) magnō impetu Belgae oppugnare coeperunt,

This town the Belgae began to attack with great force. 

The ablative of manner often expresses a situation or attendant circumstance. Thus: 

3. Intellegebat magno cum periculō provinciae futūrum (esse),

He knew that it would be with great danger to the province. 

NOTE. Certain nouns in the ablative expressing manner became felt as adverbs and were used regularly without cum. Thus cāsu, by chance; cōnsuētūdine and mōre, according to custom; iūre, rightly; iniūriā, wrongly; silentio, in silence.

APPENDIX 

52. Ablative of Accompaniment. Accompaniment or association is expressed by the ablative with the preposition cum. 

In military phrases cum is often omitted. Thus : 

I. ut cum omnibus cōpiis exīrent, to go out with all their resources. 

2. Omnibus copiis contenderunt, They hastened with all their troops.

NOTE. Cum is regularly put after a personal, reflexive, interrogative, or relative pronoun and forms one word with it; as, mēcum, tēcum, nōbiscum, vōbiscum, sēcum, quōcum, etc.

If 53. Ablative of Comparison. — In comparisons, if quam, than, is expressed, the things compared are in the same case. quam is omitted, the second thing compared is indicated by the ablative, which usually comes before the comparative. Thus: 

1. non amplius quinīs aut sēnīs mīlibus passuum, not more than five or six miles (each day).

NOTE. 

Quam may be omitted after plūs, minus, amplius, and longius, without changing the construction to the ablative. Thus: 

2. Flūmen non amplius mīlia passuum decem abest, The river is not more than ten miles away. 

Here milia is accusative of extent of space (41). 

Five deponent 

54. Ablative with Certain Deponent Verbs. 

verbs and their compounds are used with the ablative: utor, fruor, fungor, potior, and vēscor. This ablative is translated by the objective case in English. 

1. Castrīs nostrī potīti sunt, Our men took the camp. 

2. imperio potīrī, to obtain control. Thus: 

3. qui eodem iūre et isdem lēgibus ūtuntur, who have (lit., use) the same right and the same laws.

LATIN SYNTAX 

451 

NOTE. 

- The genitive is often used with potior, if the verb means becomemaster (of). Thus: 

4. totius Galliae potīrī, to-become-masters of all Gaul. 

55. Ablative of Measure or Degree of Difference. 

The measure or degree of difference is expressed by the ablative without a preposition. Thus: 

I. multo facilius, (lit., easier by much) much easier. 

2. paucis ante diebus, (lit., before by a few days) a few days before. 

3. paulo longius, (lit., further by a little) a little further. 

56. Ablative of Specification. 

The ablative without a preposition, used to specify in what respect the statement is true, is called the ablative of specification. Thus: 

1. Lingua inter sẽ differunt, They differ from one another in language. 

2. Virtute praecedunt, They excel in courage. 

3. oppidum nomine Bibrax, a town Bibrax by name. 

57. Ablative of Quality or Description. 

A noun accompanied by an adjective may be used in the ablative as an adjective phrase to describe a noun. Thus : 

1. Homines inimico animo, Men of unfriendly feeling. 

2. Germānōs ingenti magnitudine corporum esse praedicabant,

They declared that the Germans were of great stature (lit., of great size of bodies). 

NOTE. Cf." genitive of quality or description ities are generally expressed by the ablative. 99 

---

APPENDIX 58. Ablative of Time When or Within Which. 

Time when or within which is expressed by the ablative without a preposition. 

Thus : 

1. Eō tempore principātum in civitāte obtinebat,

At that time he was holding the chief position in the state. 

2. Id quod ipsi diēbus vīgintī aegerrimē cōnfēcerant, The thing which they themselves had with the greatest difficulty accomplished in (within) twenty days. 

Occasionally the ablative of "time within which" is used instead of the accusative of "duration of time." Thus: 3. Eā tōtā nocte iērunt, They went all that night. 

59. Ablative of Place in Which.

Place-in-which is regularly expressed by the ablative with the preposition in. Thus: 

I. in eōrum finibus, in their territory.

NOTE. — With names of towns and cities (also domus, home, and rūs, the country) the place-in-which is expressed by a special case called the locative. Thus: Samarobrīvae, at Samarobriva; domi, at home; rūrī, in the country. The locative of the first and second declensions has, in the singular, the same form as the genitive; in the third declension, and in all plurals, the locative has the same form as the ablative: Delphis, at Delphi. 

The preposition in is regularly omitted with loco, locis, parte, partibus, when the noun is modified by an adjective or a genitive. Thus: 

2. Nōn nullis locis vadō trānsītur, (lit., In some places it is crossed by means of a ford) It is fordable in some places. 

3. Alienō locō proelium committunt ("historical present," 

72, c),

They engaged in battle in an unfavorable place.

LATIN SYNTAX 

453 The prepositions ā (ab) and ē (ex) with the ablative often express the "point of view from which "; the phrase is usually translated as the "place where." Thus: 

4. ab Sequanis, on the side of the Sequani. 

5. ab (ex) ūnā parte, in one direction, on one side. 

60. Ablative Absolute. A noun or a pronoun in the ablative, with a participle or another noun or an adjective in agreement, may be used, like the nominative absolute in English, as a grammatically independent phrase. It is usually translated by a subordinate clause or by a phrase expressing time, cause, manner, means, condition, concession, or attendant circumstance. 

Sometimes the ablative absolute is best translated by a coördinate independent clause. Thus : 

1. Remōtīs equis proelium commīsit, (lit., The horses having been removed, he began the battle) (TIME) When the horses had been removed, he began the battle; (COÖRDINATE) He removed the horses and began the battle. 

2. eō deprecātōre, (lit., he [being] the intercessor) (CONDITION) if he were the intercessor; (ATTENDANT CIRCUMSTANCE) with him as intercessor; (MEANS) by his intercession. 

3. Sequanis invitīs īre non poterant, (CONDITION) If the Sequani should refuse, they could not go.

MEANINGS OF COMPARATIVE AND SUPERLATIVE 61. Special Meanings of the Comparative and Superlative. — 

The comparative and the superlative of adjectives and adverbs often do not imply a distinct comparison, but are merely more emphatic than the positive.

APPENDIX 

(a) The comparative is then equivalent to the English positive, spoken with emphasis, or strengthened by the adverbs rather, somewhat, or too. Thus : 

I. cupidius insecūtī, having followed too (or rather or somewhat) eagerly. (b) The superlative is then equivalent to the English positive, made emphatic by the voice or strengthened by the adverb very. Thus: 

2. Mōns altissimus impendebat, A very high mountain towered above. 

A superlative adjective or adverb is often strengthened by quam, with a form of possum expressed or understood, to express the highest possible degree. Thus: 

3. Iūmentōrum et carrōrum quam maximum numerum coëmere constituerunt,

They decided to buy up as great a number of beasts of burden and carts as possible (greatest possible number). 

4. Quam maximum potest numerum militum imperat, He levies the greatest number possible of soldiers.

REFLEXIVE, INTENSIVE, AND INDEFINITE PRONOUNS 

62. Reflexive Pronouns. (a) The reflexive pronoun with the first person is expressed by the personal pronoun (ego; pl. nōs) in the required case. Thus: 

1. Mē culpō, I blame myself. 

2. Nōs culpămus, We blame oursleves. (b) The reflexive pronoun with the second person is expressed by the personal pronoun (tū; pl. vōs) in the required case. Thus: 3. 

Tē culpās, You blame yourself. 

4. Vōs culpātis, You blame yourselves.

LATIN SYNTAX 

455 (c) The reflexive pronoun with the third person is expressed by sui (genitive), sibi, sē (sēsē), sē (sēsē) in the required case. Thus: 

5. Se culpat, He blames himself; She blames herself. 

6. Se culpant, They blame themselves.

The reflexive pronoun of the third person usually refers for its identity to the subject of the clause in which it stands. It is then called the direct reflexive. Thus : 

7. Helvetii sẽ dēdiderunt, The Helvetii surrendered (themselves).

But often, especially in indirect discourse, the reflexive in a subordinate clause refers to the subject of the main clause. 

It is then called an indirect reflexive. Thus: 

8. Dat negotium Gallis uti sẽ certiōrem faciant,

He directs the Gauls to inform him. 

Observe that the reflexive pronouns are used with inter, between, among, to express a reciprocal relation. Thus: 

9. Hi omnes inter sẽ differunt, All these differ from one another. 

10. Inter nos laudāmus, We praise each other (lit., between ourselves). 

63. Reflexive Possessive. (a) With a pronoun of the first or second person the corresponding possessive is used reflexively. Thus: 

1. Ego matrem meam amō, I love my mother. 

2. Nōs mātrem nostram amāmus, We love our mother. 

3. Tū matrem tuam amās, You love your mother. 

4. Vōs mātrem vestram amātis, You love your mother.

APPENDIX 

(b) With a noun or a pronoun of the third person the reflexive possessive is suus, -a, -um, his own, her own, its own, their own. Thus: 

5. Se suaque dediderunt,

They surrendered themselves and their (possessions). 

64. Intensive Pronouns with a Noun or a Pronoun. The in tensive pronoun (ipse, m., ipsa, f., ipsum, n., -self, very) is used to emphasize the noun or the pronoun with which it is associated. 

Thus : 

1. ego ipse, I myself. 

2. nōs ipsi, we ourselves. 

3. hic ipse, this very (man); he himself. 

66 

NOTE. - The English compound pronouns (myself, pl. ourselves; yourself, pl. yourselves; himself, herself, itself, pl. themselves), when used intensively, are in apposition with the noun or the pronoun to which they refer; as, "I myself will go "; They did the work themselves." 66 

65. Intensive Pronoun Used Alone. 

Ipse is often used alone (a) as an emphatic pronoun of the third person, (b) to emphasize an omitted subject of the first or second person. Thus: 

1. cum ipsi bellum gerunt, when they themselves (or they, with emphasis of the voice) wage war. 

2. Ipsi Caesarem laudāmus,

NOTE. 

We ourselves praise Caesar.

The possessive genitive of ipse is often used for special emphasis or contrast instead of the reflexive possessives. Thus: 

3. qui ipsōrum lingua Celtae, nostrā (linguā) Galli appellantur, who are called in their own language (lit., in the language of themselves) Celts, in ours Gauls. 

66. Indefinite Pronouns. An indefinite pronoun or adjective does not indicate definitely the person or the thing referred to. The more common indefinites are:

LATIN SYNTAX 

457 (a) Aliquis, m., f., aliquid, n., some one, something (as pronoun); aliquis or aliqui, m., aliqua, f., aliquod, n., some, any (as adjective). Thus: 

1. Aliquis id dixit, Some one said this. 

2. Aliquis (or Aliqui) amicus id dixit, Some friend said this. (b) Quis, m., f., quid, n., any one, some one, anything, something (as pronoun); quis or qui, m., qua or quae, f., quod, n., any, some (as adjective). This indefinite is rare except after si, nisi, nē, and num. Thus: 

3. si quis, if some one. 

4. nisi quis, unless some one. 

5. nē quis (lit., that not any one), that no one (negative purpose); lest any one (after an expression of fear). 

6. num quis, whether any one (in an indirect question). (c) Quidam, m., quaedam, f., quiddam, n., a certain one (thing) (as pronoun); quidam, m., quaedam, f., quoddam, n., certain (as adjective). This is the most definite of the indefinites. Thus: 

7. Quidam ex militibus dixit, A certain one of the soldiers said. 

8. Quoddam oppidum non longe aberat,

A certain town was not far away. (d) Quisquam, m., f., quidquam or quicquam n. (no plural), any one, anything. It is used chiefly in negative sentences, and in sentences implying a negative answer. The corresponding indefinite adjective is ūllus, -a, -um. Thus: 

9. Neque quisquam clarior est, And no one is more famous. (Do not use et nēmō.)

APPENDIX 

10. Estne quisquam clarior? 

Is there any one more famous? (A negative answer is implied.) 

11. Neque ulla urbs est pulchrior, And no city is more beautiful. (Do not use et nulla urbs.) (e) Quisque (quique), m., quaeque, f., quidque (quodque), n., (as pronoun) each one (thing), every one (thing); (as adjective) each, every. It is used in general assertions. Thus: 

12. Trium mensium molita cibāria sibi quemque domō efferre iubent, 

They order every one to bring from home three months' provision for himself. 

NOTE. Quisque is often used with a superlative adjective. Thus: 

13. nōbilissimi cuiusque līberī, the children of all the noblest (lit., of each noblest). 

INDICATIVE MOOD 

67. The Indicative. By mood or mode (from modus, manner) is meant the manner in which the action (or state) is expressed by the verb. The indicative mood (cf. indicō, I declare) is used chiefly to declare or assert, or to question a fact. We may say that the indicative is used when the mind of the speaker is concerned with reality — with a fact. 

The Latin subjunctive (used chiefly in subjoined or subordinate clauses) is used to express the action of the verb, not as a fact, but as an idea (73; 75 ff.).

In English the indicative is now used in most constructions for which the subjunctive was originally used.

TENSES OF THE INDICATIVE 

68. The tense of a verb (cf. Latin tempus, French temps, time) indicates the time to which the speaker refers. There

LATIN SYNTAX 

459 are in any language only three divisions of time, (1) the present, (2) the past, (3) the future. In Latin these divisions of time are expressed by changes in the form of the verb. 

The present tense 

69. The Present Division of Time. 

expresses the action as in the present, that is, at the time of speaking. It is translated, as may best suit the meaning of the sentence, by the English "simple present," by the "progressive present," or by the " emphatic present." Thus: 66 

1. Puella cantat,

NOTE. 

The girl sings ("simple present"); the girl is-singing ("progressive present"); the girl does-sing (“emphatic present"). 

The perfect indicative (used chiefly as equivalent to the English simple past ") is also often used as equivalent to the English "present perfect" (with have). The present perfect expresses the action as complete at the present, that is, at the time of speaking. It may then be felt as in the present division of time (with primary sequence, 72, b), or as in the past division of time (with secondary sequence, 113). 66 

70. The Past Division of Time. (a) The imperfect (or past descriptive) tense, used chiefly as the equivalent of the English past progressive," expresses the action of the verb as in progress at the particular past time indicated or implied in the sentence. Thus : 

1. Germānī Rhēnum trānsībant,

The Germans were-crossing the Rhine. 

The imperfect often expresses the action as repeated. It is then translated by the English "past progressive," or, if the idea of repetition is sufficiently distinct, by the English "simple past." Thus: 

2. Germanōs finibus suis prohibebant,

They were-keeping (or kept) the Germans from their territory.

SLC.-28

APPENDIX 

The Latin imperfect indicative may be represented graphically action in progress by a continuous line (—), repeated action by a series of dots ( . ). 66 (b) The historical perfect tense, used chiefly in historical narration, is equivalent to the English simple past." It expresses the action merely as in the past. Thus: 

3. Caesar Germanōs vicit, Caesar conquered the Germans. (c) The pluperfect (or past perfect) expresses the action as complete in the past — that is, as prior to the particular past time indicated or implied in the sentence. Thus: 

4. Germānī Rhenum transierant,

The Germans had crossed the Rhine. 

71. The Future Division of Time. 

(a) The future tense expresses the action merely as in the future. Thus: . 

1. Tibi scribam, I shall write to you. (b) The future perfect tense expresses the action as complete in the future that is, as prior to the particular future time indicated or implied in the sentence. Thus : 

2. Cum Rōmām pervenerō, tibi scribam, When I reach (shall have reached) Rome, I shall write to you. 

72. Special Uses of the Present Tense. There are certain special uses of the present tense: (a) The present tense is used to express a general truth. Thus: 

1. Omnes patriam suam amant, I. 

All love their own land.

A present tense thus used may be called a "timeless present," since it refers to no particular time, but to all time.

LATIN SYNTAX 

461 (b) The present is often used, instead of a past tense, for special vividness. It then presents the past action as if it belonged in the present, that is, at the time of speaking. This is called the " vivid present." It is felt as a real present (with primary sequence, 113). Thus: 

2. Hostēs statim eum circumsistunt, The enemy at once encompass him. (c) If the present tense is used instead of a past tense in historical narration and is felt distinctly as in the past division of time, it is called the "historical present." Since it is not felt as a real present, it is used with secondary sequence (113). Thus: 

3. Castico PERSUADET ut rēgnum occuparet, He PERSUADED Casticus to seize royal power. 

(d) With dum, while, the "vivid present" is regularly used to express a past action in progress (84, d). Thus: 

4. Dum haec geruntur, Dumnorīx FUGIT,

While these things were being carried on, Dumnorix FLED. 

(e) With dum, until, and antequam (priusquam), before, if the clause is expressed as an impending fact in the immediate future (so near the present that it coincides with it), the "vivid present" is often used (84, d). Thus: 

5. Hic MANE dum reveniō, REMAIN here until I come back.

SUBJUNCTIVE IN INDEPENDENT SENTENCES 

73. The subjunctive mood in independent sentences expresses the action of the verb not as a fact, but as an idea. The subjunctive, therefore, is used to express the action of the verb in the following ways: 1 

1 The starred uses are not found in the Gallic War except in indirect discourse,

APPENDIX 

*(a) Exhortations in the first person plural, present tense. 

The negative is nē. Thus: 

1. Exeāmus cum omnibus copiis nostrīs,

Let us go out with all our resources. 

NOTE. An exhortation is an action willed or urged by the speaker with reference to himself and certain others. In English, an exhortation is usually expressed by let us (let is a verb in the imperative; us is its object) followed by an infinitive without to. The subjunctive is sometimes used (in the elevated or poetical style) to express an exhortation; as, Praise we the king! *(b) Commands in the third person, usually present tense. 

The negative is nē. Thus: 

1. Obsides reddat, Let him give back the hostages.

An affirmative command in the second person is generally expressed by the imperative (see 74). Thus: 

2. Redde obsides! 

Give back the hostages!

A negative command (that is, a prohibition), if addressed to an individual second person (or persons), is politely expressed by nōli (singular), be thou unwilling, nolite (plural), be you unwilling. Thus : 

3. Nōli (pl. Nolite) hunc virum bonum culpăre, Do not blame this good man. *(c) Deliberative questions, implying uncertainty, often used to express indignation: 

1. Cur quisquam dubitet? 

Why should any one hesitate? 

2. Quam rationem pugnae insistam? 

What plan of battle should I adopt?

463 *(d) Wishes of three kinds: 

1. Future possible wish, present subjunctive, with or without utinam (0 that, would that). The negative is usually nē. (Utinam) scribat! 

O that he may write ! May he write! 

I wish that he would write. 

2. Present unfulfilled wish, imperfect subjunctive, regularly with utinam: 

Utinam adesset ! 

O that (Would that) he were present ! 

I wish that he were present. 

3. Past unfulfilled wish, pluperfect subjunctive, regularly with utinam: 

Utinam venisset ! 

O that (Would that) he had come ! 

I wish that he had come. *(e) Possibility (English “potential mood," with the auxiliary may, past tense might): 

1. Aliquis dicat, Some one may say. 

2. Tum vidērēs, Then you (usually the "indefinite you") might have seen (or one could see).

– The subjunctive may express a weak futurity (96).

THE IMPERATIVE MOOD 

74. The imperative is used to express the action of the verb as a command (or abrupt request, prayer, or entreaty). Thus: 

1. Dēsilite, commilitōnēs! 

Leap down, comrades!

APPENDIX 

NOTE. — (a) Since the present imperative has only the second person, a command in the third person requires the subjunctive (73, b). Thus: 

2. Reddant obsides! 

Let them give back the hostages! (b) If the speaker includes himself in the command (Let us praise the soldiers), the resulting exhortation (in the first person plural) requires the present subjunctive: Laudēmus militēs (73, a).

The negative with the imperative is nē. See negative commands or prohibitions (73, b).

SUBORDINATE CLAUSES IN LATIN 

75. Pure Clauses of Purpose. The subjunctive is used in a subordinate clause to express the purpose of an act. The clause, if affirmative, is introduced by ut (that, in order that); if negative, by ně (that... not, in order that . . . not). In Latin, as in English, the tense of the verb in the subordinate clause is determined by the division of time indicated by the verb in the main clause (113-114). Thus: .. 

1. Helvētiī EXEUNT, ut tōtius Galliae imperio potiantur,

The Helvetii ARE-GOING-OUT that (in order that) they-maygain control of all Gaul (or to gain control, etc.). 

2. Caesar in Galliam CONTENDIT, në Helvetii per provinciam iter facerent,

NOTE. 

Caesar HASTENED into Gaul, that (in order that) the Helvetii might not march through the province. 

The Romans felt a clause of purpose not as a fact, but as an idea, that is, an action willed or intended. The present subjunctive is used to express the purpose as willed or intended from-the-present; and the imperfect subjunctive is used to express the purpose as willed or intended from-the-past.

If the main verb expresses present (or future) time, the clause of purpose has the present subjunctive, as in 1 (cf. the English

LATIN SYNTAX 

465 auxiliary may); if the main verb expresses past time, the clause of purpose has the imperfect subjunctive, as in 2 (cf. the English auxiliary might). 

See Sequence of Tenses (113-114). 

76. Relative Clauses of Purpose. A relative pronoun or relative adverb may introduce a clause of purpose, if the antecedent of the relative is expressed or implied in the main clause. Thus : 

1. Lēgātōs MITTUNT ("historical present"), qui dicerent, They SENT envoys to say (lit., who should say). 

2. Diem DICUNT ("vivid present”), quā diē conveniant,

They APPOINT a day, on which (day) they shall assemble (on which to assemble) (72, b). 

77. Relative Clauses of Purpose Containing Comparatives. - 

A clause of purpose containing a comparative adjective or adverb is regularly introduced by quō, which is equivalent to ut eō. Thus: 

1. Castella COMMŪNIT (“vivid present"), quo facilius possit,

He BUILT forts that he might more easily be able. 

78. Relative Clauses Expressed as Facts. 

used in a relative clause expressed as a fact. clause (a) serves as an adjective to indicate 

The indicative is 

Such a relative more definitely the antecedent, or (b) it states an additional fact parenthetically. Thus: 

1. ubi ea dies quam constituerat VĒNIT, when that day which he had decided upon CAME. 

2. Santonum fīnēs, qui nōn longē ā Tolōsātium finibus absunt, the territory of the Santones, which is not far from the territory of the Tolosates. 

79. Relative Clauses of Characteristic. — The subjunctive is used in relative clauses which describe the antecedent by stating

APPENDIX 

a characteristic or tendency. A relative clause of characteristic states what naturally would be true, in view of the character of the antecedent. The clause, while not expressed as a fact in Latin, is usually translated by the English indicative. Thus : 

I. ERANT itinera duo, quibus exīre possent, THERE WERE two routes by which they could go out. 

A relative clause of characteristic is often used to express cause, the characteristic or tendency indicated by the relative clause being also the reason for the assertion in the main clause. Thus: 

2. Iniustē FACIS, qui me culpēs, You ARE DOING unjustly because you blame me (lit., who would blame me). 

80. Pure Clauses of Result. - The subjunctive is used in a subordinate clause to express the result of the act (or state) mentioned in the main clause. A pure clause of result, if affirmative, is introduced by ut, so that, that; if negative, by ut... nōn, so that (or that) . . . not. Thus: 

1. Iter tam angustum EST ut prōcēdere non possimus, The road is so narrow that we cannot proceed (lit., that we should not be able to proceed). 

2. Mōns IMPENDEBAT, ut facile perpauci prohibere possent, A mountain TOWERED above, so that a very few could easily prevent (them).

The main clause often contains an adverb (ita, tam, or adeō, so) or an adjective (tantus, so great, or tālis, such, etc.); the thought of the main clause would be incomplete without a subordinate clause expressing the result. 

NOTE. The Romans felt a clause of result not as a fact, but as an idea, that is, as what, under the circumstances, naturally would be true; it is expressed in Latin merely as probable (the fact is only implied). In English the clause of result is usually expressed as a fact.

LATIN SYNTAX 

467 

A relative pronoun or relative adverb may of result. Thus: introduce a clause 

3. Nēmō EST tam sapiens qui omnia sciat,

No one is so wise that he knows all things (lit., who would know).

After a general negative, a relative clause of result, if it contains a negative, is introduced by quin or by the relative (in the required case) and non. Thus: 

4. Nēmō EST tam sapiēns quīn id dīcat, No one is so wise that he does not say this. 

5. Nēmō EST sapiēns qui id nōn-dīcat, No one is wise who fails-to-say this. 

81. Use of Tenses in Clauses of Result. 

If the verb of the main clause refers to present or future time, the result, (a) if not completed, is expressed by the present subjunctive; (b) if completed, by the perfect subjunctive.

If the verb of the main clause refers to past time, the result, (c) if not completed, is expressed (as a past action or state in progress) by the imperfect subjunctive; (d) if merely completed, by the perfect subjunctive; (e) if both completed and prior to a particular past time, by the pluperfect subjunctive. Thus: 

1. Nostri (militēs) tam fortēs SUNT, (a) ut hostes despērent (are despairing, despair); (b) ut . . . dēs pērāverint (have despaired). 

2. Nostrī (militēs) tam fortēs ERANT,

NOTE. 

(c) ut hostes despērārent (were despairing, despaired); (d) ut dēs pērāverint (despaired); (e) ut... despērāvissent (had despaired).

After a past tense in the main clause a clause of result regularly has the perfect subjunctive to express the result merely as in the past. This "historical perfect gives to the result the emphasis of an historical statement (as in 2, d). "9

APPENDIX 

82. Causal Clauses with quod, quoniam. - Causal clauses introduced by quod, quoniam, because, since, have the indicative to express the reason as a fact. Thus: 

1. Caesar, quod memoria tenebat, Caesar, because he remembered.

The speaker may express his own reason or the reason of another (a) as a fact, using the indicative, as in 1; (b) merely as a quoted reason, using the subjunctive, as in 2, to express the reason as an idea, that is, with weaker emphasis. Thus: 

2. Legiō ei gratias EGIT, quod optimum iudicium fecisset,

The legion THANKED him, because he had expressed a very favorable opinion. 

83. Causal Clauses with cum. 

Causal clauses introduced by cum, since, because, take the subjunctive. Thus : 

1. His cum persuādēre nōn possent, lēgātōs ad Dumnorīgem 

MITTUNT (“historical present," 72, c),

Since they could not persuade these, they SENT envoys to 

Dumnorix. 

84. Temporal Clauses. Temporal clauses are classified as follows: 

(a) Postquam, posteaquam, after; ubi, ut, when, as; cum primum, simul atque (ac), as soon as, take the indicative; in past narration, usually the "historical perfect" (70, b), sometimes the "historical present " (72, c). Thus: 

1. ubi sē parātōs esse arbitrātī sunt, when they thought (that) they were ready. (b) Cum, when, while, takes the indicative, if the clause refers to present or future time; in past narration the "historical perfect or historical present are used to express a fact at a definite time in the past. Thus : 99 

2. Cum Caesar in Galliam vēnit, principēs ERANT Haedui,

When Caesar came into Gaul, the Haedui WERE leaders.

LATIN SYNTAX 

469 With the imperfect and pluperfect tenses the cum-clause, especially in narration, regularly has the subjunctive, if the clause is felt merely as giving an idea of the circumstances (not a past fact at a definite time). It is then called a cum-circumstantial clause. Thus: 

3. Cum civitas iūs suum exsequi cōnārētur, Orgetorix 

MORTUUS EST, When the state was attempting to enforce its authority, Orgetorix DIED. (c) Cum, whenever, denoting a repeated action, is generally used by Caesar with the indicative. Thus: 

4. ad quōs sẽ, cum ūsus est, RECIPIUNT, to which they WITHDRAW, whenever there is need. 

5. cum ex altō sē aestus incitāvisset, whenever the tide had rushed in from the deep. (d) Dum, dōnec, quoad, and quam diù, meaning while (= as long as), take the indicative. Thus: 

6. Dum haec geruntur, Caesarī NŪNTIĀTUM EST, 

While these things were being carried on, WORD WAS 

BROUGHT to Caesar. 

NOTE. — The "vivid present” is regularly used with dum to express a past action in progress, and is translated (as in 6) by the English "past progressive" (72, d). (e) Dum, dōnec, quoad, meaning until, take the indicative to express an actual fact; the subjunctive to express something merely as anticipated or expected. Thus: 

7. Rōmae MANĒBAM dum frāter meus advenit, (FACT) I REMAINED in Rome until my brother came. 

8. Rōmae manēbam dum adveniret, (ANTICIPATION) I remained in Rome until he should come. (f) Antequam (or ante quam), priusquam (or prius . quam), before, take the indicative to express an actual fact;

APPENDIX 

the subjunctive to express something merely as anticipated or expected. Thus: 

9. Neque prius fugere DESTITERUNT quam ad flumen 

Rhēnum pervēnērunt, (FACT) And they DID not CEASE to flee before they reached the river Rhine. 

10. Priusquam quicquam cōnārētur, Diviciācum ad sē vocări IUBET ("historical present," 72, c),

NOTE. 

(ANTICIPATION) Before he should attempt anything, he 

ORDERED Diviciacus to be called to him.

In past narration the clause with antequam or priusquam is often felt as equivalent to a cum-circumstantial clause (84, b). 

85. Adversative Clauses with the Indicative. Adversative clauses introduced by quamquam, although (and, in Caesar, etsi, tametsi, even if, although), take the indicative. Thus : 

1. Quamquam sunt eiusdem generis, SUNT hūmāniōrēs,

Although they are of the same race, they ARE more civilized. 

2. Nam etsi videbat, tamen nōn PUTĀBAT, For although he saw, nevertheless he DID not THINK. 

86. Adversative Clauses with the Subjunctive. Adversative clauses introduced by cum, ut, quamvis, although, take the subjunctive. Thus:

I. cum haec ita sint, although these things are so. 

2. ut omnia acciderent, although all things happened. 

87. Substantive Clauses of Purpose. With verbs meaning to urge, persuade, command, etc., the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause to express the action willed. The clause is

LATIN SYNTAX 

471 introduced by ut, that, if affirmative; by ně, that negative. Thus : 

1. Civitāti PERSUASIT ut exirent, not, if

He PERSUADED the state to go out (lit., that they go out). 

2. Në quis enuntiāret SĀNXĒRUNT, 3.

They ORDAINED that no one (should) tell.

EI ERAT PRAECEPTUM nē proelium committeret, HE WAS INSTRUCTED not to engage in battle (lit., It had been advised (to) him, that he should not engage in battle).

In 1 and 2 the ut-clause is used as the object of the main verb. 

In 3 the ne-clause is used as the subject of the main verb (impersonal). 

NOTE. — Iubeō, command, order, and patior, allow, are used with the infinitive and subject accusative. ordered the children to be brought. Thus

Liberōs addūcī iussit, He 

With certain impernōn) may be used as the 

88. Substantive Clauses of Result. 

sonal verbs (it happens, it remains, it is added, etc.) a substantive clause of result (with ut or ut subject. Also, with verbs of making (faciō, efficiō, perficiō, etc.) a substantive clause of result may be used as the object, or, if the main verb is passive, as the subject. Thus: 1. ACCIDIT ut esset lūna plēna, It HAPPENED that the moon was full. 

2. Obsidēs uti inter sēsē dent PERFICIT,
