He causes them to exchange hostages.

A substantive clause of result may stand as an appositive to a noun or pronoun. Thus: 

3. Poenam sequi OPORTEBAT, ut igni cremārētur,

It WAS NECESSARY for the punishment to follow, (namely) that he should be burned to death.

APPENDIX 89. Substantive Clauses with Verbs of Fearing. With verbs and other expressions of fearing, the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause introduced, if affirmative, by nẽ, that, lest; if negative, by ut, which is translated that . . . not. Thus: 

I. VERĒBĀTUR nē Dīviciācī animum offenderet, He FEARED that he might hurt the feelings of Diviciacus. 

90. Substantive Clauses with Verbs of Hindering and Pre venting. With verbs of hindering and preventing the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause introduced by nē, quin, or quominus. Nē is used when the main verb is affirmative; quin, when it is negative; quominus, in either case. Thus: 

1. Dumnorix Haeduōs DĒTERRĒBAT në frumentum cōn ferrent,

Dumnorix WAS PREVENTING the Haedui from collecting grain. 

2. Germānī retinērī nōn POTERANT quin tēla conicerent, 

The Germans COULD not be prevented from hurling their weapons.

NOTE. With prohibeō the infinitive is often used. Thus: 

3. Nostrōs nāvibus ēgredi PROHIBEBANT, 

They WERE PREVENTING our men from leaving the ships (lit., to go out from the ships). 

91. Substantive Clauses with Expressions of Doubt. With negative expressions of doubt or questions of doubt implying a negative answer the subjunctive is used in a substantive clause introduced by quin, that. Thus :

Nōn EST dubium quin Helvētiī plūrimum possint, There is no doubt that the Helvetii are the most powerful. 

- A substantive clause 

92. Quod-Substantive Clause of Fact. 

introduced by quod, that, the fact that, if expressed as a fact, has the indicative. Thus:

LATIN SYNTAX 

473 

I. ACCEDEBAT quod dolebant,

THERE WAS THE ADDITIONAL FACT that they grieved (lit., that they grieved was ADDED).

The quod-clause is often loosely attached to the sentence. Thus: 

2. Quod multitudinem Germānōrum in Galliam trādūcō, id mei muniendi causă FACIŌ,

As-to-the-fact-that I am bringing a multitude of Germans into Gaul, I AM DOING it to protect myself. 

93. Indirect Questions. An indirect question is a subordinate clause introduced by an interrogative word (a pronoun, an adjective, an adverb, or the particles -ne, num, an, whether); it is used especially with verbs of asking, knowing, telling, etc. 

An indirect question has the subjunctive. Thus : 

I. In utram partem fluat, iūdicārī nōn potest, It cannot be determined in which direction it flows. 

2. Intellego quantò id cum periculo fecerim, I know with how great danger I did this. 

CONDITIONAL SENTENCES 

94. A conditional sentence consists of a main clause and a subordinate clause introduced by sī, if, nisi, unless, sī . . . nōn, if . . . not. The subordinate clause is called the condition (or protasis); it indicates the condition with reference to which the main clause (the conclusion or apodosis) is expressed. 

Conditional sentences are classified according to the subordinate clause (the condition). The main clause (the conclusion), while usually taking the same mood and tense as the condition, may be in whatever form of expression the thought requires. 

Conditional sentences are classified as follows (95–98):

APPENDIX 

95. Simple (or Neutral) Conditions. In simple (or neutral) conditions the speaker presupposes the condition as a fact, without implying whether the condition is fulfilled or not fulfilled. Simple conditions have the indicative and are called past, present, or future, according to their time. Thus: 

I. PRESENT: Si mōns altissimus impendet, perpauci nōs prohibere POSSUNT, If a very high mountain overhangs, a very few CAN prevent us. 

2. PAST: Si Rōmae erat (or fuit), multa aedificia pulchra

VĪDIT (or VIDĒBAT), 3.

If he was in Rome, he saw many beautiful buildings. 

FUTURE ("future more vivid"): Si Helvetii per prōvinciam iter facere cōnābuntur, Caesar eōs PROHIBĒBIT, If the Helvetii attempt (or shall attempt) to march through the province, Caesar WILL PREVENT them. 

NOTE. With the future or future perfect indicative the simple condition is called the "future more vivid," because the English auxiliaries shall and will express futurity more vividly than should and would, which are expressed in Latin by the subjunctive in a "future less vivid” condition (96). 

96. Future Less Vivid Conditions. In future less vivid conditions the condition is expressed as a weak futurity, that is, merely as a possibility; weak futurity is expressed in English by the auxiliaries should and would. Future less vivid conditions have the subjunctive (present or perfect). The present subjunctive expresses a weak futurity from-the-present, the action of the verb being thought of as incomplete; the perfect subjunctive expresses a weak futurity from-the-present, the action of the verb being thought of as completed. Thus: 

1. Si Helvetii per provinciam iter facere cōnentur (or cōnātī sint), eōs PROHIBEAMUS, If the Helvetii should attempt to march through the province, WE SHOULD PREVENT them.

LATIN SYNTAX 

475 

97. Unfulfilled Conditions. - In unfulfilled conditions the condition is expressed as an unfulfilled possibility, (a) with reference to the present, (b) with reference to the past. (a) Present unfulfilled conditions have the imperfect subjunctive (in English the past subjunctive is used). Thus: 

1. Si amicus tuus adesset, ipse DICERET, If your friend were present, HE WOULD SPEAK himself. (b) Past unfulfilled conditions have the pluperfect subjunctive (in English the past perfect subjunctive is used). Thus: 

2. Si amicus tuus adfuisset, ipse DIXISSET, If your friend had been present, HE WOULD HAVE SPOKEN himself. 

98. Conditional Clauses of Comparison. A conditional clause of comparison introduced by ut si, velut si, tamquam si, quasi, as if, has the subjunctive. If the main clause refers to present or future time, the subordinate clause has the present or perfect subjunctive; if the main clause refers to past time, the subordinate clause has the imperfect or pluperfect subjunctive. 

1. Ariovisti crūdēlitātem, velut sī cōram adsit, HORRENT, They SHUDDER at the cruelty of Ariovistus, just as if he were present (lit., as [they would] if he should be present). 

2. Velut si adesset, HORREBANT,

NOTE. 

They SHUDDERED just as if he were present. 

This form of condition is usually expressed in Latin merely as a possibility; but it is often expressed as a present or past unfulfilled condition (as always in English).

PARTICIPLES 

99. A participle is an adjective form of a verb. As attributive adjectives the present and perfect participles are often best translated (a) by a subordinate clause, (b) by a coördinate clause. Thus:

SLC.- 29

APPENDIX (a) Participle Equivalent to a Subordinate Clause: 

1. Fratri ab hostibus interclūsō auxilium ferēbat, He was bearing aid to his brother (who had been) cut off by the enemy. (b) Participle Equivalent to a Coördinate Clause: 

2. Perterriti Rōmānī discessērunt, 

The Romans (were) thoroughly frightened (and) withdrew. 

3. Fūsōs hostes ad castra subsecūtī sunt,

NOTE. 

They routed the enemy and pursued them to the camp (lit., They pursued the routed enemy to their camp).

The best way of translating a participle is determined by the relation of the thought expressed by the participle to the thought expressed by the rest of the sentence.

THE PERIPHRASTIC CONJUGATIONS 

The 

100. First (or Active) Periphrastic Conjugation. 

future active participle is used with the verb sum to express immediate futurity or intention. Thus: 

1. Caesar profecturus erat,

Caesar was about-to-set-out; Caesar intended to set out. 

2. praeter quod sēcum portātūrī erant,

NOTE. 

except what they were going-to-carry with them.

The name "periphrastic " is given to a conjugation in which all the parts are expressed by a phrase rather than by a simple form. 

101. Second (or Passive) Periphrastic Conjugation. The future passive participle is used with the verb sum to express necessity or obligation.

The person with reference to whom the necessity or obligation is expressed is indicated by the dative of reference, which is here called the "dative of the implied agent" (32). Thus:

LATIN SYNTAX 

477 

1. Caesari omnia ūnō tempore agenda erant,

Caesar had to do all things at one time (lit., all things were to-be-done).

Intransitive verbs in the second (or passive) periphrastic conjugation are used impersonally. Thus: 

2. Manendum esse (sibi) non putabat, He did not think that he ought to remain.

GERUND AND GERUNDIVE 

102. The Gerund. The gerund is a verbal noun in the active voice. It has the genitive, dative, accusative, and ablative cases; the nominative is supplied by the infinitive. Thus: (a) Genitive. 

1. hominēs bellandi cupidī, men eager for warfare (lit., desirous of warring). (b) Dative. The dative of the gerund is not common in classical Latin. (c) Accusative. 

2. ea quae ad proficiscendum pertinerent, the necessary preparation for setting out (lit., those things which related to a setting-out). (d) Ablative. 

3. Reperiebat in quaerendo Caesar, Caesar found out on inquiry (lit., in inquiring).

The gerund of a transitive verb may take a direct object: 

4. spatium pīla coniciendi, time to hurl the javelins (lit., time of hurling the javelins).

But generally a gerundive phrase is used instead of a gerund with a direct object (103).

APPENDIX 

103. The Gerundive. If a future passive participle is used as an attribute adjective with a noun in the genitive, dative, accusative, or ablative case, the adjective often expresses the leading idea of the phrase and is then called a gerundive. The gerundive phrase is usually best translated by an English verbal noun with an object. Thus: (a) Genitive. 

1. facultas itineris faciendi, an opportunity of making a journey (lit., of a journey to-be-made). (b) Dative. 

2. ut rebus collocandis tempus daretur, so that time was given for arranging things (lit., for things to-be-arranged). (c) Accusative. 3. 

Vinum ad effeminandōs animōs pertinet, Wine tends to weaken the character (lit., to the character to-be-weakened). (d) Ablative. 

4. dē expugnandō oppidō, about the capturing (of) the town; about the capture of the town (lit., about the town to-be-captured). 

104. The Gerund and Gerundive in Purpose Phrases. 

(I) Purpose may be expressed by causā, for the sake (of), with (a) the genitive of the gerund, or (b) the genitive of a gerundive phrase. Thus: 

1. (a) Vēnit speculandi causă, He came for the sake of spying (to spy). 

2. (b) Germānī Rhēnum transībant Galliae occupandae causā, The Germans were crossing the Rhine for the sake of occupying Gaul (lit., for the sake of Gaul to-beoccupied),

LATIN SYNTAX 

479 (II) Purpose may be expressed also by ad, to, toward, for, (c) with the accusative of the gerund, or (d) with the accusative of a gerundive phrase. Thus: 

3. (c) RESPONDIT diem se ad deliberandum sümptūrum (esse),

He REPLIED that he would take time to deliberate (lit., for deliberating). 

4. (d) Ad eas rēs conficiendās Orgetorix dēligitur, Orgetorix is chosen to accomplish these things (lit., for these things to-be-accomplished).

INDIRECT DISCOURSE IN ENGLISH 

105. Direct Discourse in English. 

If the exact words of a statement are quoted after a verb of saying, thinking, knowing, believing, etc., they are indicated by quotation marks. Thus: 

1. HE SAID, "The bravest are the Belgae."

Such a verbatim (word-for-word) quotation is called direct discourse, because the form of the statement used in direct address remains unchanged. 

106. Indirect Discourse in English. If the thought, not the exact words, of the original speaker is quoted after a verb of saying, thinking, knowing, believing, etc., the quoted thought is generally expressed by a substantive clause introduced by the conjunction that (often omitted). Thus: 

I. HE SAYS that the bravest are the Belgae. 

2. HE SAID that the bravest were the Belgae. 

The main (or governing) verb of saying, thinking, etc., places the whole sentence in a division of time (past, present, or future); therefore, the tense of the verb in the subordinate clause must express action in relation to the time indicated by the main verb. If the main verb refers to present or future time, the

APPENDIX 

tense of the verb used in the original statement remains unchanged in the indirect discourse. If the main verb refers to past time, the verb used in the original statement changes its tense, a present changing to the past,' a present perfect changing to the past perfect. Thus the statement, "I live (or am living) in the city, but I have lived in the country," becomes in indirect discourse: 

3. HE SAYS that he lives (or is living) in the city, but he has lived in the country. 

4. HE SAID that he lived (or was living) in the city, but he had lived in the country. 

NOTE. — If the verb of saying, thinking, etc., is in the passive voice, three constructions occur: 

1. A substantive clause introduced by that is used as the subject of the passive verb: 

That the bravest are the Belgae IS SAID. 

2. The expletive (" filler ") it is used as a grammatical subject, and a that-clause follows the passive verb. Thus: 

It IS SAID that the bravest are the Belgae. 

3. The subject of the that-clause becomes the subject of the passive verb, and the verb of the clause becomes an infinitive. Thus: 

4. The bravest ARE SAID to be the Belgae. 

107. The Auxiliaries shall (should) and will (would) in Indirect Discourse. The uses of the auxiliaries shall (past tense, should) and will (past tense, would) are the same as in direct discourse, but if the first person with shall or should in the direct discourse becomes the second or third person in the indirect discourse, shall or should is retained. Thus: 

1. (Direct Discourse) HE SAYS, 

I shall die." (Indirect Discourse) He SAID that he should die. 

1 In English, a general truth usually remains in the present tense: "I told him that the earth is round."

LATIN SYNTAX 

481 

INDIRECT DISCOURSE IN LATIN SIMPLE SENTENCES 

108. Simple Statements of Fact in Indirect Discourse. 

simple statement of fact, when put in indirect discourse after a verb of saying, thinking, knowing, believing, etc., is expressed by the infinitive with subject in the accusative. The tense of the infinitive corresponds to the tense used in the original statement. 

The statement Galli differunt, The Gauls differ, becomes in indirect discourse: 

1. DICIT Gallōs differre, 

(lit., HE SAYS the Gauls to differ) He SAYS that the Gauls differ. 

2. DIXIT Gallōs differre, 

(lit., He SAID the Gauls to differ) He SAID that the Gauls differed.

The statements Germānī trānsiērunt, The Germans crossed (have crossed), and Germānī transībunt, The Germans will cross, become : 3. 

DICIT Germanōs trānsīsse; trānsītūrōs esse,

HE SAYS that the Germans crossed (have crossed); will cross. 4. DIXIT Germānās trānsīsse; trānsitūrōs esse, He SAID that the Germans crossed (had crossed); would cross.

The present infinitive expresses action as contemporary with that of the introductory verb; the perfect infinitive, as prior; the future infinitive, as subsequent. - A 

109. Commands and Exhortations in Indirect Discourse. 

command or exhortation in indirect discourse has the subjunctive, since the action of the verb must be expressed as willed. 

The tense is determined by the division of time indicated by the main verb of saying, thinking, etc. See Sequence of Tenses (113, 114).

APPENDIX 

1. Direct Address (Caesar to Ariovistus) "Redde obsides Haeduis!" "Give back the hostages to the Haedui!" 

2. Indirect Discourse 

(a) Action willed from-the-present: 

Caesar RESPONDET: reddat obsides Haeduis, Caesar REPLIES that he (Ariovistus) shall (or should) give back the hostages to the Haedui. (b) Action willed from-the-past:

Caesar RESPONDIT: redderet obsidēs Haeduīs, 

Caesar REPLIED that he (Ariovistus) should give back the hostages to the Haedui.

NOTE. The prohibition (negative command) “Nōli (Nōlite) com mittere!" Do not allow ! (lit., Be unwilling to allow !) becomes (1) after Divicō DICIT: nē committat, (that) he should not allow (lit., let him not allow); (2) after Divicō DIXIT: në committeret, (that) he should not allow.

COMPLEX SENTENCES 

110. Subordinate Clauses in Indirect Discourse. A subor dinate clause in indirect discourse has the subjunctive. The tense is regularly determined by the division of time indicated by the governing verb of saying, thinking, etc. Thus: 

1. DICIT Belgās fortissimōs esse, quod longissimē absint, HE SAYS (that) the Belgae are the bravest, because they are farthest distant. 

2. Dixit Belgās fortissimōs esse, quod longissimē abessent, HE SAID (that) the Belgae were the bravest, because they were farthest distant. 

NOTE. 1. In Latin, as in English, after the present tense (as in 1) the tense of the subordinate clause remains the same as in the original statement; after the past tense (as in 2) the tense of the subordinate

LATIN SYNTAX 

483 clause changes to the past division of time, the imperfect expressing contemporary past action or state in progress. 

2. A relative clause is often expressed as a fact (with the indicative) independently of the words of the original speaker or writer. Thus : 

3. CERTIOR FACTUS EST ex ea parte quam Gallis concesserat omnēs noctu discessisse, HE WAS INFORMED that all had departed by night from that part which he had assigned to the Gauls. 

111. Conditional Sentences in Indirect Discourse. - The condition (protasis) has the subjunctive, the tense being determined by the division of time indicated by the verb of saying, thinking, etc. The verb of the conclusion (apodosis) changes to the infinitive. (a) Simple Conditions. When put into indirect discourse, simple conditions have the subjunctive, which expresses the action of the verb merely as an idea. The tense must express the action in relation to the division of time indicated by the verb of saying, etc. Thus: 

1. DICIT, si Helvetii conveniant, perīculum magnum esse, HE SAYS that if the Helvetii are assembling, the danger is great. 

2. DIXIT, si Helvetii convenirent, periculum magnum esse, HE SAID that if the Helvetii were assembling, the danger was great. (b) Future Less Vivid Conditions. When put into indirect discourse, future less vivid conditions remain in the subjunctive; the tense is determined by the division of time indicated by the verb of saying, etc. (116). Thus: 

3. RESPONDET: si Helvetii conveniant, periculum magnum futurum esse,

He REPLIES that, if the Helvetii should assemble, the danger would be great.

APPENDIX 4. RESPONDIT: si Helvetii convenirent, periculum magnum NOTE. 

futurum esse,

He REPLIED that if the Helvetii should assemble, the danger would be great. 

The main clause (with the present subjunctive) in the original statement, since it expresses a weak futurity, becomes in indirect discourse the future infinitive. (c) Unfulfilled Conditions. The protasis of an unfulfilled condition remains unchanged in mood and tense in indirect discourse; the apodosis is expressed by the future participle with fuisse: Thus: 

5. Dicō (or Dixi) si adesset, ipsum dicturum fuisse,

I SAY (or SAID) that if he were present, he would speak himself. 

6. Dicō (or DixĪ) sī adfuisset, ipsum dicturum fuisse, NOTE. 

I SAY (or SAID) that if he had been present, he would have spoken himself. 

If the conclusion of an unfulfilled condition is in the passive voice, it is expressed by futurum fuisse with a substantive clause of result. 

Thus : 

7. DICIT (or DIXIT) si hostēs bellum gessissent, futurum fuisse ut vincerentur, HE SAYS (or SAID) that if the enemy had waged war, they would have been conquered (lit., it-was-about-to-be that they would be conquered). 

112. Questions in Indirect Discourse. 

I. Real Questions. Real questions, asked for information, when put in indirect discourse, have the subjunctive; the tense is determined by the time of the verb of saying, etc. 

1. (a) Direct Question: 

Cūr in meas possessiōnēs venis? 

Why are you coming into my dominions?

LATIN SYNTAX 

485 

(b) Indirect Discourse: 

Ariovistus RESPONDIT: Cūr in suas possessiōnēs (Caesar) 

veniret? 

Ariovistus REPLIED: Why was he (Caesar) coming into his dominions?

II. Rhetorical Questions. Rhetorical questions, not asked for information but merely equivalent to negative statements, if they have the indicative, change to the same tense of the infinitive in indirect discourse. Thus: 

1. (a) Direct Question: 

Num recentium iniūriārum memoriam dēpōnere possum? 

Can I forget the recent wrongs? 

(b) Indirect Discourse: 

Caesar RESPONDIT: Num recentium iniuriārum memoriam dēpōnere (sē) posse? 

Caesar REPLIED: Could he forget the recent wrongs?

If rhetorical questions have the subjunctive in the direct address (73, c) they remain in the subjunctive in the indirect discourse. 

2. (a) Direct Question:

Cür de victōriā dēs pērētis? 

Why should you despair concerning victory? 

(b) Indirect Discourse: 

RESPONDIT: Cūr dē victōriā dēspērārent? 

He REPLIED: Why should they despair concerning victory?

SEQUENCE OF TENSES IN LATIN 113. Sequence of Tenses in Direct Discourse. In a complex sentence the main verb places the whole sentence in a division of time (past, present, or future); therefore the tense of the verb

APPENDIX 

in the subordinate clause must express action or state in relation to the time indicated by the main verb (68). The main verb, therefore, exercises a certain control over the verb of the subordinate clause. This principle, called "Sequence of 

Tenses," because the tense of the subordinate verb usually follows the division of time indicated by the main verb, is shown by the following table:

TENSE OF VERB IN MAIN CLAUSE TENSE OF SUBJUNCTIVE IN SUBORDINATE CLAUSE PRIMARY PRESENT PRESENT TENSES 

(incomplete action)

(past time)

(incomplete action) followed by

PLUPERFECT 

(completed action) 

NOTE. Tense sequence requires not the same tense, but the same kind of tense (i.e. either all tenses primary, or all tenses secondary). 

114. Rule. The law of sequence of tenses requires that a primary tense in the main clause be followed by a primary tense in the dependent clause, and that a secondary tense in the main clause be followed by a secondary tense in the dependent clause, or more briefly, "primary" tenses are followed by primary, "secondary" tenses by secondary. 66 

The sequence of 

115. Examples of Sequence of Tenses. 

tenses, already seen in each type of subordinate clauses in 

Latin" (75 ff.), is further shown by the following indirect questions:

LATIN SYNTAX 

487 

1. Primary Sequence: RoGo, ROGĀBŌ, ROGĀVĪ, ROGĀVERŌ, ubi habitet (habitaverit), I ASK, SHALL ASK, HAVE ASKED, SHALL HAVE ASKED where he is living or lives (has lived or lived). 

2. Secondary Sequence: ROGĀVĪ, ROGĀBAM, ROGĀVERAM, ubi habitāret (habitāvisset), I ASKED, WAS ASKING, HAD ASKED where he was living or lived (had lived or lived). 

116. Sequence of Tenses in Indirect Discourse. In indirect discourse the governing verb of saying, thinking, etc., places the whole sentence in a division of time (past, present, or future); therefore the tense of the verb in a subordinate clause usually expresses action (or state) in relation to the time indicated by the governing verb of saying, etc. (110).

EXCEPTIONS. If the subordinate clause is dependent upon a perfect infinitive (representing a past tense of the direct address), the subordinate clause will have its verb in secondary sequence, even if the verb of saying, thinking, etc., is in a primary tense. Thus: 

1. Dicir sē domum rediisse, quod librum oblitus esset,

HE SAYS that he returned home because he had forgotten a book.

Often, for the sake of vividness, after a secondary tense of the governing verb of saying, etc., the subordinate clause has a primary tense. Often, especially in long passages of indirect discourse, the speaker varies the division of time referred to by the subordinate clause, " stepping from the past into the present, and from the present into the past." Thus: 

2. Caesar RESPONDIT: sē eās rēs quās lēgātī commemorāssent (Secondary), memoria tenere. . . . .. Cum ea ita sint (Primary), sẽ tamen pacem facturum esse, Caesar REPLIED that he remembered the things which the envoys had mentioned. . . . Although this was so (lit., is so), he would nevertheless make peace.

APPENDIX THE INFINITIVE 

117. The Infinitive as a Verbal Noun. The infinitive in 

Latin, as in English, is often equivalent to a verbal noun (cf. 102), and may be used as the subject of a verb. Thus: 

I. Facile est tōtius Galliae imperiō potīrī,

It is easy to get control of all Gaul. 

The infinitive is here used as a neuter verbal noun; the predicate adjective facile is neuter. 

118. Infinitive Phrase as Subject and Object. The infinitive, with or without a subject accusative, may be used as the subject or object of a verb. Thus: 

1. Pontem rescindi oportet,

It is necessary for the bridge to be destroyed. 2. Obsidēs ad sē addūcī iussit, He ordered (the) hostages to be brought to him. 

119. Complementary Infinitive. The infinitive, without a subject accusative, may be used to complete verbs meaning be able (can), wish, dare, begin, etc. Thus: 

1. Ūnō itinere exire possumus, We can go out by one route. 

120. Infinitive with Verbs of Saying, etc. After verbs of saying, knowing, thinking, believing, etc., an infinitive with subject accusative is used to express a simple statement of fact in indirect discourse (108).

THE SUPINE 

121. The supine is a verbal noun of the fourth declension, found only in the accusative (-um) and ablative (-ū).

The supine in -um is used with verbs of motion to express purpose. Thus:

2. Lēgātōs mittunt rogatum auxilium,

A ROMAN BURIAL URN

THE ROMAN CALENDAR 

122. The Roman year consisted of twelve months, the names of which are adjectives (with mensis, m., month, understood): (January)

Iānuārius, -a, -um (February) 

Februārius, -a, -um (March)

Mārtius, -a, -um (April) 

Aprīlis, -e (May) 

Maius, -a, -um (June)

Iūnius, -a, -um (July) 

Quinctilis, -e (August)

Sextilis, -e 

(September) 

September, -bris, -bre 

(October) 

October, -bris, -bre (November) 

November, -bris, -bre 

(December) 

December, -bris, -bre

The first month of the Roman year originally was March, the month of plowing and planting; July (named for Julius Caesar) was originally the fifth month (Quinctīlis); August (named for the emperor Augustus) was the sixth month (Sextilis); September, the seventh month (September), etc.

Before the calendar was changed by Julius Caesar all months had twenty-nine days except March, July, October, and May, which had thirty-one days, and February, which had twentyeight days. Since the calendar year of 355 days was shorter than the solar year, a month of varying length was inserted after February 23 every other year and was called the mēnsis intercalaris. 

At the beginning of 45 B.C. the "Julian Calendar " was instituted by Caesar, the calendar year having 365 days, and all months having thirty-one days except April, June, September, 490

ROMAN CALENDAR 

491 and November, which have thirty, and February, which has twenty-eight (in every fourth year, twenty-nine). 

123. How the Romans Expressed Dates. 

1. A particular year was usually designated by the names of the two consuls.

The names with cōnsulibus form an ablative absolute phrase (60). Thus:

M. Messālā M. Pisōne consulibus, in the consulship of Marcus Messala and Marcus Piso. 

2. The days of the month were reckoned as on or before one of three reckoning points: (a) The Kalends (Kalendae, -ārum, f.), the first day of each month. (b) The Nones (Nōnae, -ārum, ƒ.), usually the fifth day of the month, but in " March, July, October, May, the Nones fall on the seventh day." 66 (c) The Ides (Idūs, -uum, ƒ.), usually the thirteenth, but in 

March, July, October, May, the Ides fall on the fifteenth day.” 

3. Dates on the Kalends, Nones, and Ides were expressed by the ablative of time when (as Idibus Martiis, on the Ides of March).

Dates between the Kalends and the Nones were reckoned as so many days before the Nones; dates between the Nones and the Ides as so many days before the Ides; dates after the Ides as so many days before the Kalends of the next month. 

4. In thus reckoning forward, "the day before the Kalends, Nones, or Ides " was designated as pridie (with accusative)

Kalendās (or Nōnās or Idūs). Other days were designated by ante diem (a. d.) with the ordinal number required in counting forward to the nearest reckoning point. The Romans counted both ends of the series of days, and so, before the Nones and Ides, one more than we do; before the Kalends, two more than we do. Thus:

SLC. - 30

APPENDIX 

(a) July 4, in Roman reckoning "four days before the Nones " (see 2 b), is expressed as ante diem quartum Nōnās Iūliās (lit., before the fourth day, [namely] the July Nones). The expression is abbreviated to a.d. IV. Nōn. Iul. (b) March 28, in Roman reckoning "five days before the 

April Kalends," is expressed as ante diem quintum Kalendās 

Aprīlis (lit., before the fifth day, [namely] the April Kalends), abbreviated to a.d. V. Kal. Apr.

FORMATION OF LATIN WORDS NOUN SUFFIXES 

The more common suffixes used in forming Latin nouns are shown by examples chiefly from Book I of the Gallic War. 

Examine each derivative and the Latin word to which it is related.

124. A. Nouns in -or denoting activity, condition, or state: timor, -ōris, m., fear (timeō, fear) clamor, -ōris, m., shout (clāmō, shout)

II. NOUNS FORMED FROM OTHER NOUNS 

128. A. Nouns in -tūs, -tās, -ia denoting condition or quality : virtūs, -tūtis, f., manliness, character, courage, virtue (vir, man) servitūs, -tūtis, f., slavery (servus, slave) auctoritas, -tātis, f., authority, influence (auctor, adviser) victoria, -ae, f., victory (victor, conqueror, victor) militia, -ae, f., military service (miles, soldier) 

129. B. Nouns in -ātus denoting office or official body: principātus, -ūs, m., leadership (princeps, leader) magistrātus, -ūs, m., magistracy, magistrate (magister, master) senātus, -ūs, m., senate, i.e. " an official body of old men " (senex, old man)

LATIN WORD FORMATION 

495 

III. NOUNS FORMED FROM ADJECTIVES 

130. Nouns in -tās, -tūdō, -ia, -tia denoting condition or quality: cupiditās, -tātis, f., desire, eagerness, greed (cupidus, -a, -um, desirous, eager) facultas, -tātis, f., ability, opportunity; pl., means, resources (facilis, -e, easy) altitūdō, -dinis, f., height, depth (altus, -a, -um, high, deep) audācia, -ae, ƒ., boldness, daring (audāx, bold, daring) grātia, -ae, ƒ., favor, popularity; pl., thanks (grātus, -a, -um, pleasing) angustiae, -ārum, f., narrowness, narrow pass; difficulties (angustus, -a, -um, narrow) amicitia, -ae, f., friendship (amicus, -a, -um, friendly) iustitia, -ae, f., justice (iūstus, -a, -um, just) 

ADJECTIVE SUFFIXES 

The more common suffixes used in forming adjectives are shown by examples from Book I. 

131. A. Adjectives in -ilis, -bilis denoting capability (in a passive sense): facilis, -e, easy, i.e. "capable of being done" (faciō, do, make) nobilis, -e, famous, distinguished, noble, high-born (nōsco, learn) 

132. B. Adjectives in -idus denoting quality: cupidus, -a, -um, eager, desirous (cupio, be eager, desire) timidus, -a, -um, fearing, fearful (timeō, fear) 

133. C. Adjectives in -āx, -ulus denoting a tendency (usually faulty): audax, bold, daring (audeō, dare) crédulus, -a, -um, credulous (crēdō, believe)

APPENDIX 

134. D. Adjectives in -ālis, -ilis, -āris, -anus, -ārius, -ius, -ivus denoting the relation belonging to or pertaining to: nāvālis, -e, naval; i.e. " belonging to ships" (nāvis, ship) civilis, -e, civil; i.e. " belonging to citizens" (cīvis, citizen) militāris, -e, of a soldier, military (miles, soldier) Rōmānus, -a, -um, of Rome, Roman (Rōma, Rome) cotidiānus, -a, -um daily (cotidie, adv., daily) legiōnārius, -a, -um, of a legion, legionary (legio, legion) ēgregius, -a, -um, excellent, unusual (ē, out from + grex, herd, crowd) captivus, -a, -um, captive (capiō, take) fugitivus, -a, -um, fugitive, fleeing; as a subst., runaway slave, fugitive (fugiō, flee) 

135. E. Adjectives in -ōsus denoting full of: periculōsus, -a, -um, dangerous, full of danger (periculum, danger)

LATIN PREFIXES 

136. Besides the prepositions, the following prefixes are used: con- (com-, co-), together; thoroughly di- (dis-), apart in- (im-), not, unre- (red-), back, again

THE PRONUNCIATION OF LATIN PROPER NAMES IN ENGLISH 

Latin proper names in English translation should be pronounced as English words. Thus Caesar, which in Latin is pronounced "kai'sar," in English is pronounced "sē'zȧr.” In the Latin-English Vocabulary, the English pronunciation is shown by a phonetic respelling in parentheses. The sounds of the vowels in the respelling for pronunciation are indicated by diacritical marks, as in the following table. 

1. ǎ, as in ăm; Casticus (kǎs'ti-kus) 2. a, as in ale; Aquitāni (ǎk-wĭ-tā'ni) 

A 3. á, as in senate;

Garumna (gá-rum'nȧ) 

4. ä, as in ärm; 

5. ȧ, as in sofà; 

1. ě, as in ĕnd; 

2. e, as in eve;

Arverni (är-vēr'nī) Valerius (vå-lē'ri-us) Haedui (hěd'ù-î) 

Helvetii (hěl-vē'shyi) 

3. é, as in event; Nemetes (něm'è-tēz) 

4. e, as in maker; Verbigenus (vēr-bĭj'ê-nus) 

1. I, as in ill; Dīviciācus (div'ĭ-shi-ā´kus) 2. I, as in ice; Latobrīgi (lǎt-ô-brī'ji) 

1. Ŏ, as in odd; Allobrogēs (â-lõb’rô-jēz) 

2. ō, as in ōld; Boii (bō'yi) 

3. o, as in obey; Vocontii (vo-kon'shyi) 

4. ô, as in ôrb and a as in all (ôl); Orgetorix (ôr-jět'ō-rìks), Aulerci (ô-ler'si) 5. oo, as u in rumor (rōōmôr); Rüfus (rōō'fus) 

1. ŭ, as in ŭp; Dumnorix (důmʼnô-riks) 

U 2. u, as in use; Lūcius (lu'shyus) 

3. u, as in unite; Haedui (hĕd'ů-i) 

In the respelling for pronunciation the values of the following consonants should be carefully observed: 

1. soft g (before e and i) j (see E, 4; I, 2; 0, 1). 

2. soft c (before e and i) = s (see O, 4); hard c = 

3. x = ks (see O, 4). k (see A, 1; I, 1). 

4. c and t before io and iu have the sound of sh, and the i has the sound of y in yes (see U, 2). 

5. final s after e has the sound of z (see O, 1). 

6. qu = kw (see A, 2).

When Latin words are pronounced as English words certain tendencies of pronunciation are noticeable : 

1. In Latin words of three syllables the first syllable, if long, is generally shortened in English. Thus, Haedui (hěd'û-î), Sēquani (sèk'wȧ-ni), Divicō (div'i-kō), Aulerci (ô-lēr'sī). 

2. A short vowel in an open syllable, if accented, generally becomes long. Thus, Arar (āʼrär), Titus (tī'tŭs), Bibrax (bīʼbråks). 

3. A long vowel in a closed syllable is generally shortened in English. 

Thus, Quintus (kwin'tŭs), Publius (pub'lĭ-us). 

4. The vowels o and u, if short and in an open syllable, often acquire a longer sound in English. Thus, Eburōnēs (ĕb-u-rō'nēz), Santones (săn′to-nẽz). 590
